## 📚 Paper List

- **[What Does a Skill Vocabulary Cost? A Closed-Loop Decomposition over Demonstrated Motions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32858)**  `arXiv:2611.32858`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Maximilian Xiling Li, Nils Blank, Paul Mattes, Pankhuri Vanjani, Rudolf Lioutikov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete skill policies execute motions drawn from a finite vocabulary. In most current methods, this vocabulary is a latent codebook learned jointly with a selector and a decoder. When such a policy fails in closed loop, the failure cannot be traced to the vocabulary, to the choice of skill or to its execution. We present Movement Prototype Quantization (MPQ), which places a library of demonstrated motions after a trained policy instead of inside it. MPQ replaces each predicted action chunk with its nearest library element, corrects that element toward the prediction within a bound, and trains no parameters. Across six architectures, including a 7B vision-language-action model, the wrapped policy is statistically equivalent to the original. A reference policy executed through the library is therefore a baseline for any selector over the same library. Substituting a selector's components one at a time attributes its shortfall to them. On LIBERO-90, with selectors on three backbones, quantization costs nothing and the choice of skill costs at most 2 points under the reference's placement and correction. The selectors' own placement and correction account for nearly all of the remaining gap, up to 12 points. Offline reconstruction predicts none of these differences and misjudges library size, design and transfer across benchmark suites. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[iWAM: Efficient Action Prediction from World Models without Explicit Future Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33141)**  `arXiv:2611.33141`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Vansh Kapoor, Max Sobol Mark, Sreyas Venkataraman, Max Simchowitz, Aviral Kumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models (WAMs) build policies on pretrained video-generation models, on the premise that predicting how a scene evolves helps determine how to act. However, generating future scenes is expensive for real-time control. This motivates efficient proxies for future imagination, but how to train such proxies without sacrificing action-prediction quality remains unclear. An effective proxy need not generate a high-resolution frame; it only needs to provide enough information to distinguish between possible futures. This leads to the design principle behind iWAM: rather than denoising an entire future frame, can we condition the action policy on intermediate features from a single video-model evaluation? We show that the answer is yes. Exploiting the reversible dynamics of flow-matching models, we invert each training future into the corresponding latent noise vector. However, a single velocity evaluation at this latent does not necessarily yield informative features for action prediction. We therefore learn a bidirectional flow map that amortizes future-generation computation, enabling a single model evaluation to provide effective conditioning features. At inference, we sample a latent noise vector from the prior and condition the policy using just one video-backbone forward pass. Across simulated benchmarks and real robots, iWAM recovers the generalization benefits of WAMs while substantially reducing its inference cost.
  </details>

- **[ROAM-Bench: Evaluating Embodied Active Spatial Intelligence through Evidence Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33143)**  `arXiv:2611.33143`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Xingjian Ran, Xiaoye Mo, Sihao Liu, Li Luo, Taiqiang Wu, Ji Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied spatial intelligence requires agents to gather information through exploration and interaction, then use it to reason and act. Existing benchmarks insufficiently distinguish correct outcomes from completion of these task requirements. We introduce ROAM-Bench to evaluate these abilities under robot motion and collision constraints. Independent checks use simulation state and episode records to assess outcomes and task requirements, with stronger checks of geometry, coverage, or placement. It comprises 250 manually validated tasks across 11 types in five groups and 27 indoor environments, covering object and room search, counting, geometric judgments, direction reasoning, and relocation. Human evaluators achieve 84% strict task success on 100 sampled tasks, with every correct outcome satisfying the required checks. Across seven frontier vision-language models, we identify two complementary failure modes: correct outcomes with unmet task requirements, and incorrect outcomes despite passing stronger checks. Oracle-input diagnostics show that more complete target observations and calibrated feedback improve final spatial judgments.
  </details>

- **[Support2Sim: Visually Faithful Real2Sim with Stable Object Supports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32727)**  `arXiv:2611.32727`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Samarth Chopra, Ankit Prabhu, Akshayan Nagarajah, Sumer Murthy, Pratik Chaudhari, Vijay Kumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing a scene for physics simulation requires recovering its spatial layout and the support relationships that govern its behavior. We present Support2Sim, a Real2Sim system that reconstructs simulation-ready scenes from a single RGB image. Our design objective is to maximize source fidelity, support realization, and stability within a practical time budget for scene generation. Support2Sim infers a support graph, which a contact-guided pose initializer uses to propose poses for objects with multiple supports. The initializer generates and ranks candidates by geometric fit and source-image agreement. A coarse-to-fine refiner allocates a fixed search budget across supported objects and evaluates pose updates with physics rollouts. After an accepted update, it searches from the new pose at a finer translation and rotation scale. We benchmark our method on 50 scenes spanning four datasets. Compared with the strongest baseline, Support2Sim improves source-mask IoU after 10 seconds by 21.4 pp, realizes 87.1% versus 73.2% of intended supports, and reduces mean object drift from 79.6 to 45.3 mm. See our project page atthis https URLfor more details.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Benchmark: Localizing Released Autonomous Driving Models to Real Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31814)**  `arXiv:2611.31814`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Morui Zhu, Deyuan Qu, Qi Chen, Qing Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Released autonomous-driving models achieve strong benchmark performance, yet deploying them on a new real vehicle remains challenging. We study this deployment gap and find that released models can remain coupled to assumptions from their source sensing, data, and execution processes even after conventional adaptation. We introduce Released-to-Real (R2R), a deployment-localization methodology that identifies such source-system coupling and corrects only the affected components while preserving the released model. For data and behavior adaptation, we find that the mismatch between trajectory-level optimization and receding-horizon execution enters through the ego state rather than the observation, and introduce Human-Anchored Reinforcement Learning (HA-RL), which corrects it on recorded target driving without the costly rollouts of generative closed-loop reinforcement learning (RL). For physical sensor adaptation, R2R localizes and corrects source-dependent spatial and temporal representations with frozen perception weights, requiring neither target annotations nor retraining. HA-RL improves the closed-loop success rate of a released diffusion-based planner from 82.2% to 95.2%, while sensor adaptation across multiple released detectors and three real sensor rigs improves the vehicle mean average precision (mAP) of a released multi-camera detector from 0.041 to 0.420 on a five-camera target rig. These results provide a practical path for preparing released models before deployment on new real vehicles. Our project page is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Hi3R-VLA: Grounding Semantics and Actions in 3D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31535)**  `arXiv:2611.31535`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Shizhe Chen, Paul Pacaud, Cordelia Schmid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While recent vision-language-action (VLA) models increasingly incorporate 3D geometry, how to effectively exploit it for precise and generalizable action prediction remains a central challenge. We introduce Hi3R-VLA, a 3D policy that grounds heterogeneous modalities - vision, language, and action - in explicit 3D geometry for robotic manipulation. Our model integrates spatially-aligned visual features and language conditioning into point cloud representations, enriching scene geometry with fine-grained, task-relevant semantics. It then anchors low-level control commands in the same 3D coordinate frame and uses 3D-RoPE enhanced action-to-point attention over coarse-to-fine point features to model geometric relationships between action targets and observed points. Our analysis demonstrates the importance of semantic fusion within 3D and spatial alignment between observations and actions. With fewer than 250M trainable parameters, Hi3R-VLA achieves state-of-the-art performance on RoboCasa, RLBench, RoboTwin2, DexJoCo and DexSuite across diverse manipulation tasks and embodiments, from single-arm and dual-arm grippers to dexterous hands. Real-robot evaluations further demonstrate its effectiveness in the physical world. The model, code and dataset are publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HumanoidPose: Monocular 3D Pose Estimation for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27181)**  `arXiv:2611.27181`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Jinlin Gan, Mingzhe Li, Junsheng Zhang, Zhibo Zhou, Jiahan Geng, Yuhua Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As humanoid robots increasingly enter real-world environments, recovering their 3D whole-body pose from monocular imagery is becoming increasingly important for external state perception, motion analysis, and robot learning. However, Humanoid Robot Pose Estimation (HRPE) lies at the intersection of conventional Robot Pose Estimation (RPE) and Human Pose Estimation (HPE), while not being fully addressed by either paradigm: RPE primarily focuses on non-humanoid robots, particularly fixed-base manipulators, whereas HPE is built upon human-specific skeletal topologies, body models, and motion priors. Despite recent progress in humanoid 2D keypoint detection and HPE adaptation, humanoid-native 3D pose recovery remains insufficiently studied, and a dedicated benchmark with suitable training data and evaluation protocols is still lacking. To address this gap, we introduce Humanoids, a benchmark for monocular 3D humanoid robot pose estimation, and propose HumanoidPose, a framework for this task. Humanoids provides synthetic 3D supervision across diverse humanoid embodiments, manually annotated real-world 2D data, and real-world RGB sequences paired with robot states for 3D evaluation on a physical Roban humanoid. HumanoidPose combines human-inspired visual perception with robot articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. Experiments on Humanoids and the DHRP-H1 benchmark demonstrate strong performance in articulation recovery and 3D pose estimation, including zero-shot transfer to real-world 2D and 3D evaluation. Ablation studies further show the contribution of robot-native articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. The dataset and code will be publicly released to support future research in the community.
  </details>

- **[YUBI-STAG: Contact and Semantic-Rich Alignment for VLAs via Automated Video-Language Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09718)**  `arXiv:2610.09718`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Masatoshi Tateno, Takehiko Ohkawa, Yueh-Hua Wu, Hanlong Li, Tatsuya Matsushima, Yoichi Sato, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models acquire broad manipulation capabilities via large-scale pretraining, yet eliciting them through language requires fine-grained alignment between instructions and physical interactions. Existing robot demonstrations typically provide only coarse task descriptions, omitting how actions are executed, including which gripper acts, which object is contacted, and how it is grasped and moved. We introduce YUBI-STAG, a framework for Spatio-Temporal Annotation and Grounding that automatically enriches manipulation demonstrations with interaction-rich semantics to align pretrained VLAs with fine-grained manipulation language. Combining contact-object segmentation with vision-language models, YUBI-STAG annotates object identities, attributes and states, per-gripper actions, bimanual coordination, and spatially grounded interactions. To address YUBI-STAG's reliance on localized sequences and multi-stage VLM inference, we distill it into YUBI-VLM. YUBI-VLM directly recovers action structure and annotations from raw, unsegmented video in few inference calls and operates from wrist views alone. We evaluate both frameworks on YUBI-STAG-Bench across temporal, semantic, and spatial grounding tasks. YUBI-VLM retains much of YUBI-STAG's annotation accuracy with fewer inference calls and shorter runtime while generalizing to unseen manipulations. Finally, post-training VLA policies on these annotations aligns them with fine-grained language and contact-aware structure. Bimanual experiments demonstrate improved performance and instruction following, including control over object identity, acting gripper, target location, and spatial relations absent from original labels.
  </details>

- **[MRBench: Benchmarking Physical Necessity Reasoning in Heterogeneous Multi-Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32803)**  `arXiv:2611.32803`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yanzhe Chen, Zhijun Cao, Ruihe An, Chang Gong, Mike Zheng Shou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multi-robot manipulation have shown how robots can collaborate to accomplish complex tasks. However, successful coordination does not reveal which robots are actually needed. The same task may require different teams as robot capabilities change, resources become unavailable, or objects replace robotic support. We argue that reliable multi-robot manipulation requires physical necessity reasoning: understanding what a task demands, which resources can fulfill those demands, and which are truly necessary. We introduce MRBench, a benchmark for physical necessity reasoning in heterogeneous multi-robot manipulation. It comprises eight tasks with distinct concurrency and reach constraints, each evaluated under five configurations that change the required resources while preserving the task goal. Our evaluation reveals that existing coordination agents perform well with reference teams but struggle when robot embodiments change or passive objects can replace robotic support. To address this gap, we propose MRScissors, which separates task requirements from measured physical capabilities and identifies minimal feasible teams through constrained search. Across two model backbones, MRScissors achieves 85.4% and 91.2% team-selection accuracy, outperforming the strongest baselines by 10.4 and 9.5 points, respectively. Together, these findings expose a critical gap between coordinating robots and understanding their physical necessity.
  </details>

- **[On Agentic Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892)**  `arXiv:2611.09892`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Chen Chen, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. Seethis https URLfor code and videos.
  </details>

- **[CALVIN-MAX: When Does a Mid-Execution Change Hurt a Robot Policy?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31868)**  `arXiv:2611.31868`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Sajjad Pakdamansavoji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots must often keep working after the world changes, as when a block moves or the camera shifts while the robot still acts on what it saw before. Recent work showed that one change during a task lowers success for every policy tested, but did not separate the effect of its timing from the cost of the changed world, or measure what it costs afterwards. We introduce CALVIN-MAX, a benchmark of 7,828 cases on CALVIN's five-instruction chains, each with one change to the objects, the camera, the robot, or the instruction. Each case compares three runs that are identical until the affected instruction begins: one without the change, one with it in place from that moment, and one in which it arrives while the robot is moving. The second run against the first gives the cost of the changed world, and the third against the second the effect of meeting it mid-motion. On 1,526 held-out cases per policy, with hypotheses fixed in advance, each of ten released policies loses 4.4 to 9.9 percentage points on the affected instruction over the eight core changes. Whether timing matters depends on the change: one that alters what the robot sees costs 5.4 points less mid-motion than from the start, whereas a change to the scene itself or to the robot shows no detectable effect. Rewinding the robot to the start of the instruction removes the benefit for every policy; the stricter test we fixed in advance, that the rewound run then matches the second run, passes for three. The cost also outlasts the affected instruction: the change stays in place, and a policy that still completes that instruction is 5.0 to 16.8 points less likely to complete the next one.
  </details>

- **[CATWAY: Cost-sensitive agentic three-way robot decisions with calibrated language-model confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31694)**  `arXiv:2611.31694`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Erik Derner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language-model robot agent must decide whether to execute, question or refuse an instruction, and an unsafe execution costs far more than an unneeded question. No prior robot gate derives all its thresholds from error costs or acts on calibrated probabilities, and none was fitted in one domain and evaluated unchanged in another. We propose CATWAY, a cost-sensitive three-way decision gate: a loss matrix over four instruction conditions (clear, ambiguous, infeasible, unsafe) fixes execute, ask and refuse regions on the probability simplex, the question being the information-gathering step. Its probabilities fuse a ten-sample vote with graded scores of ambiguity, infeasibility and risk by logistic regression. Fitted on household instructions and frozen, CATWAY was tested on text-level decisions of three small models in-domain, in two new robot domains and on the external TRUST-NAV benchmark. At eleven generations per decision against one, it was significantly cheaper than a direct prompt, a cost-aware prompt and an unguarded agent in all 18 household comparisons (mean loss per item 0.28 against 0.57-0.83), in 9 of 18 in the new domains and in 10 of 18 on TRUST-NAV (Holm per set), and never significantly costlier. It executed none of the 142 unsafe test instructions. On a unanimous vote, the minimum-expected-loss rule reduces calibration to one loss-derived threshold, so for two of six model configurations a gate on the vote alone never executed a clear instruction, costing 0.49 per item on TRUST-NAV against CATWAY's 0.13 and 0.09; the fused estimate avoided this on every set.
  </details>

- **[Next-Manip: On-Demand Agent-Policy Collaboration for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32533)**  `arXiv:2611.32533`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayi Liu, Ke Ye, Jiaming Zhou, Yufei Huang, Haotian Liang, Kun-Yu Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose robotic manipulation requires task reasoning, failure recovery, and precise control, yet limited manipulation data make these abilities difficult to obtain from a single policy. Recent multimodal agents show promise in task reasoning and recovery but lack reliable fine-grained control and incur high inference cost, whereas learned policies provide efficient fine-grained execution but struggle beyond their learned skills. Although their complementary strengths suggest combining the two, doing so requires repeatedly deciding which executor should act as task demands change. Instead of relying on the agent for each decision, we present Next-Manip, an on-demand agent--policy collaboration framework that decouples executor allocation from costly agent inference. We propose an Agent Invocation Controller (AIC) that allocates control between the policy and the agent through a lightweight classifier of current execution demands. We further introduce Task Dynamics Context (TDC), which packages informative manipulation changes from in-context examples into compact task references to support both agent reasoning and executor allocation. Across two simulation benchmarks and real-world experiments, Next-Manip achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing agent inference costs.
  </details>

- **[ContinualDrive: A Deployment-Anchored, Multi-Axis Benchmark for Continual Updates of Learned Driving Planners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32529)**  `arXiv:2611.32529`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zirui Li, Zhiqi Mao, Yangang Ren, Heye Huang, Haohan Yang, Qihang Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned driving planners keep being updated after deployment as new logs arrive, yet continual learning evaluations rarely ask whether an update improves on the deployed model, and their verdicts rest on unstated evaluation choices. We present ContinualDrive, a deployment-anchored benchmark for post-deployment continual learning of driving planners. It updates a deployed planner on real-world nuPlan logs one scenario-type group at a time, evaluates the same checkpoints in open and closed loop, makes four measurement axes explicit (evaluation loop, reference checkpoint, temporal aggregation and stream protocol), and anchors a three-valued release verdict to the deployed checkpoint. We study two planners (PlanTF and Diffusion Planner) under full fine-tuning and adapter or LoRA updates, with and without replay or gradient projection, in three task orders. Verdicts can reverse or vanish when one axis changes: open-loop ADE ranks the final PlanTF model above the deployed one in half of its core-suite runs, whereas every closed-loop score ranks it below; backward transfer misses this loss, which on average is already present when each group has just been learned; and regime rankings vary far less across training seeds than across task orders. In the core suite of full and adapter updates, no regime measurably improved on the deployed planner in closed loop; on a panel more than ten times larger every evaluated final checkpoint trails it, and joint training on all groups recovers much of the loss without exceeding its nuPlan closed-loop score. We distill these findings into a reporting contract and will release the code and all checkpoints.
  </details>

- **[Roboclock: A Unified Benchmark for Time-Aware Robotic Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32233)**  `arXiv:2611.32233`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Zhao, Anthony L. Hu, Zimeng Huang, Zhijing Wu, Luobin Wang, Richard Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time is a fundamental dimension of robotic intelligence: a robot may fail despite physically correct actions if it acts outside a valid time window, responds too slowly to an event, or fails to meet required throughput. Yet existing robotic benchmarks primarily emphasize spatial correctness and logical task completion, largely overlooking the temporal constraints of real-world applications. We present RoboClock, a unified benchmark for time-aware robotic manipulation featuring 60 tasks across five application domains. Mirroring these failure modes, RoboClock structures tasks around three types of temporal constraints: Temporal Window, Event-Reactive, and Throughput. Furthermore, RoboClock incorporates a Latency-Aligned evaluation protocol that continuously advances environment time during model inference and control-interface delays, paired with time-sensitive metrics. Evaluating over 30 methods, we find that the best-performing approach is the trained VLA, yet it achieves only 8.3% temporal success even on Easy-level tasks, with persistent challenges in generalization and transfer. These findings reveal fundamental limitations in current robotic systems and highlight the need to jointly optimize physical competence, temporal reasoning, and execution latency.
  </details>

- **[FoveaWAM: Role-Aware 4D World-Action Modeling for Dynamic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31705)**  `arXiv:2611.31705`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaqi Yin, Baoyue Zhang, Jiyuan Zhang, Wenxuan Liu, Zhaofei Yu, Yue Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To manipulate a moving object, a policy must predict the scene at the moment its action executes, yet every millisecond spent predicting pushes that moment further away. Scene-level forecasts are computationally expensive, whereas target-centric forecasts can omit nearby objects that affect contact. Our key insight is that what a policy must predict decouples by role: only the target's pose, which determines contact, requires millimeter precision, while the surroundings matter primarily through the clearance they leave and the motion they impart. We present FoveaWAM, a native 4D world-action model that allocates precision according to these roles. Specifically, it models the target's past and future motion as a metric SE(3) trajectory, the surroundings as sparse 3D flow, and relies on the current frame for everything static. This compact representation lets FoveaWAM act in 15.1 ms on one RTX 5090, about 10 times faster than video world-action baselines, leaving substantially less time for the scene to evolve before action execution. To evaluate each policy under its own latency, we introduce OnTimeBench, a dynamic manipulation benchmark in which the scene keeps moving while the policy computes. FoveaWAM reaches 82.1% success on OnTimeBench, 20.8 points above the strongest baseline, and also leads on DOMINO and real-robot tasks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[RoboInF: A Benchmark for Instruction Fidelity in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30555)**  `arXiv:2611.30555`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Guo, Yu Huang, Dijiang Dong, Yue Liu, Peiran Ren, Wei Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise language control requires embodied models to follow instructions governing both task outcomes and execution. Although recent methods train policies to follow increasingly detailed commands, existing benchmarks often assess instruction following alongside perception, planning, and generalization to unfamiliar scenes. Higher task success therefore offers limited evidence of closer adherence to instructions. We introduce RoboInF, a benchmark measuring how reliably manipulation policies adapt their behavior to changes in an instruction. For each of eight tasks, six to twelve expert-validated instruction branches vary the requested goal, procedure, or manipulation detail while holding the initial scene and robot state fixed. Task-specific checkers verify the terminal goal and use recorded trajectories, contacts, and event sequences to assess compliance throughout execution. The accompanying RoboInF-4K dataset pairs 4,242 expert demonstrations with simulator-derived annotations of motion and contact. We evaluate leading manipulation policies ($\pi_{0.5}$ and OpenWAM) and inference-only agents (GPT-6 Astra and Qwen3.8-Flash). All four models exhibit a gap between task completion and faithful execution, indicating substantial room for fine-grained instruction following.
  </details>

- **[PyRUA-Dex: Learning Programmatic Policies for Dexterous Bimanual Manipulation with Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33144)**  `arXiv:2611.33144`  `cs.RO`  
  _Feixiang Ruan, Guodong Lv, Kewei Zhang, Wenbo Huang, Yintianrun Zhang, Qiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning for dexterous manipulation is limited by the scarcity of high-quality demonstrations: retargeting from human video is imprecise, teleoperation gives no force feedback to the operator, and exoskeleton-based collection is hard to scale up. We present PyRUA-Dex, a framework that enables coding agents to develop dexterous manipulation through programmatic policy, without the need of demonstrations. The programmatic policy is developed in two stages: during exploration, multiple agents explore in parallel for task skills and ways to compose them, and a distilling agent condenses the successful attempts into a task library and one program. During refinement, this program is improved through randomized layouts for enhanced robustness and execution efficiency. In simulated environments, PyRUA-Dex achieves SOTA on Bench2Dex with a score of 72.9 and reaches 81.0% success on DexJoCo under full randomization, ahead of every trained policy on both benchmarks. In real world experiments on a bimanual platform with two 22-DoF hands, PyRUA-Dex learns to play piano, write calligraphy, and other tasks efficiently: learning a new piece from sheet music takes as little as 30 minutes. These results suggest that PyRUA-Dex is an effective framework for developing dexterous manipulation policies with coding agents in the absence of high quality dexterous manipulation demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[EmbodiSafety: Benchmarking Risk Understanding and Response in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33054)**  `arXiv:2611.33054`  `cs.RO`  
  _Erfei Cui, Shengjia Zhao, Yan Qin, Kaixuan Wang, Junming Chen, Ping Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in embodied foundation models is largely measured by task success, while the physical safety of their decisions remains underexamined. Safety evaluations centered on rule following and binary judgments do not adequately assess whether embodied models understand environmental hazards and make safe decisions. We introduce EmbodiSafety, comprising 1,092 snapshot tasks and 540 trajectory tasks across 844 visual scenarios to assess safety over short and long horizons. Six response modes cover direct execution, refusal, clarification, constrained execution, autonomous recovery, and human escalation. Distinguishing recovery from human intervention makes the boundary of safe autonomy an explicit evaluation target. Our protocol assesses mode correctness, risk identification, and actionable handling, with complete decision accuracy requiring all applicable checks to pass. Claude-Opus-5.5 achieves the highest complete decision accuracy among reference models, reaching 56.1% on snapshots and 61.5% on trajectories. We further develop a data construction pipeline and curate 40k examples for safety alignment. Supervised training on this corpus raises the complete decision accuracy of Qwen3.5-2B and Qwen3.5-4B.
  </details>

- **[AG-WAM: Attention-Guided World Action Model for Accurate and Efficient Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32722)**  `arXiv:2611.32722`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hongcheng Zhang, Lei Sun, Songxuan Liu, Tengteng Pan, Yuqiang Zhai, Xianglong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently developed World Action Models (WAMs) jointly predict future observations and robot actions, providing predictive context for control. However, existing WAMs typically learn implicit attention allocation with limited explicit action-relevant attention guidance, leading to limited performance. To unleash strong capabilities of WAMs, we introduce explicit attention guidance as a unifying principle and propose our Attention-Guided WAM (AG-WAM), an end-to-end framework with three plug-and-play attention guidance modules that directly reformulate internal attention paradigm in WAMs: the Content-Adaptive Predictive Attention module, the Guidance-Point Visual Attention module, and the State-Conditioned Text Attention module. Without any external plugin module adopted, our end-to-end framework with the unifying principle of explicit attention guidance boosts the accuracy and efficiency of WAMs. Without using any private embodied training data, our AG-WAM establishes new state-of-the-art performance on multiple public robotic manipulation benchmarks including LIBERO-X, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin-2.0, significantly outperforming other methods, even those using massive private embodied training data by a notable margin, meanwhile notably improving training and inference efficiency.
  </details>

- **[DeCoNav-Act: Benchmarking Collective Physical Capability in Multi-Robot Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32683)**  `arXiv:2611.32683`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yunzi Wu, Sunyao Zhou, Xiaotao Zhang, Yucheng Deng, Yuehao Huang, Lin Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot teams can split workloads or combine complementary bodies, but only if each robot leaves the shared world in a state its teammate can use. Existing multi-robot benchmarks emphasize task allocation and skill sequencing, rarely testing whether the resulting physical states are usable. To evaluate collective physical capability, we introduce DeCoNav-Act, a benchmark for language-guided multi-robot mobile manipulation in a shared physics simulation. Its 350 homogeneous and 150 heterogeneous tasks, each verified by joint reference execution, span two coordination mechanisms: parallel workloads in shared space and physical dependencies in which one robot establishes a condition for a teammate to use. Beyond team success, DeCoNav-Act measures path efficiency and handoff coverage and success, and adds a diagnostic from verified pre-handoff states. Evaluating sensor-grounded adaptations of ASPIRE and RAX and our agent CoMoMa, we find that with GPT-5.6-luna no method completes a task, at most 8.8% of dependencies are reached, and even from pre-handoff states no handoff is completed; with GPT-6.1-sol, CoMoMa establishes the condition in 26.5% of trials, but only 4.1% succeed.
  </details>

- **[Follow-Bench 2.0: An End-to-End 3D Benchmark for Socially-Aware Robot Person Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32579)**  `arXiv:2611.32579`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hanjing Ye, Tianle Zeng, Jianwei Peng, Yanci Wen, Yuchen Zhou, Yonggen Ling, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Socially-aware robot person following (RPF) requires a mobile robot to follow a designated person in dynamic human environments while maintaining target identity, avoiding obstacles and pedestrians, and preserving socially comfortable formations. Existing benchmarks only partially evaluate this coupled problem: embodied visual tracking benchmarks emphasize target visibility with limited social interaction, person re-identification benchmarks study perception without closed-loop control, and motion-planning benchmarks often assume ground-truth target and pedestrian states. We introduce Follow-Bench 2.0, an end-to-end 3D benchmark for evaluating socially-aware RPF under coupled perception--planning challenges. Built in Unreal Engine, Follow-Bench 2.0 provides difficulty-leveled scenarios with diverse pedestrian flows, lighting and weather conditions, cluttered layouts, bottlenecks, queueing behaviors, distractors, and temporary target occlusions. The benchmark supports modular perception--planning pipelines, end-to-end visual policies, and foundation-model-based active trackers under a unified closed-loop protocol. It further evaluates both back- and side-following configurations and reports safety--comfort metrics covering task success, target visibility, target recovery, social-space intrusion, following formation, and motion smoothness. Experiments show that RPF-oriented modular pipelines substantially outperform current end-to-end visual policies on close and socially comfortable following, but also reveal that socially-aware RPF remains far from solved in interaction-heavy environments and especially in side-following. Follow-Bench 2.0 therefore provides a diagnostic platform for studying how perception errors, occlusions, crowd interactions, and following configurations jointly affect robot person following.
  </details>

- **[Self-supervised quality evaluation of grid maps without ground truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32550)**  `arXiv:2611.32550`  `cs.RO`  
  _Unal Artan, Manuel Castellano-Quero, Johannes A. Stork, Martin Magnusson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Maps are widely used in the robotics community for localization and path planning and the ability to identify incorrect or inaccurate regions of a map is an area of active research interest. For large environments, manual edits of the map can be very time consuming and a tool which can assist a map editor is needed. With this paper, we contribute to robot introspection through self-assessment of map quality, using only a given map as input to be independent of the underlying mapping algorithm. Current methods for map quality assessment rely on ground-truth or labelled information for training classifiers, both of which can be difficult to obtain. In this paper, we propose a self-supervised learning approach to map quality assessment of 2D occupancy grid maps which identifies regions of a map that require further investigation. We leverage an autoencoder for patches extracted from the map to capture the dominant structure of the map. By evaluating the reconstruction loss of a patch reconstructed with the learned model, we can evaluate whether it contains an unusual structure, which indicates the need of further investigation. We evaluate our method on the outcome of state-of-the-art grid mapping methods using benchmark datasets as well as maps from warehouse environments, highlighting the application to more complex environments. We show that the method identifies regions with sparse data, clutter, and misregistrations.
  </details>

- **[ViTacLab: Physically Consistent Visuo-Tactile Simulation, Benchmarking, and Representation Learning for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32476)**  `arXiv:2611.32476`  `cs.RO`  
  _Linjing You, Zeming Yang, Zhenhao Shen, Shengqiang Xu, Guanda Li, Wenxuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tactile sensing is essential for contact-rich robotic manipulation, but existing visuo-tactile simulators trade physical fidelity for scalability, and benchmarks largely focus on two-finger grippers. We present ViTacLab, a framework comprising ViTacSim, ViTacBench, and ViTacPolicy. ViTacSim uses a tri-modal tactile primitive: a global sparse contact-force lattice, a local dense force array, and tactile images. Anchored to PhysX contact-solver outputs, it corrects normal inconsistency through local stiffness alignment and reconstructs tangential forces under decoupled slip-stick regimes while preserving GPU parallelism. A joint projection preserves the sparse wrench and enforces local friction cones. ViTacBench spans parallel grippers, dexterous hands, and bimanual systems, with tasks ranging from vision-dominant to tactile-dominant regimes. ViTacPolicy aggregates variable numbers of heterogeneous tactile inputs using spatial-pose embeddings and a Transformer, and aligns force-array and tactile-image modalities through a two-stream VQ-VAE with a shared codebook. Controlled Hertzian-reference indentation and real-sensor sliding tests reduce normal and tangential force RMSE from 2.31 and 1.85 N to 0.42 and 0.53 N, respectively. Throughput reaches 2,482.9 tactile-pipeline FPS at 256 parallel environments. Simulation and real-world experiments show gains on several contact-rich tasks, while low absolute success on tactile-dominant tasks highlights the remaining difficulty of purely tactile control.
  </details>

- **[VLAs Execute, Code Recovers: 3D Skill Envelopes Bridge the Compositional Gap in Agentic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32245)**  `arXiv:2611.32245`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiajie Zhang, Yankai Xiang, Changhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Composing frozen Vision-Language-Action (VLA) skills under an agentic planner promises scalable long-horizon manipulation without long-horizon demonstrations, yet strong atomic skills routinely fail at seams even under a correct plan. Standard remedies reset the robot's pose or draft entry conditions in language, missing the physical cause we uncover via causal interventions: even after re-homing, a frozen VLA rigidly retraces its habitual trajectory and collides with displaced scene residue, whether or not it can see it. We propose the Skill Envelope, a policy-measured 3D geometric precondition capturing the volume a frozen skill sweeps during successful executions. Exploiting the insight that the rigidity making a frozen skill fragile at seams also makes its trajectory predictable in advance, we expose each envelope as two classical 3D tools (CHECK to detect blocking residue and FREE to locate safe placement regions), establishing a division of labor where VLAs execute in-distribution manipulation and code repairs out-of-distribution states. On LIBERO-Goal with frozen $\pi_{0.5}$, our zero-failure envelope predicts collisions at 0.946 AUROC (0.944 from one RGB-D view) and beats frontier VLMs on seam readiness (0.902 vs. 0.793); using it for skill ordering and code repair lifts success from 82.8% to 92.3% on two-step seams and from 29.7% to 93.8% on drawer-bearing 3-4 step chains, matching privileged world reset. Together, these results show that reliable VLA composition hinges on measuring a policy's physical workspace rather than guessing preconditions in language. Ongoing work closes the loop with autonomous LLM orchestration, synthesized free-object recovery, and cross-policy evaluation on cluttered benchmarks and real hardware.
  </details>

- **[KeyMemVLA: Subtask-Guided Key Memory for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32081)**  `arXiv:2611.32081`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yongwei Zhang, Tao Chen, Sicheng Ma, Yuanzhe Xing, Pengwei Zhang, Yongen Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory-dependent long-horizon manipulation requires vision-language-action (VLA) policies to infer task progress and recall evidence absent from current observations. Under a limited memory budget, the challenge is to retain history that resolves stage ambiguity and supports subsequent decisions. We present KeyMemVLA, a subtask-guided key memory framework based on progress indication and decision-evidence retention. A two-level annotation process selects subtasks satisfying either criterion and labels their final frames as keyframes to supervise online memory writes. A shared vision-language backbone jointly learns subtask, action, and keyframe prediction. At inference, the policy first predicts the current subtask from the instruction, visual context, and memory, then uses this semantic condition to predict actions and future memory-write positions. Actual observations are retained at the selected times during execution. We introduce KMBench to evaluate active memory retention for decisions across subtasks. Evaluations on RMBench-$M(n)$, RoboTwin-MeM, KMBench, and real-world manipulation tasks show higher average success rates than the compared baselines. Ablation studies and representation analysis support the roles of key memory and explicit task-progress semantics. These results highlight the value of coupling sparse key memory with online progress inference for history-dependent control.
  </details>

- **[PreWAM: Exploring the Design of Latent Future Prediction for World-Action Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31901)**  `arXiv:2611.31901`  `cs.RO`  
  _Wan Chi, Sen Li, Zihang Cui, Chuqin Huang, Yunlong Wang, Nanshan Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models combine action generation with future prediction, allowing visual transitions to serve as supervision for policy learning. Our controlled study of prediction spaces shows that spatially structured semantic features support better overall generalization under visual perturbations and make motion information more readily recoverable from learned predictive representations. Building on these observations, we introduce PreWAM, a world-action model that organizes future supervision around state changes in a prediction space defined by pretrained DINO features. Specifically, Residual World Prediction jointly learns source-relative changes and their resulting future states to emphasize dynamic information while preserving future-state semantics. Temporal-Scale Prediction conditions world queries on temporal distance to capture both short-term local motion and longer-horizon state evolution. These predictive objectives jointly shape a shared vision-language backbone with action supervision during pretraining and downstream policy learning. Experiments across the LIBERO benchmark family, SimplerEnv, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world robotic manipulation demonstrate strong manipulation performance and improved generalization and robustness under diverse distribution shifts.
  </details>

- **[RESET: One Demonstration, One Verified World, Self-Improved Skills Across Embodiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31894)**  `arXiv:2611.31894`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zheng Sun, Zhuo Li, Zhihao Li, Hengyi Sim, Ge Wang, Ziyue Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) coding agents can iteratively self-improve robot manipulation skills in simulation benchmarks, yet real-world tasks provide neither ground-truth success scorers nor resettable practice environments. Our key observation is that a single real demonstration simultaneously specifies scene geometry, defines the terminal success criterion, and serves as a physical reference trajectory for verifying world fidelity---all decoupled from the source robot's kinematics. We present {RESET}: we reconstruct a contact-rich world and derive a frozen read-only criterion from one demonstration ({R}ebuild); check that world against the demonstration by open-loop replay and silhouette consistency ({E}xamine); evolve modular white-box task graphs and skill code from per-node failure evidence ({S}elf-improve); deploy frozen code on real robots with zero runtime LLM calls ({E}xecute); and share the verified world and criterion with unseen robots without new demonstrations ({T}ransfer). On 8 manipulation tasks, each rebuilt from one real demonstration, RESET achieves 97% average success on unseen layouts in simulation, whereas zero-shot code reaches 27%, per-task fine-tuned pi0.5 policies 31%, and Diffusion Policy 50%; injecting reconstruction defects shows that practice tolerates many reconstruction errors, that geometric errors can still stall or mislead it, and that the check flags most of them while physical randomization covers the rest. Without any target demonstrations, 3 heterogeneous robots acquire the same task skills in the shared world: a robot with similar arms copies 95% of its skill functions unchanged from the source robot, while robots with different arms and hands write their own. In real-robot experiments, the resulting programs run on 4 robots and succeed in 80% of 280 trials.
  </details>

- **[Co-DRIS: Physics-Informed Predictive Belief and Variable-Impedance Learning for Reactive Catching under Coupled Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31843)**  `arXiv:2611.31843`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yonglong Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reactive robotic manipulation is acutely sensitive to simultaneous sensing noise, measurement dropouts, joint execution errors, and contact parameter variations. While single-frame frameworks like Domain-Randomized Instance Set (DRIS) achieve strong robustness under isolated uncertainties (86.4%), they degrade severely when perception and actuation uncertainties co-occur (27.6% across 10 coupled stress scenarios) due to memoryless encoding and fixed high-stiffness PD control. We propose Co-DRIS + SuperMimic, a perception-action co-design framework coupling: (i) an inertial world-frame physics-informed world model combining exact ballistic kinematics with a zero-initialized contact residual to roll forward missing observations and accelerate training via zero-cost counterfactual dropout consistency; (ii) an action-conditioned dual-stream causal GRU belief encoder resolving the denoising-vs-lag deadlock; and (iii) phase-aware variable-impedance soft-catching that transitions the 7-DoF Franka Research 3 arm into an overdamped regime (zeta/zeta0 ~ 2.26x) at the torque layer. Under unbiased 3-seed tail evaluation on unseen throws (15,360 throws/scenario), our method achieves 89.6 +/- 0.4% on standard benchmarks (+10.5% on 4-sigma noise to 70.5%) and 66.6 +/- 0.5% across 10 coupled/dropout stress scenarios (+39.0% over DRIS; +30.4% on 50% dropout). We further validate impedance decoupling, causal contact triggering under +0.65 s flight-time shifts, and cross-task transfer to steel-plate ping-pong bouncing (87.5% to 100.0%) and FR3+Hand catching (+8.4% under 4x noise with 6-13% lower peak impact force).
  </details>

- **[Exteroceptive Sensor Faults in Autonomous Vehicles: A Cross-Modal Taxonomy and Fault Injection Toolkit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31667)**  `arXiv:2611.31667`  `cs.RO`  
  _Davide Possenti, Daniele Cianca, Stefano Arrigoni, Francesco Braghini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous vehicles rely on cameras, LiDAR, and radar to perceive their surroundings, yet these exteroceptive sensors can be degraded by hardware aging, environmental interference, miscalibration, embedded software errors, and combinations of these causes, with direct consequences for perception quality and safety. Existing surveys and benchmarks mostly address individual sensors, isolated conditions, or vehicle-level consequences, and no common vocabulary relates fault origins across the three modalities. This paper reviews the literature on exteroceptive sensor faults and proposes a cross-modal, origin-driven taxonomy that organizes them into five categories, namely hardware, environmental, calibration, software, and synergistic faults, mapping each entry to its physical or logical mechanism, observable manifestation, and affected subsystem, and relating the categories to the safety analyses of ISO 26262 and ISO 21448. To make the taxonomy operational, we release an open-source fault injection toolkit with 94 symptom-level routines (41 camera, 27 LiDAR, and 26 radar), four graded severity levels, reproducible burst scheduling, and per-frame labels recording the fault category, affected subsystem, and severity. Examples on the Boreas dataset illustrate how each category alters camera images, LiDAR point clouds, and radar scans. Together, the taxonomy and the toolkit provide a shared reference and a controlled testbed for studying sensor reliability, developing and benchmarking fault detection and diagnosis methods, and building fault-annotated datasets for the safety validation of perception systems. The toolkit is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FabricBench: A Standardized Policy Benchmark for Bimanual Fabric Manipulation with a Contact-Driven Deformable Simulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31577)**  `arXiv:2611.31577`  `cs.RO`  
  _Wenkang Hu, Haoran Wang, Liu Liu, Wenchao Huang, Yiji Chen, Mengao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fabric manipulation challenges robot policies with changing geometry, self-occlusion, and contact, yet standardized evaluation remains focused on rigid objects. We introduce FabricBench, a benchmark for bimanual fabric manipulation with six tasks spanning garments, silk, and bags, 10,000 simulation-ready assets, and controlled tests of configuration and appearance generalization. Built on an open-source solver in Newton, our simulator enables stable bimanual grasping, holding, and release through contact forces, without artificial attachment. Redesigned cloth-rigid contact and bidirectional force coupling support interactions among robot arms, fabric, and free rigid objects, allowing a bag to support its contents and transmit their weight to the grippers. In a controlled grasp-hold-release evaluation, our solver succeeds in all 45 trials, compared with none for the tested baseline configurations. Evaluating six representative policies, including vision-language-action models and world action models, reveals substantial remaining challenges: the best policy achieves 77% average success, while no policy exceeds 36% on long-horizon T-shirt folding. Under the most deformed initial T-shirt configurations, success falls to 6-14% across policies, with larger drops than under the evaluated appearance shifts. Zero-shot deployment of $\pi_{0.5}$ on a real robot largely preserves task difficulty ordering across six tasks (Spearman $\rho=0.94$). These results establish FabricBench as a reproducible testbed for measuring fabric-manipulation capabilities and exposing limitations in configuration generalization. The project website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EvoMisty: Building, Optimizing, and Evolving Capabilities on a Social Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31341)**  `arXiv:2611.31341`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xiao Wang, Lu Dong, Ifeoma Nwogu, Srirangaraj Setlur, Venu Govindaraju_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social robots face diverse and changing tasks in companionship and education. LLM-based methods can generate task programs, but they still rely on manually developed control interfaces and predefined capabilities. They also lack a general mechanism to optimize completed tasks based on user-specified metrics and reuse performance improvements acrossthis http URLpresent EvoMisty, a plugin-based framework that enables coding agents to build and continually improve robot capabilities. EvoMisty builds a control program from official documentation and verifies candidate capabilities on a physical robot. Verified capabilities are stored in an atomic capability library and combined with existing skills to solve tasks. After task completion, EvoMisty can collect execution data, measure user-specified metrics, and iteratively improve task programs. It then distills reusable skills from final solutions into a skill library for futurethis http URLevaluate EvoMisty on a physical Misty II robot using Claude Code and Codex. The two agents construct 109 and 107 verified atomic capabilities, respectively. Both complete all 28 AutoMisty benchmark tasks and seven additional tasks beyond AutoMisty's capabilities. Optimization improves all seven evaluated metrics across five tasks. For example, dialogue response latency decreases by 52% and 71%, respectively. Reusing skills from one optimized dialogue task reduces latency by 33% to 72% in three other dialogue tasks without further optimization. All code and experimental videos will be publicly released through the webpage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ConsisX: Cross-View Consistency Thinking in 3D for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30308)**  `arXiv:2611.30308`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yunhe Zhang, Long Gao, Yuedong Fu, Gang He, Li Xu, Chang Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models integrate visual perception and language understanding from vision-language models (VLMs) with action prediction for robotic manipulation. Although multi-view observations provide complementary scene information, VLMs model cross-view interactions based on 2D feature similarity, which can lead to physical inconsistency in the form of cross-view incorrect association between distinct entities and cross-view information fragmentation about the same entity. To address these challenges, we propose ConsisX, a novel VLA framework that integrates cross-view association and entity coherence to enable cross-view consistency thinking in 3D for physically consistent robotic manipulation. Specifically, Cross-View Association Modeling (CAM) is proposed to introduce cross-view spatial proximity constraints in a shared 3D space to reduce cross-view incorrect association. Entity Coherence Organization (ECO) is designed to organize cross-view local information into coherent 3D entity evidence, thereby addressing cross-view information fragmentation. Consistent Action Conditioning (CAC) is introduced to condition action prediction on task-relevant 3D entity evidence for cross-view consistent action execution. Experiments on 3 real-world manipulation tasks and 4 simulation benchmarks establish ConsisX as an effective and competitive framework for robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[SuperNav-1.5: Structured Spatial Memory For Agentic Navigation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29912)**  `arXiv:2611.29912`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiarui Guo, Jinkai Zhang, Yuanhong Yu, Yongchen Zai, Futang Zhu, Ruizhen Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper addresses the challenge of scene-level memory design for efficient navigation across sessions and successive object goals. Recent methods commonly represent a scene using scene graphs or image--text records. However, dense scene-graph representations impose substantial reasoning and context overhead on multimodal large language models, while image--text records cannot be directly converted into executable geometric routes. To address these limitations, we propose SuperNav-1.5, an agentic navigation system equipped with a structured spatial memory constructed by the agent. The memory consists of an area-segmented occupancy map annotated with agent-maintained, map-grounded memory nodes. This map guides the agent toward unvisited areas and provides executable routes to known locations. For multi-session navigation, we introduce agent-guided relocalization, whereby the agent infers an area prior from current observations and stored memory records to constrain geometric matching and localize the robot within the stored map. Together, these designs enable efficient navigation across session and successive object goals. Experiments show that our method outperforms baselines on single-object, multi-object, and demand-driven navigation benchmarks, with particularly strong gains in path efficiency. Multi-session evaluation demonstrates that the constructed memory can be reused, while deployment on a real quadruped robot further shows the system's applicability. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[LocoMuJoCo: A Comprehensive Imitation Learning Benchmark for Whole-Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26293)**  `arXiv:2611.26293`  `cs.RO`  
  _Firas Al-Hafez, Davide Tateo, Oleg Kaidanov, Aditya Bhatt, Artur Zelik, Zhengyi Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LocoMuJoCo is an open-source benchmark for imitation learning (IL) and reinforcement learning (RL) on whole-body control. The library couples MuJoCo (CPU) with MJX and the MjWarp backend (GPU) under a single API, exposes 9 humanoid, 3 (musculo-)skeletal, and 4 quadruped embodiments, and ships a curated native motion-capture dataset for every humanoid, to which it can also retarget AMASS and LAFAN1. A modular plug-in architecture makes observations, rewards, control modes, terrains, and domain randomization fully interchangeable, while compact JAX implementations of many RL and IL algorithms provide JIT-compiled end-to-end training, and the companion loco-mujoco-real repository provides sim-to-real deployment. We describe the design, the SMPL-to-robot and robot-to-robot retargeting pipeline, and a usage example. LocoMuJoCo is released under MIT atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RoboValue: A Fine-Grained Sim-and-Real Benchmark for Unified Evaluation of Robotic Value Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26663)**  `arXiv:2610.26663`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shengbang Liu, Zhengye Du, Zhilong Wan, Honghao Su, Chenxiang Xia, Chang Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic value models assess task execution from visual observations and instructions, providing feedback on execution quality and progress toward the task goal. However, existing evaluations cover only selected aspects of execution understanding, leaving the reliability of value feedback insufficiently tested. We introduce RoboValue, a fine-grained sim-and-real benchmark for unified evaluation of robotic value models. Its framework evaluates four complementary capability dimensions through shared interfaces: task-state understanding, temporal progress monitoring, failure and recovery reasoning, and value consistency. Diagnostic trajectories and instruction variations test whether value judgments accurately reflect task requirements and changes throughout execution. RoboValue-Dataset covers 15 simulation and 20 real-world manipulation tasks, providing expert demonstrations for training and annotated trajectories for fine-grained evaluation. We evaluate representative robotic value models and general-purpose multimodal models in zero-shot and one-shot settings under standard conditions and separate shifts in robot embodiment and environment. Models trained to grade partial outcomes and individual task steps exhibit strong fine-grained grounding, while reasoning over observation histories supports tracking of accumulated task effects. Strong forward-progress and memory scores can nevertheless coexist with poor responses to task regression. Even models with strong grounding and progress estimates struggle to recognize effective recovery and to give consistent feedback within and across valid executions. These findings identify concrete targets for value supervision, while the dataset and public leaderboard support continued assessment of feedback reliability. The website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ComVLA: Communication-Aware Split Inference for VLA Models in 6G-Connected Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07838)**  `arXiv:2609.07838`  `cs.RO`  
  _Boliang Liu, Wint Yi Poe, Jingyun Di, Riccardo Trivisonno, Giuseppe Caire_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Connected robotics is an emerging 6G application where mobile robots follow natural-language instructions to manipulate physical objects. The Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models that enable this are too large to run on the robot; a common trend is to offload inference to the cloud. The wireless link, however, limits how much sensing data the edge can transmit per control step. Two recent lines address this constraint: semantic communication codecs compress sensor data but require channel-specific retraining, and VLA token pruners select tokens from image but ignore the channel. Our insight is that the dense semantic information contained in the language already indicates which visual tokens matter. We propose ComVLA, a framework that uses this language guidance to adapt the VLA token budget to the channel capacity. Transmitting 32 tokens instead of 512 on the LIBERO benchmark, ComVLA cuts inference compute by 74% and inference latency by 22% versus the original OpenVLA-OFT baseline, at a cost of 1.5 pp in average task success (95.4% vs. 96.9%), and it stays within the capacity budget under Rayleigh and Rician fading. These results demonstrate that co-designing VLA inference and wireless communication is a practical direction for 6G-connected robotics.
  </details>

- **[Explore From Sketch: Accelerating UAV Exploration in Large-scale Environments with Prior Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11708)**  `arXiv:2606.11708`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tiancheng Lai, Yuman Gao, Xiangyu Li, Ruitian Pang, Xingpeng Wang, Siqi Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous exploration with UAVs in large-scale, topologically complex environments often suffers from low efficiency due to suboptimal scheduling and detours. Prior maps (e.g., construction drawings), although usually imprecise and flawed, are readily available in many scenarios and have the potential to provide global structural guidance. This paper presents a novel exploration framework that leverages sparse, unaligned, and even discrepant 2D prior maps for LiDAR-based UAV exploration. First, a robust 2D-3D point cloud registration pipeline is proposed to align LiDAR observations with prior maps. The registration pipeline combines a GeoContext descriptor for single-frame candidate retrieval, a multi-frame verification mechanism for coarse transformation estimation with outlier rejection, and a Scale-ICP algorithm for refinement. The registration module can handle map discrepancies and provide multiple hypotheses when geometric ambiguities arise. To effectively utilize the registration results for exploration planning, we further develop a hierarchical viewpoint planning strategy under localization uncertainties. The hierarchical strategy first spatially attaches local viewpoints to prior guidepoints and adopts a Monte Carlo Tree Search solver to determine their traversal sequence under each registration hypothesis. To mitigate registration uncertainty, a risk-aware selector evaluates prior sequences using confidence-weighted travel risk, and a fixed-endpoint traveling salesman problem is formulated to generate an efficient local coverage path under the selected prior guidance. Benchmark evaluations reveal up to 34.2% improvement in exploration efficiency and 37.9% reduction in flight distance compared to state-of-the-art methods, while extensive simulations and field experiments further demonstrate robustness to prior map incompleteness and deformations.
  </details>

- **[Follow-Bench: A Unified Motion Planning Benchmark for Socially-Aware Robot Person Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10796)**  `arXiv:2509.10796`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hanjing Ye, Weixi Situ, Jianwei Peng, Yu Zhan, Bingyi Xia, Kuanqi Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot person following (RPF)---mobile robots that follow and assist a specific person---has emerging applications in personal assistance, security patrols, eldercare, and logistics. To be effective, such robots must follow the target while ensuring safety and comfort for both the target and surrounding people. In this work, we present the first comprehensive study of RPF, which (i) surveys representative scenarios, motion-planning methods, and evaluation metrics with a focus on safety and comfort; (ii) introduces Follow-Bench, a unified benchmark simulating diverse scenarios, including various target trajectory patterns, crowd dynamics, and environmental layouts; and (iii) re-implements eight representative RPF planners, ensuring that both safety and comfort are systematically considered. Extensive simulation experiments provide a quantitative study of safety- and comfort-related physical indicators, while real-world trials of two MPC-based implementations on a differential-drive robot illustrate deployment feasibility and practical following behaviors. These results reveal open challenges and future research directions.
  </details>

- **[Measuring Counterfactual Credit in a Replayable Wildfire-Suppression Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31499)**  `arXiv:2611.31499`  `cs.MA` `cs.LG`  
  _Alireza Moghaddasi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning, learned critics are usually evaluated by how well they predict the team's return. Yet predicting a team's return and assigning credit to individual actions are distinct tasks. To test credit directly, we introduce FLARE (Fireline Learning Agents in a Replayable Environment), a stochastic wildfire suppression benchmark in which any moment of a fire can be replayed with different actions under the same random events. Replaying one step measures the immediate effect of each agent's suppression, which we call simulator credit and use for training. Replaying to the end of the episode measures the effect of each action, which we use for grading critics. In a comparison over eight seeds with multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO), simulator credit reduces the interquartile mean burn score (each cell's burning time, capped and averaged over the map; lower is better) from 26.6% with shared reward to 20.9%. Learned credit from a centralized COMA-style critic does no better than the shared reward. Four trained critics predict the team's return well, yet the credit they assign is 9 to 42 times larger than the credit measured by replay. The critics also give heterogeneous agents nearly identical action rankings. These results show that a good fit to the team's return is a weak test of a critic's credit.
  </details>

- **[Unifying Partner and Environment Diversity to Improve Human-AI Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32031)**  `arXiv:2611.32031`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Patrick Q. Zhang, Yancheng Liang, Adrienne Fairhall, Natasha Jaques_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world applications of modern AI systems require generalization to both unseen environments and novel human users. The field of zero-shot coordination (ZSC) has focused primarily on deploying AI to cooperate with novel partners in otherwise familiar environments. Popular population-based methods achieve this by partnering the agent with a diversity of simulated human users during training in a fixed environment, but does not consider the more realistic scenario where both the users and the environment are unseen during training. Recently, training across environments has been explored in training a single self-play agent, which shows better generalization on both novel human users and unseen environments. In this work, we ask whether jointly modeling partner diversity and environment diversity yields stronger coordination with novel partners in novel environments. We introduce Cross Environment Co-Play (CECP), a two-stage framework that first trains a population of self-play agents across a distribution of procedurally generated environments, and then trains a cooperator policy against this partner population. To further couple social and environmental reasoning, we add a next-state prediction module that encourages representations to encode both partner behavior and task structure. We evaluate CECP on both a neuroscience-inspired cooperative foraging task, and the popular ZSC benchmark Overcooked, under held-out partner and held-out environment conditions. CECP achieves the strongest performance among the compared baselines and attains state-of-the-art results under our evaluation protocol for human-AI collaboration. We analyze the model's latent representation encoded by the next-state prediction module, showing it can not only improve the performance of the cooperative AI, but also provide more interpretable latents, demonstrated by the inference of partner skill levels.
  </details>

- **[EvolveMAS: A Unified Self-Evolution Framework for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34450)**  `arXiv:2610.34450`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Yalun Wu, Qiwei Ma, Bingbing Xu, Dan Zhang, Long Bai, Boyang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decentralized multi-agent systems (MAS) enable large language model agents to interact without centralized control. Agents need to adapt to other agents at test time and reuse accumulated experience to refine their interaction strategies for future interactions across cooperative, mixed-motive, and adversarial settings. However, existing approaches often develop adaptation mechanisms for particular interaction settings. This raises a central question: how can decentralized MAS support test-time adaptation and reliable experience reuse across interaction settings? We propose EvolveMAS, a unified self-evolution framework that supports experience-driven adaptation within and across episodes. EvolveMAS pairs each main agent with a dedicated MemoryAgent for test-time memory governance, which maintains isolated Structured Memory outside the main agent's reasoning context. EvolveMAS organizes adaptation into two complementary processes: Within-Episode Online Adaptation and Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution. During Within-Episode Online Adaptation, MemoryAgent updates episodic, semantic, and working memory from interaction evidence to inform subsequent actions. During Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution, the main agent uses memory and episode outcomes to distill reusable interaction knowledge comprising partner profiles and interaction strategies for subsequent episodes. Across five benchmarks and six evaluation configurations spanning the three interaction settings, EvolveMAS achieves the highest normalized average score of 77.2 among the compared methods. Ablations show that test-time memory governance and cross-episode knowledge reuse both improve performance. Interaction traces further illustrate how updated memory and distilled knowledge guide subsequent interactions. These results support experience-driven adaptation in decentralized MAS without updating model parameters.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Collective Intelligence: Emergence on an Equal-Budget Ladder in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32606)**  `arXiv:2611.32606`  `cs.MA`  
  _Lixuan He, Shengqu Cai, Fang Wu, Shikang Zheng, Liwei Jiang, Manling Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly work as teams of agents, and a team is expected to solve problems beyond the reach of any single member. Most evaluations credit a team for outperforming a single agent, without accounting for the additional computation: a team may consume many times the tokens of one agent, and a plain vote over independent answers already explains most of the reported gain. Two questions therefore remain open: When does interaction itself make a group smarter, and can a group improve its next generation by learning from its own work? We answer both with the equal-budget ladder, which grades a group against independent agents at three levels: outperforming a single agent, a compute-matched majority vote, and compute-matched pass@n. On this ladder we define Recursive Collective Intelligence (RCI) as a group whose own output, without a teacher or external labels, makes its next generation smarter at equal cost. Three findings follow. First, interaction improves collective intelligence when it moves information across agents: forwarding split facts lifts groups above pass@n at three model scales, by about a third at 27B. Second, organization emerges spontaneously: identical 27B agents form hubs and improve accuracy by 18 percentage points; encoding their organization in a prompt enables them to reach level 3 at less than half the token budget in a descriptive comparison. Third, groups recursively improve themselves: Two label-free generations progressively climb the ladder, the second 9B generation surpassing both pass@$n$ and a control trained on base-model turns; on PlanBench, HiddenBench and Hanabi, each generation outperforms its predecessor on unseen problems for four to six generations. A group therefore outperforms its members when it forwards useful information more efficiently than it spends tokens on coordination, and learns to do so from its own work.
  </details>

- **[Near-Zero Forgetting Is Not Enough: Out-of-Distribution Robustness in Continual Learning of Pretrained VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31511)**  `arXiv:2611.31511`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Adrià Ruiz, Bartłomiej Twardowski, Tinne Tuytelaars, Sergi Masip_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work shows that pretrained Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models exhibit near-zero forgetting in specific continual learning settings with experience replay. However, robustness to domain shift of learned tasks remains unexplored. In this work, we study the robustness of Pi0 fine-tuned with experience replay under out-of-distribution (OOD) data using the LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks. While the in-domain (ID) setup achieves near-zero catastrophic forgetting, OOD performance on previous tasks quickly collapses. Specifically, we show that during the continual learning process, the OOD capabilities of the model degrade, following a shared representational pattern across domain shifts, which is also visible behaviorally in the two simpler suites. Representational analysis shows that drift concentrates in the multimodal and action features. Linear probes indicate that OOD features still retain task-relevant information, but appear unreadable, consistent with representation misalignment. These findings suggest that even when ID stability remains intact, severe OOD forgetting can still occur, accompanied by representational drift during continual learning.
  </details>

- **[Quantile-Coupled Flow Matching for Distributional Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08515)**  `arXiv:2605.08515`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Michael Groom, Victor-Alexandru Darvariu, Lars Kunze, James Wilson, Nick Hawes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distributional Reinforcement Learning (DRL) models the full return distribution, making it well-suited to uncertainty-aware and risk-sensitive decision-making. Conditional Flow Matching (CFM) critics are increasingly adopted in DRL, offering dense velocity supervision and a natural axis for scaling critic computation. Yet they carry a hidden metric mismatch: the distributional Bellman operator is a contraction in the Wasserstein distance, but CFM critics pair source and Bellman target samples arbitrarily, so their loss does not control the Wasserstein distance to the Bellman target. We show that this mismatch can be fixed with a single sorting operation.
