Our method, FlowIQN, sorts source and target samples within each mini-batch so that each source quantile is transported to its matching target quantile, approximating the monotone optimal transport coupling. We prove that this quantile coupling makes the CFM loss upper-bound, up to a constant, the squared 2-Wasserstein projection error. To our knowledge, this makes FlowIQN the first flow-matching critic whose training is an approximate Wasserstein projection of the Bellman target, and the guarantee extends to shortcut models for few-step inference. Empirically, the quantile coupling recovers multi-modal returns more accurately than an otherwise identical uncoupled flow with as few as two integration steps. FlowIQN improves substantially on the flow critic Value Flows in a fixed-policy diagnostic and is competitive on offline RL benchmarks across multiple policy extraction methods, identifying the coupling as a central design axis for flow-based critics. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Hidden Clues in WM-811K: Uncovering the Spatial Signals Behind Wafer Map Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31292)**  `arXiv:2611.31292`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Kabir Tolani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  WM-811K (also known as LSWMD) is a wafer map dataset first made publicly available in a 2015 paper by Wu et al. Convolutional neural networks have been used to achieve high classification accuracy on this dataset, yet it's not easy to decipher which features of the dataset are driving that performance. This study attempts to answer this specifically for WM-811K: how much of the predictive performance results from the wafer layout, the count of failed dies, their distance from the center and their exact 2-D location. After analyzing duplicate wafer maps, removing same wafer maps with conflicting labels and ensuring that the same manufacturing lot or exact same map doesn't appear in training, validation and test data, we were left with 25,513 defect-labeled wafer maps from eight classes. We then constructed four variations of each wafer map. The Original was the actual wafer map present in the dataset. The Layout-only kept only the layout of the non-background dies. The Global-shuffle kept the count of failed dies while shuffling them around. The Radial-shuffle randomized the locations of failed dies only in their radial bin, which was chosen out of 8 radial bins based on distance from the center. The data split, preprocessing and CNN architecture remained the same for all four of them. Macro-F1 progressed like this: 0.3829 (Layout-only), 0.5803 (Global-shuffle), 0.7439 (Radial-shuffle) and 0.8563 (Original). These results show that WM-811K contains useful information at several levels: wafer layout, number of failures, distribution of failed dies in a given radius, and exact 2-D locations of the failed dies. Therefore, high classification performance on this benchmark does not come only from learning the exact defect configuration.
  </details>

- **[Fix How It Acts, Not How It Thinks: Surgical Fine-Tuning of Frontier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32064)**  `arXiv:2611.32064`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Sandeep Singh Sandha, Prann Bansal, Arvind Sundararajan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-weight LLMs are competent software engineers, but they sometimes fail at tasks they are otherwise able to do. Often it is not a failure of reasoning. It is a few small missteps over a long trajectory (repeating a step that has already failed, or declaring the task done while a requirement is still unmet) that make the difference. Correcting this calls for a surgical weight update that steers the model toward the right actions without interfering with its reasoning. Such updates, typically learned with LoRA adapters, are often too small to survive merging into a released checkpoint. In our BF16 release, the median weight change is only 1.5% of a single rounding step, and merging loses roughly two thirds of the update's norm. When the same adapter is merged into the mixed FP8/NVFP4 release of the same model, 93% of the update disappears. Instead of increasing the size of the update, we directly train on the surviving part. We introduce merge-aware fine-tuning. Standard LoRA rounds the merged weights to the storage format only at export, so the loss never sees the rounded weights. We instead round the sum of base weights and adapter update to the release format at every training step, keeping the vendor's quantization scales, and compute the loss on these weights. The forward pass matches the serving engine numerics, and the gradient passes straight through the rounding, so the model we train is the model we ship. The loss optimizes only the model's actions and keeps its reasoning close to the base model. Applied to Nemotron-3.5, the recipe improves agentic coding in both the BF16 and the NVFP4 release, by up to 8.4 points on Terminal-Bench 2.1, and we publicly release both models.
  </details>

- **[Byte Patches versus BPE at Matched Data: A Matched-Twin Comparison at 350M Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31790)**  `arXiv:2611.31790`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Asadullah Maliyawala, Andrej Lisal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We compare a MEGABYTE-style byte-patch Transformer with a 64-byte sliding local decoder (local64) to a matched byte-level 8k BPE decoder (BPE-8k) under identical raw bytes, document order, and approximately matched bytes per optimizer step: 350M parameters and 10 billion training bytes. Under matched-span document sliding (identical scored byte ranges), BPE-8k leads with relative gaps of -5.2%, -4.9%, -9.3% and -6.1% in bits per byte (overall, text, code, math; paired bootstrap). Downstream, CharBench equal fine-tuning favors local64 (53.7% vs 40.0%), fact200 favors BPE-8k (16.5% vs 7.0%), and PIQA (raw and length-normalized) and ARC-Easy length-normalized favor BPE-8k after Holm correction at alpha = 0.05; remaining tasks are ties. Evaluation pitfalls that changed conclusions include context-length extrapolation past the BPE training context, bits per token reported as bits per byte, and inconsistent byte denominators. Unlike controlled tokenizer studies that vary the subword tokenizer under a fixed token budget, or FLOP-controlled byte-model scaling studies, we hold the raw bytes, their order and the optimizer recipe fixed and ask whether a fixed-patch byte model can replace the tokenizer at all. Limits: one size, one seed, one 8k vocabulary fixed before the runs, and a known difference in embedding initialization between the twins.
  </details>

- **[Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation of RL Experts: Toward Continual Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27376)**  `arXiv:2611.27376`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhen Zhang, Changyi Yang, Xuehai He, Chengzhi Liu, Yutong Yin, Ziyue Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RL post-training typically produces domain experts that must then be consolidated into one model, and as new experts keep arriving, this consolidation becomes a continual process. Weight merging and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) must re-consolidate the whole model whenever an expert is added, and can erode capabilities outside the target domains. We propose Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD), which keeps the pre-RL model frozen, distills each RL expert independently into a soft prompt, and composes experts by concatenating their prompts without joint training. A single soft prompt suffices to distill an RL expert, recovering at least 90% of the teacher's gain in 22 of 23 math, code, and agent settings. Independently trained prompts compose well. In a multi-expert setting, the average rises with every expert added, and the full composition matches MOPD (67.83 vs. 67.79). The composition generalizes best across six held-out benchmarks. Input-space adaptation adds little serving overhead, keeping throughput within 1.5% of the unadapted backbone and reaching 2.40x that of rank-4 LoRA. These results point toward continual post-training on a fixed backbone.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30986)**  `arXiv:2611.30986`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yihong Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the trainable policy velocity onto rollouts from a behavior distribution. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This behavior--policy mismatch breaks baseline invariance and injects baseline-induced gradient drift, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching (iTDM), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance across group-wise online/offline RL and pairwise offline RL. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly 3\times faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to 0.87 without collapse. Furthermore, iTDM scales effectively to the 5.6B FLUX.3 model, achieving better performance across both in-domain and unseen metrics. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[LatentLens: Rethinking Latent Interface in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31582)**  `arXiv:2611.31582`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ziqing Yang, Kewei Cheng, Mingjie Li, Yang Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent interfaces let agents in a multi-agent system communicate through continuous representations rather than decoded text. Prior work reports performance and efficiency gains, but neither separates latent communication from the agent harness nor examines its internal components, leaving its actual contribution unclear. We propose LatentLens, a framework that decouples the latent interface at two levels: externally from the agent harness, to measure its contribution to overall performance (RQ1), and internally into realignment compatibility, information accessibility, and propagation dynamics, to identify the benefits and limitations of each (RQ2). Across seven benchmarks with Qwen3 models, adding the latent interface to the same harness does not improve and can even substantially degrade accuracy. Internally, latent representations retain some domain information, but incompatible realignment can substantially degrade performance, and repeated latent steps can amplify representation-level failures. Thus, while latent representations may preserve information, they do not necessarily ensure effective communication, which also requires compatible realignment, information accessibility, and stable propagation.
  </details>

- **[Speculative Self-Distillation enables Efficient Knowledge Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12016)**  `arXiv:2611.12016`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shayan Talaei, Agam Bhatia, Arshia Soltani Moakhar, Jonas Hübotter, Amin Saberi, Azalia Mirhoseini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many real-world applications require language models to internalize knowledge that was absent from pretraining, to incorporate continually updated facts, or to learn new domain skills. Self-distillation has emerged as a natural approach for this task. A model conditioned on the source material acts as the teacher, and an unconditioned student is trained to reproduce the teacher's behavior closed-book. We propose Speculative Self-Distillation (SSD), an efficient per-token mixed-policy method for self-distillation. The student generates each rollout by default, while the teacher intervenes only at positions where its next-token distribution substantially diverges from the student's. Unlike off-policy distillation with teacher-generated prefixes, this design keeps training rollouts close to the student's test-time behavior, while avoiding the low-signal states that fully on-policy rollouts drift into. In effect, SSD lets the teacher steer the rollout exactly where the privileged context changes the prediction. On three closed-book QA benchmarks measuring fact recall, knowledge updates, and skill acquisition, SSD nearly matches on-policy distillation while using 38-69% fewer supervised tokens and consistently outperforms off-policy distillation on the held-out test set. On a forgetting stress test, SSD retains the most general capability while also achieving the best in-domain accuracy and using less supervision than the on-policy baseline. Overall, SSD establishes a new accuracy-efficiency-forgetting Pareto frontier for self-distillation.
  </details>

- **[MobileMoE: Scaling On-Device Mixture of Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27358)**  `arXiv:2605.27358`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yanbei Chen, Hanxian Huang, Ernie Chang, Jacob Szwejbka, Digant Desai, Zechun Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) has become the de facto architecture for hundred-billion-parameter language models, yet its advantages at sub-billion scales for on-device deployment remain largely unexplored. To close this gap, we present MobileMoE, a family of on-device MoE language models with sub-billion active parameters (0.3-0.9B active and 1.3-5.3B total) that establish a new Pareto frontier for on-device LLMs. We first formulate an on-device MoE scaling law that jointly optimizes MoE architecture under mobile memory and compute constraints, identifying an on-device sweet spot - moderate sparsity with fine-grained and shared experts - that is simultaneously memory and compute-optimal. Building on the derived architectures, we train MobileMoE with a four-stage recipe covering pre-training, mid-training, instruction fine-tuning, and quantization-aware training, all on open-source datasets. Across 14 benchmarks, MobileMoE matches or exceeds leading on-device dense LLMs with 2-4$\times$ fewer inference FLOPs, and matches or surpasses the state-of-the-art MoE OLMoE-1B-7B with up to 60% fewer parameters. To bridge the last mile to mobile deployment, we provide the first efficient MoE inference on commodity smartphones with comprehensive on-device profiling. At comparable INT4 weight memory, MobileMoE-S delivers $1.8$-$3.8\times$ faster prefill and $2.2$-$3.4\times$ faster decode than the dense baseline MobileLLM-Pro.
  </details>

- **[LEGOSpec: A Composable and Efficient Framework for Speculative Draft Design and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33004)**  `arXiv:2611.33004`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hanzhen Wang, Jiaming Xu, Guohao Dai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) generation using a lightweight draft. Existing methods evaluate complete recipes under different training configurations, leaving component effects, interactions, and composition benefits unclear. Therefore, we present LEGOSpec, a composable framework for speculative draft design and training. LEGOSpec decomposes draft models into Context, Backbone, Proposal, and Objective components that share a unified training infrastructure. Using LEGOSpec, we systematically vary components and evaluate selected combinations under matched training conditions to isolate their effects and interactions. These experiments reveal three findings: (1) Different workloads favor different backbones, depending on context length and memory budget, with within-block dependency modeling particularly important for long contexts. (2) Proposal design must balance acceptance and latency. (3) The training objective should match token distributions locally and optimize acceptance length across the block. These findings guide workload-aware configurations and DFlux, a balanced general-purpose draft combining a convolution-equipped SWA--GQA hybrid backbone, Residual Corrector, and hierarchical supervision. Across Qwen and Gemma models, DFlux improves acceptance length over the strongest baseline by up to 13.7% on regular benchmarks and 34.7% on long-context workloads. To support efficient training, LEGOSpec integrates metadata-aware scheduling and asynchronous execution, increasing training throughput by up to 79% and accelerating offline supervision preparation by up to $3.11\times$ over SpecForge.
  </details>

- **[RetraQ: Manifold-Constrained Critic Guidance for Flow Policies in RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32148)**  `arXiv:2611.32148`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xuanzhuo Liu, Wu Chen, Ruixiang Wu, Shuai Yuan, Chuankang Li, Tongxin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A learned critic can improve a pretrained flow-matching policy by steering sampled actions toward higher predicted value. However, even a critic that fits the behavior data well can provide misleading action gradients. Gradient components pointing away from the data-supported region are weakly constrained by behavior data and can lead guidance to exploit critic extrapolation errors. We introduce RetraQ (Retraction-based Q-guidance), which uses geometry encoded by the pretrained flow to constrain both the direction and the destination of critic guidance. A single velocity evaluation of the frozen flow defines a denoising map. RetraQ filters the critic gradient with the map's Jacobian and applies the map itself to correct the updated action. Our analysis characterizes when these operations approximate tangent filtering and retraction onto the behavior manifold. The same update guides actions at test time and provides targets for policy distillation and online reinforcement learning. In controlled studies, RetraQ improves true return while keeping refined actions close to the data-supported region, and its distillation targets remain more stable than those of raw critic ascent. In offline-to-online training on LIBERO and OGBench, RetraQ achieves higher mean final success than the evaluated critic-guidance baselines. On a real-world plug-insertion task, test-time RetraQ improves the success rate of a behavior-cloned $\pi_{0.5}$ policy from 55.0% to 76.7%. Online training further increases success to 98.3% in only 30 minutes and 100% within one hour. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ADE: A Zero-Training Unlearning Method for Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31998)**  `arXiv:2611.31998`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sirui He, Chi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models learn compact recurrent dynamics from offline trajectories, yet deployed simulators can retain backdoor triggers or stale transitions from outdated physical regimes. Existing unlearning methods fail in autoregressive physics: gradient updates distort latent manifolds and compound rollout drift, naive output zeroing suppresses valid control responses on trigger onset, and single-step deletion leaves corruption inside recurrent hidden states. We propose Analytical Dynamics Excision (ADE), a deterministic, zero-training closed-form unlearning algorithm for latent world models. Using only cached pretraining moments, ADE recovers retain covariances via single-pass matrix downdating without raw retain data and cascades closed-form edits across the input bottleneck, hidden states, and downstream heads. Pairing orthogonal excision with whitened recurrent damping and mean-preserving affine calibration neutralizes out-of-manifold backdoors and realigns in-manifold shifts without variance collapse. Across four regimes on three MuJoCo benchmarks, ADE outperforms seven tuned baselines on DreamerV1, eliminating HalfCheetah and Hopper velocity-flip triggers $4.4\times$ faster than fine-tuning, and transfers zero-shot to DreamerV3.
  </details>

- **[Two-Stage Post-Training for Flow-Based Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31850)**  `arXiv:2611.31850`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaming Zhang, Yao Lyu, Haoning Wang, Yongjun Chen, Liping Zhang, Shengbo Eben Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale pretraining provides embodied models with general capabilities, but reliable task execution still requires post training. In multitask settings, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) faces distribution mismatch between training and deployment, whereas reinforcement learning (RL) is limited by sparse rewards and uneven learning progress across tasks. Multi-teacher on policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates expert capabilities through dense supervision on the model's own trajectories, but achieving performance beyond the teachers remains challenging. We propose a two-stage post training framework for flow-based embodied models, constructing distillation objectives from the velocity regression formulation of conditional flow matching. Stage 1 efficiently consolidates expert capabilities through MOPD. Stage 2 combines RL with teacher distillation that weights velocity errors according to the sampling noise, where distillation is restricted to failed trajectories to supplement sparse rewards with dense guidance without directly constraining successful behaviors. Experiments across multiple models and benchmarks show that the framework outperforms joint SFT and exceeds the corresponding teachers' aggregate success rates. Sample efficiency comparisons show higher success rates than direct RL with fewer environment interactions.
  </details>

- **[Conformalized Tree-Based Gradient Ensembles for Out-of-Distribution Detection and Plausible Recourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31319)**  `arXiv:2611.31319`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mariam Zakaria Moussa Ali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual recourse provides actionable feature adjustments that reverse unfavourable predictions made by automated decision models. However, when recourse search optimizes purely against classifier confidence or narrow proxy scores over gradient-boosted decision trees (GBDT), search procedures can exploit boundary artifacts and low-density regions---an instance of Goodhart's law---yielding out-of-distribution (OOD) recommendations that appear favourable nominally while lacking manifold plausibility. In this paper, we study conformalized OOD gating coupled with actionability-constrained counterfactual search across two tabular benchmarks: Adult and Give Me Some Credit (GMSC). Calibrating finite-sample anomaly gates (gate_IF, gate_LOF, and the conjunctive gate_ALL) at significance level delta = 0.05 over isolation forest, local outlier factor, and k-nearest-neighbor signals, we audit generated counterfactuals against held-out GMM and OCSVM detectors alongside ablation studies (gen10, gen100, and relax). Across matched recourse pairs where both constrained and unconstrained searches succeed, gate_ALL significantly reduces held-out OCSVM flags on Adult (N=541, Delta flag_OCSVM = -0.0296, p_Holm = 4.0e-4) and lowers both held-out flags on GMSC (N=540, Delta flag_OCSVM = -0.0346, p = 1.0751e-4; Delta flag_GMM = -0.1663, p = 3.5679e-16) while increasing matched recourse cost (Delta cost = +0.3035, p = 1.3365e-5) and narrowing overall feasibility. Our findings formalize the cost--feasibility--plausibility trade-off and demonstrate how conformal OOD gating audits recourse reliability on tabular ensembles.
  </details>

- **[Actionwave: A Systematic Benchmark for Waveform-Centric Clinical Prediction Across Care Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31249)**  `arXiv:2611.31249`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Peng, Davis Hobley, Song Wang, Zachary PlotkinB, Tianlong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous bedside waveforms record rapid physiological changes that can inform clinical decisions. Existing benchmarks examine selected prediction conditions, but it remains unclear how model advantages depend on signal duration, warning time, and available inputs across care settings. We introduce Actionwave, a benchmark for waveform-centric clinical prediction across intensive-care, operating-room, and emergency-department settings. Actionwave uses a common decision-time prediction interface and groups clinically grounded tasks by what is inferred and when. It varies conditions relevant to clinical use, including the warning required before an intervention. We compare diverse architectures and pretrained biosignal encoders under specified model-size and compute budgets. The protocol requires patient-level splits, strict information cutoffs, and comparisons that isolate individual modeling factors. Across 20 tasks and 1.4M task-labelled windows from source datasets, the experimental grids account for over 87,000 model fits. These comparisons identify task-specific choices and show why matched cohorts, unimodal controls, and explicit timing are necessary when interpreting waveform model performance.
  </details>

- **[RLVR$^{2}$: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rubric-based Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23457)**  `arXiv:2609.23457`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hao Li, Zhengkun Zhang, Gangqiang Hu, Zhen Zhang, Yude Gao, Dai Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is expanding from tasks with well-defined correctness signals, such as mathematics and code, toward multifaceted quality requirements specified by multi-dimensional rubrics. Since policy optimization consumes one scalar per rollout, rubric-based pipelines must map multiple criterion scores into a scalar reward. This aggregation is often treated as score scaling, but it implicitly determines how quality dimensions trade off during training. The prevailing practice, normalizing each criterion and taking a linear combination, assumes that cardinal score differences are comparable across criteria and that gains on one criterion compensate for failures on another; both assumptions are unreliable when criteria are semantically heterogeneous. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rubric-based Ranking (RLVR$^2$), a verifiable ranking paradigm for rubric-based RLVR. For each criterion, RLVR$^2$ converts rubric scores into criterion-specific within-group ordinal outcomes, recovers a latent utility from the resulting comparison matrix, and merges these utilities into one training signal. By retaining only within-group ordering and discarding raw score magnitudes, RLVR$^2$ avoids calibrating heterogeneous rubric scales. It further supports objective-preserving attribute adjustment: auxiliary attributes that correlate with observed rankings but are not training objectives can enter the estimation without expanding the rubric or rewarding them directly. Across three model scales and 16 benchmarks, RLVR$^2$ consistently outperforms representative rubric-based baselines, achieving the best overall performance on most benchmarks at every scale. Analysis shows it controls systematic effects tied to reasoning efficiency and response formatting while preserving the quality objective.
  </details>

- **[From Synthetic Priors to Model Behavior: Structural Coverage in Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06912)**  `arXiv:2609.06912`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _He Zhao, Ryan Thompson, Daniel M. Steinberg, Ashfaqur Rahman, Edwin V. Bonilla, Cheng Soon Ong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are commonly pretrained on large collections of procedurally generated synthetic tasks, yet it remains unclear how well these synthetic pretraining priors support the downstream tasks on which the models are evaluated. We study this from a distribution-level attribution perspective. We recover or reconstruct the synthetic data generators of four TFMs and compare their generated tasks with datasets from two widely used tabular benchmarks. Each dataset is represented by a common set of structural descriptors capturing schema, feature distributions, dependence structure, response properties, and feature--response relationships. We then measure how broadly and repeatedly each synthetic prior reaches benchmark tasks using structural coverage and normalized density, and examine whether stronger local support is associated with better predictive performance. We find substantial differences across synthetic pretraining priors: some generators provide broader and denser support for benchmark tasks than others. Moreover, stronger synthetic-to-benchmark support is generally associated with better relative model performance, although this association is largely driven by the model with the broadest support and mostly disappears once each model's average support is removed. These results suggest that structural coverage provides a useful diagnostic for characterizing synthetic pretraining priors, whereas its relationship to downstream model behavior mainly reflects differences between models and remains confounded with architecture and training.
  </details>

- **[VI-CuRL: Stabilizing Verifier-Independent Reinforcement Learning Reasoning via Confidence-Guided Variance Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12579)**  `arXiv:2602.12579`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xin-Qiang Cai, Masashi Sugiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a dominant paradigm for enhancing Large Language Models (LLMs) reasoning, yet its reliance on external verifiers limits its scalability. Recent findings suggest that RLVR primarily functions by eliciting latent capabilities, motivating the development of verifier-free algorithms. However, in such settings, standard methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization face a critical challenge: destructive gradient variance that often leads to training collapse. To address this issue, we introduce Verifier-Independent Curriculum Reinforcement Learning (VI-CuRL), a framework that leverages the model's intrinsic confidence to construct a curriculum independent from external verifiers. By prioritizing high-confidence samples, VI-CuRL effectively manages the bias-variance trade-off, specifically targeting the reduction of action and problem variance. We provide a rigorous theoretical analysis, proving that our estimator guarantees asymptotic unbiasedness. Empirically, VI-CuRL promotes stability and consistently outperforms verifier-dependent/independent baselines across math and general reasoning benchmarks with/without verifiers.
  </details>

- **[One-Shot Clustering for Federated Learning Under Clustering-Agnostic Assumption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.01587)**  `arXiv:2509.01587`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Maciej Krzysztof Zuziak, Roberto Pellungrini, Salvatore Rinzivillo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning (FL) is a widespread and well-adopted paradigm of decentralised learning that allows training one model from multiple sources without the need to transfer data between participating clients directly. Since its inception in 2016, it has been divided into numerous subfields that deal with application-specific issues, such as data heterogeneity or resource allocation. One such sub-field, Clustered Federated Learning (CFL), is dealing with the problem of clustering the population of clients into separate cohorts to deliver personalised models. Although a few remarkable works have been published in this domain, the problem remains largely unexplored, as its basic assumptions and settings differ slightly from those of standard FL. In this work, we present One-Shot Clustered Federated Learning (OCFL), a clustering-agnostic algorithm that can automatically detect the earliest suitable moment for clustering. We empirically evaluate our methodology by testing various one-shot clustering algorithms for over forty different tasks on five benchmark datasets. Our experiments showcase the good performance of our approach when used to perform CFL in an automated manner without the need to adjust hyperparameters. We also revisit the practical feasibility of CFL algorithms based on the gradients or weights of the clients, providing firm evidence of the high efficiency of density-based clustering methods when used to differentiate between the loss surfaces of neural networks trained on different distributions. Moreover, by inspecting the feasibility of local explanations generated with the help of GradCAM, we can provide more insights into the relationship between personalisation and the explainability of local predictions.
  </details>

- **[File Size as a Potential Dataset Bias in Audio-Visual Deepfake Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32979)**  `arXiv:2611.32979`  `cs.LG`  
  _Marcella Astrid, Niki Maria Foteinopoulou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deepfake detection benchmarks are commonly designed to evaluate whether models can distinguish authentic from manipulated audiovisual content. However, benchmark construction pipelines may introduce unintended associations between class labels and simple file-level properties. In this work, we identify a potential file-size bias in two widely used audio-visual deepfake datasets. In the 1M-Deepfakes Detection Challenge dataset, a score derived from file size alone achieves 70.13% AUC on the validation set. When file size is considered together with video duration, simple file-level methods exceed 90% AUC on 1M and 85% AUC on FakeAVCeleb. Although the same pattern is not observed in DFDC, the results suggest that the observed association is dataset-dependent rather than an inherent property of deepfake videos. We discuss several possible explanations, including differences in encoding, compression, source data, manipulation procedures, and post-processing, although the present analysis does not establish the underlying cause. We further discuss whether the factors associated with file size may also leave traces in decoded video or audio, and consequently whether they could influence content-based deepfake detection, as an important direction for future work. Our findings highlight the importance of examining simple file-level properties when constructing and evaluating deepfake datasets and benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Eliciting the Recall Capacity of Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32969)**  `arXiv:2611.32969`  `cs.LG`  
  _Johan Lindqvist, Antonio Orvieto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear attention models exhibit a disproportionate deficit relative to softmax attention in associative recall (AR) in language modeling. We investigate this gap through natural language evaluations, synthetic recall tasks, and targeted training interventions. Using a synthetic setting, we quantify the role of state-size in how many items can be recalled from memory and find a similar empirical scaling across the tested linear-attention architectures. Testing this relationship reveals that even a single layer at scale can learn to retrieve thousands of associations across long distances. Yet, standard language model training does not reliably teach a linear model to copy from context, whereas a comparable softmax model learns to do so. Considering this, we strengthen recall supervision during training through in-context sentence repetition and context-sensitive loss reweighting. Both methods improve recall-intensive benchmark performance and narrow the long-distance AR gap to the softmax baseline without changing the architecture, token budget, or number of optimization steps, at a slight cost to general language modeling. These targeted changes to training can thus elicit substantial but underutilized retrieval capacity in linear-attention models. These findings suggest that, although linear-attention architectures have substantial capacity for recall, their inductive biases and the demands of language model training make it difficult to develop this capability without dedicated incentives.
  </details>

- **[EPRO: Robust Policy Optimization Through an Imperfect Execution-Policy Predictor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32810)**  `arXiv:2611.32810`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yibo Wang, Changyi Yang, Yuanxin Liu, Zhaoran Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RL for LLMs often separates rollout generation from policy training. Although the two numerical engines share model parameters, they can induce different policies. This creates a challenge beyond correcting a known training-inference mismatch: during learner updates, the execution policy corresponding to the current parameters cannot be directly observed. We view this as optimizing an unobserved execution policy through an imperfect predictor of its evolution. This perspective raises two fundamental questions: how can we estimate the execution policy's response to learner updates, and how much should we trust that estimate when optimizing the actual execution policy? We introduce Execution-Policy Robust Optimization (EPRO) to address these challenges. Starting from the observed rollout-time execution policy, we use training-policy changes to construct a differentiable predictor of the current execution policy, whose score yields Transported Score Centering (TSC). Our robust-improvement analysis reveals how prediction error limits the magnitude of reliably justified updates. For practical implementation, we introduce Policy Shift as an observable proxy for prediction risk and use Shift-Aware Soft Clipping (SSC) to attenuate risky updates. Across four Qwen3 model settings, EPRO achieves the highest average held-out accuracy among the compared methods on seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[HiCal: Offline Calibration for Hierarchical Scale Encoding in Accurate HiF4 Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32624)**  `arXiv:2611.32624`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiyuan Zhang, Yanzhao Li, Hui Dong, Sifan Cheng, Mingen Zheng, Hui Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block floating-point (BFP) quantization is widely adopted for efficient low-bit large language model (LLM) inference, where the choice of block scales plays a critical role in quantization accuracy. HiFloat4 (HiF4) employs a hierarchical scale encoding scheme consisting of a global E6M2 scale and two levels of binary scale extensions. However, its conventional rounding rule and fixed scale extension thresholds may not fully capture the statistical characteristics of the quantized tensors in practice, leading to suboptimal scale representations. In this work, we propose HiCal, an offline calibration approach that improves HiF4 quantization accuracy by refining the encoding decisions across all three scale levels. Specifically, we introduce a calibrated rounding bias for the Level-1 E6M2 scale and optimize the scale-extension thresholds at Levels 2 and 3 based on the empirical distributions of local group magnitudes. The resulting calibration parameters are determined offline and remain fixed during inference, while the hierarchical encoding continues to adapt to individual blocks through their local values. This design eliminates the need for per-block multi-candidate scale search while preserving the original deterministic HiF4 encoding procedure. Experiments across five synthetic and real activation distributions show that the proposed method reduces quantization MSE by 9.91%-23.50% relative to the original HiF4 scheme, including a 13.00% reduction on real Qwen3-8B activations. End-to-end evaluations across six benchmarks further show average-score improvements of 0.91 and 1.25 percentage points on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-30B-A3B, respectively, without per-block multi-candidate search during inference.
  </details>

- **[ST-GNN-AAAS: Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks for Anytime Automatic Algorithm Selection in the Traveling Salesman Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32602)**  `arXiv:2611.32602`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zeyu Zhang, Mustafa Misir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anytime Automatic Algorithm Selection (AAAS) aims to make solver choices under varying time budgets. The existing AAAS approaches largely rely on static instance characteristics and make limited use of temporal information revealed during solver execution. We propose ST-GNN-AAAS, a Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Network framework for AAAS in the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP). The framework represents evolving incumbent tours using time-varying node features, including distances from each node to its neighbors in the current tour, and combines these features with solver performance observations over a short history window. Given this short history, ST-GNN-AAAS predicts solver selections over a longer future horizon. We evaluate the method on a subset of diverse TSP benchmarks from NATIONAL, TNM, TSPLIB, and VLSI, releasing the corresponding solver execution data to support reproducibility. Our model achieves a selection accuracy of 76.59% and closes 46.05% of the average rank gap between the single best solver (SBS) and the virtual best solver (VBS).
  </details>

- **[On the Effectiveness of Resampling for Supervised Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32598)**  `arXiv:2611.32598`  `cs.LG`  
  _Cyril Perosino, Rino Di Chiara, Hamida Seba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Class imbalance and scarce anomaly labels motivate oversampling in supervised graph anomaly detection (GAD), but its benefits across detectors and benchmarks remain unclear. In the low-label setting of GADBench, with 20 labelled anomalies and 80 labelled normal nodes, we compare class weighting and up to six oversampling strategies in ten detector configurations from three families: graph neural networks (GNNs), tree ensembles on neighbourhood-aggregated features, and tabular foundation models, on 17 datasets. On the eight datasets with organic anomalies, no strategy has a statistically resolved average gain in any configuration, and which nodes are labelled matters as much as the strategy. On the nine datasets with injected anomalies, Gaussian-noise oversampling (ROSE) gains up to 5.2 AUPRC points in four of the six tree and tabular foundation model configurations, and in all six only on attribute-swap anomalies; in four GNNs its gain is not distinguishable from zero. This gain depends on the injection recipe, which gives swapped nodes an atypical feature norm: it is no longer distinguishable from zero when the swap uses a single random donor or preserves the norm, although most detectors then also stop detecting the swaps. Over the 17 datasets, tuning the tree detectors adds about three points, as much as ROSE, and cuts ROSE's gain by about half or more. With the same source nodes and class weight, sampled graph views are not distinguishable from plain copies of those nodes. We recommend tuning baselines, repeating labelled splits, reporting organic and injected anomalies separately, and checking label-free degree and feature-norm baselines.
  </details>

- **[Representation and Model Contributions on Small Molecular Benchmarks: Controlled Comparisons and External Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32568)**  `arXiv:2611.32568`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nora A. M. A. Talkhan, Ibrahim Abdelbaky, Fatma A. Omara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improvements in molecular property prediction are usually attributed to the model, although part of the gain may come from the molecular representation. New models are commonly benchmarked against a random forest (RF) trained on a molecular fingerprint, a comparison that changes the model and the representation at the same time. We separate the two contributions with a two-by-two factorial design in which the RF and a second model (CatBoost or the tabular foundation models TabPFN and TabFM) are each trained on a fingerprint and on RDKit descriptors, and each effect is tested with a paired scaffold bootstrap. Seven small MoleculeNet datasets (3 regression, 4 classification) are evaluated on the published split of a recent graph transformer (DGT), on 10 additional scaffold splits and on external datasets. For solubility and lipophilicity, the representation effect exceeds the model effect in every main factorial design, in all three evaluations. For hydration free energy, the result depends on the starting fingerprint: from ECFP4 the representation effect is larger, and from MACCS neither effect is significantly larger. On the classification datasets, only one main factorial design has a significant difference between the two effects. Although the model effect is smaller on solubility and lipophilicity, TabFM outperforms the RF on all 7 datasets when both use RDKit descriptors. With RDKit descriptors in place of a fingerprint, the RF baseline reported for DGT surpasses the transformer's published solubility score. We recommend that model comparisons evaluate classical baselines on more than one representation and report the representation and model effects separately.
  </details>

- **[InducedTFMs: A Family of Sub-Quadratic Tabular Foundation Models Using Induced Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32504)**  `arXiv:2611.32504`  `cs.LG`  
  _Salome Gobbi, Nicolas Atienza, Nicola Cancedda, Michael Arbel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) have emerged as a serious competitor to gradient-boosted trees, predicting on a new table in a single forward pass by in-context learning. However, their attention is quadratic in the number of samples and features, which caps pretraining at small synthetic tables and forces inference on large ones to rely on chunking or subsampling. Row-compression models reduce the feature-wise cost, but only by discarding the cell-level representation, and remain quadratic in the number of samples. We introduce InducedTFM, which replaces both attention stages of a bidimensional TFM with the induced attention of the Set Transformer, preserving cell-level representations while making its cost linear in both samples and features. Under a training budget matched to a full-attention TabICLv2 reimplementation, InducedTFM matches its accuracy on TabArena and on a benchmark of larger datasets, and clearly outperforms a kernel linear-attention baseline of the same asymptotic cost. It overtakes TabICLv2 in inference time on narrow tables, reaching a 30x speed-up at 640K rows, although row compression remains preferable on wide tables.
  </details>

- **[When Knowledge-Tracing Rankings Depend on Available History: A Source-Correction and Matched-Information Audit on ASSISTments 2009 and 2015](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32489)**  `arXiv:2611.32489`  `cs.LG`  
  _Devis Wawan Saputra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge-tracing benchmarks often compare models that have different access to a learner's previous responses. We examine how data provenance, history length, and representation affect those comparisons. First, we compare a pinned ASSISTments 2009 sequence mirror against the corrected official release. Of 4,148 learners represented in both, 817 have different ordered skill-response sequences; the mirror also contains fewer labeled events. Second, we retain earlier learner-disjoint model evaluations on the 2009 and 2015 datasets, distinguishing exploratory results from a corrective 2015 replication. Finally, we evaluate a new, nonoverlapping sample of 4,000 ASSISTments 2015 learners using three models constrained to the same one-event or 20-event prior-response window. On 20,592 test responses from 582 learners, the 20-event Brier scores were 0.17667 for count-based logistic regression, 0.17389 for an order-aware logistic model, and 0.17514 for a GRU. The GRU-minus-order-aware difference was 0.00125 (95% learner-cluster bootstrap interval [0.00008, 0.00243]); at one prior event, the corresponding difference was 0.00212 [0.00120, 0.00301]. Earlier comparisons had favored the GRU against simpler history summaries. The newer analysis instead favors a logistic comparator that represents order using the same permitted event stream. These results concern predictive performance on particular historical cohorts and model settings. They neither isolate an architectural effect nor establish generalization to contemporary classrooms.
  </details>

- **[The Teacher Is Already Inside: Self-Distilling Looped Language Models Beyond Their Last Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32452)**  `arXiv:2611.32452`  `cs.LG`  
  _Linghua Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models apply one shared transformer stack several times per token, and every loop is readable through the shared language-model head. Post-training them is hard. Reward-only reinforcement learning needs a verifier and learns from one sparse signal per response; spreading the reward over loops collapses the entropy of every loop; and cross-loop self-distillation, as in LoopOPD, can only teach intermediate loops, because the last loop cannot learn from its own frozen copy: the loss is zero at initialisation. We observe that a looped model already contains a policy that decodes better than its last loop: the next-token distribution extrapolated from the first loop through the last. ELPSD (Extrapolated-Loop Policy Self-Distillation) turns this distribution into a self-distillation target: a frozen copy of the model computes it, and the model's own last loop is distilled towards it on its own rollouts, using the exact reverse KL at every visited prefix. Teacher and student are the same model, so ELPSD needs no reward, no external teacher and no privileged context, and it leaves rollouts and decoding unchanged. We show that the objective pulls the last loop towards the base model's last loop with weight 1+w and away from the base model's first loop with weight w, and that the teacher must stay frozen, since an idealised teacher recomputed from the current model extrapolates without bound. We evaluate ELPSD on Ouro-1.4B-Thinking across five math benchmarks against the base model, GRPO, RLTT and OPSD. Without any reward, ELPSD raises the average avg@8 from 61.15 to 63.69 and is the best method on all five benchmarks, ahead of GRPO (62.32), which uses a verifier, and the self-distillation method OPSD (61.77), which needs reference solutions.
  </details>

- **[Optimal Transport Meets t-SNE: Integrated Visualization without Batch Effects in OT-tSNE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32402)**  `arXiv:2611.32402`  `cs.LG`  
  _Azhar Akhmetova, Edward Eichhorn, Boris Landa, Dmitry Kobak, Sebastian Damrich_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual exploration is an integral step in understanding the structure of datasets. However, systematic variation between datasets collected under different conditions, known as batch effects, can obscure shared structure and complicate their joint visualization. Existing methods often rely on cross-batch nearest-neighbor relations to integrate the batches. Yet, these local correspondences can be unreliable for challenging cross-batch geometries. We propose optimal-transport t-SNE (OT-tSNE), which replaces nearest-neighbor-based matching with globally coupled affinities derived from entropic optimal transport. Controlled synthetic experiments demonstrated that nearest-neighbor-based methods can deteriorate, while optimal-transport affinities and OT-tSNE embeddings remain robust. Across seven real single-cell datasets, we benchmarked OT-tSNE against a broad range of integration and visualization methods and found that it achieves a Pareto-efficient trade-off between batch mixing and biological conservation, and integrates batches particularly well.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Reasoning Effort Training in Large Language Models: On-Policy Distillation or Reinforcement Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32131)**  `arXiv:2611.32131`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianliang Lv, Hao Li, Xinchao Xu, Zhengkun Zhang, Ju Huo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have shown strong capabilities on complex tasks, yet how to control their reasoning effort remains a significant challenge for practical deployment. This paper provides a systematic investigation of reasoning-effort training, contrasting reinforcement learning (RL) with on-policy distillation (OPD). We find that compressing reasoning and separating effort tiers are governed by different signals. Length-penalized RL reliably compresses a single policy, reducing thinking on AIME25 by 70\% without accuracy loss, but collapses the tiers when they are trained jointly. Multi-teacher OPD separates them: across 33 benchmarks, low and medium use 58\% and 43\% fewer thinking tokens than high, while high improves on the reference policy by 3.1 points. We trace this difference to the training signal. A sequence-level reward cannot tell tier-specific behavior from shared behavior, whereas OPD supplies a tier-specific target at every visited state, including the decision to stop. Building on this analysis, we show that a single token-level KL anchor on the high tier is sufficient to learn separated tiers within one RL stage, and that natural-language effort instructions make this separation emerge from the start of training, yielding a policy competitive with multi-teacher OPD at matched thinking length. Finally, the separation built by dense targets holds only on the states seen in training: it does not carry over to unseen agentic tasks, where effort acts mainly through the number of interaction turns, whereas the compression induced by sequence-level rewards does.
  </details>

- **[Scale-Robust and Global-Aware Representation Learning for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32033)**  `arXiv:2611.32033`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gyuwon Lee, Saejoon Park, Won-Seok Hwang, Bogoan Kim, Yunyong Ko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTSAD) is an essential task for identifying abnormal system behaviors and supporting reliable operations across diverse real-world applications. Despite substantial advances in MTSAD, two key challenges in representation learning remain underexplored: (C1) how to learn scale-robust temporal representations that capture anomalies across different temporal scales and (C2) how to learn global-aware relational representations that capture inter-series dependencies by incorporating series-level global characteristics beyond local windows. To address these challenges, we propose STAGER, a novel method that jointly learns two complementary representations: scale-robust temporal and global-aware relational representations. Specifically, STAGER employs (1) a scale-robust temporal encoder that constructs a multi-scale temporal directed acyclic graph (DAG) to learn scale-robust temporal representations and (2) a global-aware relational encoder that incorporates learnable series-level global embeddings into the temporal representations and models inter-series relationships through graph neural networks. Two complementary auxiliary objectives further enhance the distinctiveness of the global embeddings. Extensive experiments on three real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that STAGER achieves the best average rank among ten evaluated MTSAD methods across three evaluation metrics. Ablation and sensitivity analyses validate the effectiveness of the proposed components and auxiliary objectives, while efficiency evaluations demonstrate that STAGER achieves a favorable trade-off between detection performance and inference time compared with existing methods.
  </details>

- **[Predict Backbone, Graft Dynamics: A Decoupled Paradigm for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31970)**  `arXiv:2611.31970`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lin Zhang, Zilin Li, Jingmei Zhao, Jiayang Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern long-term forecasters are typically optimized for point-wise accuracy, which rewards dominant low-frequency backbones but often suppresses high-utility local dynamics such as abrupt variations, transient surges, and turning points. We propose Predict Backbone, Graft Dynamics, a decoupled forecasting paradigm that separates stable structural prediction from grounded dynamic restoration. We instantiate it as GRAFT, where a base forecaster predicts the reliable trajectory backbone, and a dedicated grafting path restores historically recurrent dynamics through retrieval-based evidence, phase-aware release, channel-wise control, and restoration-cooperative optimization. Unlike generic post-hoc revision methods that correct residual errors, GRAFT formulates the second stage as grounded dynamic grafting, where historical response evidence is calibrated into transferable dynamics and selectively grafted onto the backbone under phase-, channel-, and confidence-aware constraints. Across the full evaluation grid of seven benchmarks, two metrics, four horizons, and six backbones, GRAFT improves over the corresponding backbone in 87% of all comparisons, with average MSE/MAE reductions of 4.6%/3.9%. Dynamic-window analyses show that GRAFT restores local dynamics while preserving the stable backbone structure. These results suggest a new principle for high-utility forecasting: predict the backbone, then graft the dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Sounding the Black Box: Online Training of Embedded Neural Networks in Forward-Only Physical Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31964)**  `arXiv:2611.31964`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruiqi Shu, Tianrun Gao, Haomin Wen, Xiaomeng Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid physics-neural simulators embed neural networks in partial differential equation (PDE) solvers to model unresolved subgrid dynamics while preserving resolved physics and conservation laws. Because closure outputs feed back across time steps, single-step offline regression does not directly minimize multi-step rollout error, motivating online trajectory optimization. Solver backpropagation through time (BPTT) is the standard online solution when solver derivatives are available, yet many operational Fortran and C/C++ simulators expose only non-differentiable, forward-only interfaces. We introduce FORGE (Forward-Only Response and Gauss-Newton Estimation), an online framework that trains embedded networks from forward runs alone by decoupling exact network derivatives from black-box simulator dynamics. Stage I warms up the rollout via a low-dimensional Gauss-Newton step along output-orthonormal weight directions, canceling multi-layer feature drift via a final-layer correction. Stage II perturbs spatial output fields to fit a gain-bounded causal linear response operator across rollout steps, solves for regularized target outputs, and updates all weights via network backpropagation. Across five 2D and 3D benchmarks spanning phase separation, reaction-diffusion, fluid and MHD turbulence, and ocean circulation, FORGE outperforms gradient-free, reinforcement-learning, and emulator-based baselines, cutting coarse-solver test mean squared error (MSE) by 50%-96% in 2D and 70% in 3D, outperforming the strongest forward-only baseline by 16%-69%, and recovering 88%-98% of Solver BPTT's gains. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Last Loop: Reinforcement Learning with a Teacher Extrapolated from the Model Itself](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31939)**  `arXiv:2611.31939`  `cs.LG`  
  _Linghua Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models apply one shared transformer stack several times per token, and every loop can be read out through the shared language-model head. Post-training them with verifiable rewards is hard: the outcome reward carries one bit per response, spreading it over loops trains intermediate loops on tokens sampled from the last loop, and distillation needs either a teacher that ignores the reward or an external, larger model. We observe that a looped model already contains a teacher stronger than its last loop: the next-token distribution extrapolated from the first loop through the last. ELGO (Extrapolated-Loop Group-relative Optimization) adds to GRPO a dense per-token distillation term towards this distribution. The teacher is computed from an anchor copy of the model, which stays frozen and is replaced by the current model once the student's KL to the teacher falls below a threshold, so the teacher improves with the student without chasing its own extrapolation. The outcome reward splits probability between correct and incorrect responses, and the teacher decides which tokens are preferred inside a response. Between refreshes the objective is KL-regularised RL anchored at the teacher, whose optimum tilts the teacher by the reward. On Ouro-1.4B-Thinking, ELGO raises the average avg@8 over five math benchmarks from 62.32 with GRPO to 64.20 and is the best compared method on every benchmark, while spreading the reward over loops falls below the base model.
  </details>

- **[OrangeTree: A Linear and Tree-based Time Series Forecasting Model Supporting Multiple Input and Output Lengths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31905)**  `arXiv:2611.31905`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yiqi Tang, Zhichen Lai, Yuxuan Yang, Dalin Zhang, Jinpeng Chen, Gang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting (TSF) models suffer from a critical rigidity: the inability to handle Multiple Input and Output Lengths (MIOL) within a single architecture. Current workarounds, such as padding or autoregression, often incur computational redundancy or suffer from error accumulation. We propose OrangeTree, a linear and tree-based architecture that decouples model parameters from sequence lengths. It employs a Segment Tree Encoder and an Inverse Tree Decoder to translate multi-length input history into multi-length output predictions within a unified framework. Crucially, a Range Weighter and a Feature Fuser bridge these components, dynamically selecting and fusing relevant ranges into optimal contexts for forecasting. Experiments on six benchmarks confirm OrangeTree achieves the SOTA performance. In MIOL settings, it matches the accuracy of length-fixed models without substantial accuracy degradation or a substantial increase in inference latency typical of heuristic methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Tokenization and Architecture Jointly Allocate Component Roles in Time Series Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31730)**  `arXiv:2611.31730`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mingyu Kim, Doguk Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series transformers have achieved strong forecasting performance, yet it remains unclear which of their components, attention or the feed-forward network (FFN), constructs temporal features, and how tokenization such as patching changes this. We propose a temporal probing framework that uses 15 token-level diagnostic tasks to measure feature construction as the gain in linear decodability of temporal properties from one sub-layer to the next (a matched MLP probe already saturates at the embedding). It targets models whose probed representations are read by a linear forecasting head. Applied to 5 supervised models trained on 8 long-term forecasting benchmarks, it finds that the constructing component is not fixed but allocated by the interaction of tokenization and architecture. Under patch tokenization (PatchTST), the FFN's gain exceeds attention's in probing on all 8 datasets, and its 8-dataset mean is 3.2x attention's. Without attention, the FFN alone reaches nearly the same total gain, both when attention is removed from a trained model and when PatchTST is trained without it, and the retrained model forecasts within a few percent of the full model. In probing, attention dominates under variate tokenization (iTransformer); in CATS, cross-attention is the only input path. In a controlled manipulation of PatchTST, single-scalar tokens (P=1) nearly eliminate the FFN's construction, while one more point per token (P=2) restores it. Our forecasting-side interventions (activation patching, knockout, and training without attention) establish component-level reliance and sufficiency. These results offer a representational reading of prior findings on the gains from patching, the competitiveness of self-attention-free models (TSMixer, PatchMLP), and the distinct role of cross-attention in CATS.
  </details>

- **[Curvature-Equilibrated Policy Optimization for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31680)**  `arXiv:2611.31680`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gang Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradient-based actor optimization is central to deep reinforcement learning. However, conventional first-order optimizers do not explicitly exploit the local curvature of neural actor losses. We propose EQUIP (Curvature-Equilibrated Gradients for Policy Search), a novel approach that seeks to balance local contraction and expansion of the actor parameter space by equilibrating positive and negative curvature. EQUIP constructs a trajectory-informed search subspace and uses the projected Hessian of actor loss to build a bounded local metric for curvature equilibration. First-order loss matching preserves the approximated first-order decrease in actor loss. Meanwhile, gradient mixing and curvature-based gradient selection adjust the gradient applied for actor optimization. We establish the geometric properties of curvature equilibration, derive bounds on gradient alignment and magnitude, and identify conditions for improved actor loss reduction and convergence toward stationarity under direct gradient updates. We integrate EQUIP into Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) without modifying its actor loss, critic learning, or replay mechanism. Experiments on 6 MuJoCo continuous control benchmarks show that the resulting EQUIP-SAC algorithm achieves higher mean sample efficiency than SAC and two second-order policy optimization baselines. Ablation studies further highlight the benefits of curvature equilibration and trajectory-informed geometry for policy training.
  </details>

- **[JevBench: Metamorphic Coherence Testing for Typed Probabilistic Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31533)**  `arXiv:2611.31533`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chen Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed probabilistic decision models return distributions over specified answers, such as yes or no, a set of options or levels on an ordered scale. We ask whether their probabilities remain coherent when questions about the same input are reworded, negated, combined, given different options or placed in different batches. JevBench tests this coherence without gold labels. Its 50 metamorphic relations each test one law of probability or choice across five dimensions: representation, batch independence, probability measure, logical order and choice-set conditioning. Each test changes the question or batch while keeping the input fixed. Every model receives the same requests, fixed in advance. JevBench-240 contains 12,000 tests across 240 synthetic cases in 12 decision domains. A fixed subset of 1,200 tests, JevBench-mini, closely reproduces the full-suite ranking (Spearman 0.983). Across 21 openly released models, coherence scores range from 56.5 to 83.9. Batch independence holds for most models. Additivity fails broadly, particularly for complements, where the probabilities of a proposition and its negation should sum to one. A uniform reference model ignores the input and scores 89.4 at chance accuracy, exceeding every evaluated model's coherence score. Among decoders, accuracy ranges from 82.4% to 89.2%, while coherence ranges from 64.2 to 83.9. These results show why both measures are needed to assess decision models. JevBench is available as an open-source Python package.
  </details>

- **[Is 4 Bits Enough for EEG Sleep Staging? A Subject-Level Benchmark of MXFP4, NVFP4 and FP8](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31436)**  `arXiv:2611.31436`  `cs.LG`  
  _Emese Batizi, Hossein Valavi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Staging sleep on a wearable device keeps the raw electroencephalogram (EEG) private and saves the energy needed to transmit it. It also requires small, energy-efficient neural networks. We benchmark 13 post-training quantization (PTQ) configurations, from FP16 to the block-scaled 4-bit formats MXFP4 and NVFP4, for five-stage sleep staging from single-channel Fpz-Cz EEG. We use Sleep-EDF-78 (78 subjects), subject-wise cross-validation, three training seeds, value-exact emulation, and per-subject paired statistics. All 8- and 16-bit formats stay within 0.45 percentage points (pp) of FP32 Macro-F1. With 4-bit weights and activations, an epoch-wise CNN loses 1.98 to 3.04 pp, and a temporal-context network loses at most 1.19 pp. A same-encoder ablation shows that temporal context explains little of this gap. At equal block size, NVFP4 outperforms MXFP4. The first layer, which quantizes the raw EEG, is the most sensitive layer in both networks. Keeping the first and last layers in FP8 brings both tested 4-bit formats within 0.48 pp of FP32. This recipe adds at most 2.1% weight storage, needs no retraining, and is equivalent to quantization-aware training (QAT) within an exploratory 1-pp margin. The 4-bit losses fall on the same subjects across architectures (Spearman correlation coefficient = 0.69 to 0.79), and they reduce N3 recall the most. On the nested Sleep-EDF-20 subset, NVFP4 with FP8 first and last layers again stays within 0.25 pp of FP32. Under MXFP4, however, the ranking of the architectures reverses, and FP8 first and last layers do not fully recover two of the three tested models. In our emulation, NVFP4 with FP8 first and last layers thus loses little accuracy without QAT in both cohorts.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Harmful Short Videos in Toxic Diet Culture: A Governance-Ready Evidence Framework with Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31354)**  `arXiv:2611.31354`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wenli Zhang, Shuang Geng, Jiaheng Xie, Zhonghan Xiang, Sudha Ram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Algorithmic content moderation is increasingly important for governing short-form video content, yet harmful-content detection research focuses primarily on prediction or limited explanations. This leaves a governance challenge underexplored: producing decision-linked evidence that supports the review and justification of moderation decisions. Using toxic diet culture as an empirical case, we conceptualize governance-ready evidence as computational traceability, evidence localization, policy grounding, and evidence inspectability. We develop the Sparse Autoencoder-based Policy-Aligned Contrastive Evidence (SPACE) framework in a pretrained SAE feature space derived from a large multimodal model. A global relevance gate and decomposable linear evidence head identify decision-linked evidence; segment supervision and within-video ranking localize this evidence; policy alignment maps it to platform guidelines; and Pareto-efficient selection yields a compact, task-specific evidence vocabulary. On 3,711 labeled TikTok videos, SPACE achieves the highest harmful-category Macro F1 among benchmarks across four families, at 0.8507. Its highest-scoring observation overlaps a ground-truth harmful span for 88.0% of harmful test videos, while its evidence representation retrieves the corresponding policy category for 87.0%. Stakeholder evaluations indicate that the SPACE evidence report is perceived as useful for understanding, reviewing, documenting, and responding to moderation decisions. The study contributes computational design science knowledge for embedding governance-ready evidence within predictive content-moderation systems.
  </details>

- **[Vasicek Graph Diffusion Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30605)**  `arXiv:2611.30605`  `cs.LG`  
  _Junyi Tang, Chang Liu, Kun Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based graph generation models progressively transform Gaussian noise into structured graphs through stochastic dynamics. We observe that the effectiveness of this transformation is fundamentally determined by the evolution of the diffusion trajectory. Existing graph diffusion models often suffer from excessive uncertainty during diffusion and delayed growth of the signal-to-noise ratio, causing useful structural information to emerge only in the later stages of generation. To address this issue, we propose the Vasicek Graph Diffusion (VGD), a graph diffusion process derived from the Vasicek dynamics. The key insight of VGD is data-anchored mean dynamics with a closed-form solution, which analytically establishes a continuous transition from Gaussian noise to the target graph distribution. Unlike conventional graph diffusion processes that rely predominantly on stochastic evolution, VGD introduces a data-anchored drift that continuously steers the latent state toward the target distribution throughout diffusion. As a consequence, VGD maintains a smoothly evolving uncertainty while rapidly increasing the signal-to-noise ratio throughout diffusion, allowing informative graph structures to emerge much earlier during generation. Extensive experiments on multiple graph generation benchmarks demonstrate that VGD outperforms existing diffusion-based graph generative models in terms of generation quality and structural fidelity.
  </details>

- **[LoopPrune: Recurrent Pruning for Looped Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22757)**  `arXiv:2611.22757`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Huaijia Kou, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped diffusion Transformers reuse shared blocks to deepen computation and match larger models with fewer parameters, reducing storage and deployment burdens. Yet repeated attention and MLP computations remain costly. Pruning reduces storage and computation by removing redundant parameters or units. Training-free methods struggle to resolve recurrent contributions, while training-based methods lack explicit constraints on position-specific errors affecting later inputs and final outputs. We propose LoopPrune, a pruning and recovery framework addressing these requirements. Recurrent importance estimation combines shared-removal effects and position variation to select a shared structure; shared reconstruction pools recurrent input statistics to compensate weights. Loop-position recovery compares complete denoising predictions at identical student states and matches text guidance, updating all student parameters against a fixed dense teacher. Extensive experiments across four open-source models, six benchmarks, and three pruning paradigms demonstrate effectiveness. At 30% structured parameter pruning, Looped-DiT B/16 retains 93.14% of dense six-benchmark average accuracy, exceeding the best baseline by 9.49%. Across PyTorch, vLLM-Omni, and SGLang Diffusion, pruned SDXSv3 reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 34.36-44.64% and accelerates denoising 1.46-1.74x over dense models.
  </details>

- **[Alleviating Limited Effectiveness and Multi-Class Collapse in Deep Graph-level Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06052)**  `arXiv:2611.06052`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyang Lin, Renchi Yang, Yuqi Chen, Xin Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a collection of $N$ graphs, graph-level clustering (GLC) seeks to partition the $N$ graphs into semantically meaningful groups, which serves as a fundamental task in analyzing graph-structured data. While Graph Isomorphism Networks (GINs) have become the de facto encoders for GLC, they still fail to distinguish graphs due to their neglect of global topology. On top of that, existing deep clustering frameworks suffer from severe "clustering collapse" issues, particularly in imbalanced multi-class scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose S$^3$GLC, a novel framework that integrates global topological characteristics with local neighborhood features for balanced GLC. Theoretically, we leverage the Hoffman-Wielandt inequality to construct Spectral Signature as a rigorous lower bound for graph edit distance (GED), providing a computationally efficient proxy for global topological features. We additionally propose a joint manifold-preserving sparse regression module to fuse Spectral Signature with Spatial Representation, eliminating noise while preserving the complementary information. Furthermore, to mitigate clustering collapse, we introduce a balanced clustering objective based on optimal transport, harnessing the Sinkhorn-Knopp normalization to enforce equipartition constraints on cluster assignments. Extensive experiments on 10 real-world datasets demonstrate that S$^3$GLC significantly outperforms 15 state-of-the-art baselines, achieving remarkable improvements on benchmark datasets such as REDDIT-12K and OGBG-PPA.
  </details>

- **[HiPPO Functional Memory: Coordinate-Queryable Attention for Irregular Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24501)**  `arXiv:2610.24501`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaodong Guo, Haoyun Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregular time series require models to answer temporal queries at arbitrary coordinates, yet existing approaches typically retain observation-dependent representations that must be repeatedly resolved for each query. We introduce HiPPO Functional Memory (HiPPO-FM), a functional-memory attention operator that compiles an irregular history into a reusable coordinate-addressable state. Instead of storing observation-indexed tokens or latent trajectories, HiPPO-FM writes observations into a fixed-rank functional space and performs direct coordinate evaluation through matched functional geometry. Kernel-smoothed HiPPO coordinates and Gram-based readout define a shared write/read mechanism, while observed-support self-attention enables content-dependent representations before compression. We characterize the induced finite-dimensional query space and derive an error decomposition separating functional geometry approximation from learned-memory mismatch. Across continuous-coordinate reconstruction, irregular representation learning, real-world extrapolation, and event-sequence prediction, HiPPO-FM consistently improves strong irregular time-series baselines, achieving strong reconstruction accuracy on Spiral, competitive performance across 20 UEA datasets, improved real-world extrapolation, and superior results on several irregular event benchmarks. After encoding, the compact functional state can be reused across different query sets without reprocessing the observation history.
  </details>

- **[The Ephemeral Cost of Switching Optimizers Across LLM Training Stages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22650)**  `arXiv:2610.22650`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xinyu Zhao, Davis Wertheimer, Ashutosh Sharma, Rameswar Panda, Paul Pu Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work suggests that switching optimizers between pretraining and subsequent training stages incurs a performance cost, but systematic investigation of this phenomenon is lacking.
