We study the mismatch between AdamW and Muon optimizers by pretraining 1.2B Llama-3 models from scratch while varying the proportion of updates taken with each optimizer, and similarly interpolating between the two optimizers during continued pretraining and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Surprisingly, we find little evidence of a meaningful optimizer mismatch penalty: final performance is largely explained by the independent effects of the pretraining and post-training optimizers, with downstream performance generally improving as the proportion of Muon updates during SFT increases. Although matching the pretraining optimizer can reduce forgetting during SFT, this does not translate into improved final benchmark performance. We further evaluate AdamW and Muon for task-general SFT of open-weight models, across the Llama and Qwen families up to 14B parameters, where Muon remains competitive with AdamW, despite these models having been pretrained with AdamW. Finally, we investigate whether our findings extend to RL via experiments on our 1.2B models as well as Qwen3-1.7B and 4B, and find that AdamW and Muon achieve comparable final performance in math RLVR. Together, our results suggest that the choice of optimizer for post-training can be made largely independently of the optimizer used during pretraining.
  </details>

- **[Can We Predict kNN-Based Anomalous Sound Detection Performance from Embedding-Space Geometry?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26460)**  `arXiv:2609.26460`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kevin Wilkinghoff, Zheng-Hua Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomaly detection systems are often trained using normal data alone, while model selection and evaluation typically require labeled anomalies. We study anomaly-free selection for k-nearest neighbor (kNN)-based anomaly detectors operating on embedding spaces, using pretrained audio representations and temporal pooling for first-shot industrial anomalous sound detection as a practically relevant application. We derive a lower bound on the area under the ROC curve (AUC) at the score level and use it to analyze kNN-based detection, relating score variability to the geometry of the embedding space. Under a local scaling model, we characterize how density variation, intrinsic-dimensional heterogeneity, and cross-domain mismatch contribute to inlier-score variability, and show how local density-based score normalization (LDN) affects these contributions. We then investigate anomaly-free model selection and show that inlier score variance alone does not reliably predict performance across different representations. To address this limitation, we introduce simple pseudo-anomaly probes that provide a reference for estimating relative score separation. Experiments on the DCASE 2022-2025 benchmarks, spanning four pretrained audio encoders and 208 candidate systems evaluate both kNN and LDN. The results show that LDN improves detection performance, while pseudo-anomaly-based estimators substantially improve anomaly-free model selection. The AUC Bound provides robust selection across pseudo-anomaly constructions, and a simple metadata-free construction offers a competitive alternative to existing approaches, even outperforming conventional development-set selection when combined with LDN.
  </details>

- **[SAVOR: Score Once, Learn Deeper in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24141)**  `arXiv:2609.24141`  `cs.LG`  
  _Keye Zheng, Hanyu Li, Yuan Gao, Zhan Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own samples with token-level scores from a stronger teacher, and it effectively transfers reasoning ability to small language models. Each training step pays for student rollouts and teacher scoring, yet common OPD configurations make a single pass over the scored batch and then discard it. We observe that reusing the batch alone is not enough, because no single fixed teacher target suits every batch. In our runs, a fixed $\alpha$ of 0.3 exceeds a KL envelope at every training step, while 0.2 leaves over a quarter of it unused. We propose SAVOR, which selects for each batch the strongest teacher target inside the KL envelope, freezes the scored batch, and reuses it for a fixed number of student updates without further teacher calls. On five math reasoning benchmarks with a 1.7B student and a 30B-A3B teacher, SAVOR raises macro accuracy over TOP-D from 15.41 to 17.78 with two passes and to 19.36 with three passes at about the same teacher budget. With three passes, SAVOR nearly matches the final accuracy of TOP-D after one third of training while using 67.2% fewer teacher-scored tokens. These results suggest budgeting reuse depth explicitly in OPD.
  </details>

- **[Graph-Based Stochastic Power-UCT: Monte-Carlo Graph Search with Power Mean Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19956)**  `arXiv:2609.19956`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tung Tran, Viet Bao Mai, Hoang Ta, Tuan Dam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tree-based Monte-Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) duplicates the same state when it is reached through different trajectories, which can waste simulations in stochastic MDPs. We introduce Graph-Based Stochastic-Power-UCT (GS-Power-UCT), which shares states reached at the same planning depth while keeping separate values for states reached at different depths. This design applies to general stochastic MDPs, including problems with cycles. We prove that for a fixed planning horizon, the root estimate converges to the finite-horizon value at rate $O(n^{-1/2})$, matching tree-based Stochastic-Power-UCT while reusing samples across shared states. We also study two full-state variants: GS-Power-UCT-F, which stores one node per physical state to increase sample sharing but may mix values from different remaining horizons, and GS-Power-UCT-F$^+$, which uses an adaptive horizon to control this bias. The latter converges to $V^{\star}(s_0)$, the optimal infinite-horizon discounted value at the root state $s_0$, when the remaining cross-depth gap vanishes. Experiments on stochastic planning benchmarks show improved sample efficiency over tree-based and graph-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[CommitKV: Commit-Aligned KV Cache Compression for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07855)**  `arXiv:2608.07855`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Jinchao Zhang, Xiawu Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents gather visual evidence through reasoning and tool use, extending their ability to solve tasks beyond the initial input. However, accumulating observations increases KV cache memory and inference costs. Token eviction reduces these costs by retaining useful cached tokens. Existing methods use attention, redundancy, or stage-specific queries to rank tokens, without explicitly comparing the same history across tool interactions. When observations shift attention, they may mistake temporarily reduced influence for lost usefulness and evict information needed later. We propose CommitKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that accounts for this changing evidence use. Commit-aligned measurement compares the deletion effects of the same page before a tool call completes and after its observation returns. Lifecycle-aware compression retires pages with low influence in both windows and temporarily protects pages influential before the call. Joint scoring combines attention importance, key redundancy, and token-level deletion effects to fill the remaining budget. Extensive experiments cover four open-weight multimodal models and eight benchmarks. CommitKV achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared compression methods on all four models. On Qwen3-VL-8B, it achieves 69.85% at a 512-token budget, only 0.91% below FullKV, and exceeds the strongest compression baseline by 8.44% at 256 tokens. Deploying the Qwen3-VL-8B agent with SGLang and vLLM at a 256-token budget reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 54.37%-55.23% and accelerates end-to-end inference by 2.12-2.17$\times$ over FullKV on the matched workload.
  </details>

- **[LaWanle: Why Can't Leading Driving Models Make a Simple Turn?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32650)**  `arXiv:2611.32650`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Chengzhi Gao, Junzhe Gao, Yongzhi Liu, Weichao Zhuang, Weilong Hu, Taonian Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We were surprised to find that leading end-to-end driving models across major paradigms, despite scoring close to human drivers on NAVSIM, cannot reliably make a simple 90-degree turn in photorealistic closed-loop simulators. To investigate why, we build LaWanle, a benchmark of turns drawn from three such simulators. Driving each turn from several starts, we find that the same model passes it from one start and fails it from another, and that the two drives look alike until they enter the junction. Rotating the car by a few degrees at the junction entry can fail a turn it otherwise passes. Conversely, a slight heading correction to the plans can rescue about half of the failed turns we tested. Such errors arise when a model drives by itself, so we post-train an open-source lane-keeping model with closed-loop DAgger on its lateral path. Training 5% of its parameters is enough to raise its turn pass rate from 19% to 76%. The gain transfers to the two other benchmarks, where the policy also scores among the leading models. Further fine-tuning on recovery data repairs the deviations it is trained on but, against a control trained equally long, improves turns on none of the three benchmarks, and every variant stays far below a human driver.
  </details>

- **[GOLD-SLAM: Gaussian-splatting Omnidirectional LiDAR-Depth SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32335)**  `arXiv:2611.32335`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Weicong Zhang, Yuan Huang, Yu Zheng, Lin Chenxiao, Yingying Li, Lilong Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Panoramic cameras capture nearly the entire spherical field of view in a single frame. However, the mismatch between this panoramic coverage and the field of view of LiDAR poses a substantial challenge for panoramic Gaussian splatting SLAM that relies on LiDAR depth priors. We introduce GOLD-SLAM, a Gaussian-splatting Omnidirectional LiDAR-Depth SLAM system that combines high-quality rendering with accurate geometric reconstruction. It performs tracking and mapping in the spherical domain and uses tracked visual features in regions without LiDAR coverage to improve pose estimation. To address insufficient coverage of newly observed surfaces and floating artifacts during incremental mapping, we introduce Depth-Aware Map Extension and Pruning (DAEP). To enable broader evaluation, we collect a new dataset, WHOLE, using a UAV platform across Weak-geometry, High-dynamic, Omnidirectional, Low-light, and Easy scenarios. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks and WHOLE demonstrate competitive rendering quality and improved geometric accuracy relative to perspective systems. Compared with existing panoramic baselines, GOLD-SLAM improves mean PSNR by 5.56-9.10 dB and reduces mean Depth-L1 by 71.9-83.9%. To assess deployment feasibility on resource-constrained platforms such as UAVs, we evaluate an embedded configuration of GOLD-SLAM on a Jetson Orin NX (16 GB), demonstrating online operation at the cost of reconstruction quality.
  </details>

- **[RoboProbe: Benchmarking Physical Plausibility of Robot World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32152)**  `arXiv:2611.32152`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhuoying Yang, Jiachen Ni, Yichen Guo, Xiaoshan Wu, Mengyu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating the physical plausibility of generated videos is critical for reliable robotic planning with world models. These videos inform action proposal and assessment before real-world execution, so undetected physical violations can lead to erroneous assessments of action feasibility and safety. Yet current video generators often produce physically implausible interactions. Detecting such violations directly with vision-language models (VLMs) remains challenging as physical verification requires evidence-grounded spatiotemporal reasoning beyond high-level semantic understanding. We develop five law-specific metrics for evaluating physical plausibility by combining spatiotemporal evidence from segmentation, tracking, 3D geometry, or appearance comparisons with targeted VLM reasoning: Contact fidelity, Object persistence, Solidity, Robot deformation, and Object deformation. Building on this metric suite, we introduce RoboProbe, an agentic evaluation framework that coordinates metric execution and selectively refines evaluations through constrained actions. To evaluate the physical plausibility of robot world models, we further introduce RP-Bench, with 761 human-labeled generated videos and 130 real demonstrations of 239 manipulation tasks from 12 robot datasets. On RP-Bench, all five metrics achieve AUROCs above 0.85 and outperform the evaluated learned judges, prior evaluators, and a frames-only Gemini baseline. Applying these metrics to five video generators reveals distinct patterns of physical violations across models, in both violation types and frequencies.
  </details>

- **[TACTUS: Unifying Surgical Action Understanding in Time and Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31956)**  `arXiv:2611.31956`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Lurui Wang, Hao Ding, Chenhao Yu, Chenyan Jing, Jiru Xu, Ruoqi Zuo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancing surgical automation requires a comprehensive spatio-temporal understanding of how instruments act on tissue throughout an action. Existing surgical benchmarks, however, largely define supervision through task-specific targets, leaving the relationships between action semantics, instrument motion, and tissue interaction incompletely specified. To address this limitation, we introduce TACTUS (Tool, Action, Contact and Trajectory Understanding in Surgery), a new dataset and benchmark that establishes a unified annotation framework for complete surgical actions. The framework jointly specifies tool and action identities, nested action and interaction windows, instrument-specific landmark trajectories, and tissue-region tracks. To construct these annotations at scale, we develop a two-tier pipeline combining human annotation, point tracking, and model-generated spatial prompts. Using this pipeline, we re-annotate 141 videos from five public sources, producing 22,995 action clips spanning 2.9 million frames. The dataset comprises 15,668 Gold clips with human spatial prompts followed by review and re-checking, and 7,327 Silver clips combining human semantic and temporal annotations with model-generated spatial prompts. We further introduce TactusNet, the companion reference network, to benchmark semantic, temporal, and spatial prediction from shared video features. Across TactusNet and 13 published baselines, tool-action accuracy reaches 95.8% with per-head encoder fine-tuning, while the best temporal recall and contact Dice reach 46.6% at temporal IoU 0.7 and 0.351, respectively. Moreover, adding Silver improves the default TactusNet's pair accuracy from 93.8% to 95.5%. Together, these contributions establish a foundation for learning and evaluating the instrument-tissue interactions underlying future surgical automation.
  </details>

- **[VLG-Drive: Unifying Vision-Language and Geometric Priors for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31952)**  `arXiv:2611.31952`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Junge Zhang, Jie Zhu, Yanqi Jia, Xiangyu Xu, Zixun Huang, Wenbin He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A driving planner needs both scene semantics, which vision-language models (VLMs) provide, and spatial structure, which geometric foundation models recover from visual history. We present VLG-Drive, which keeps both models frozen and trains only a fusion interface and a planner from trajectory labels. At each camera-grid cell, the interface fuses the paired VLM and geometry features into one joint token through a geometry-anchored residual, and a command-conditioned planner reads these tokens to predict the ego trajectory. VLG-Drive achieves 31.78 EPDMS on NavHard under perturbed initial states, the highest among the compared methods, and 88.27 PDMS on NavTest from cameras alone. Pointwise fusion outperforms independent VLM and geometry streams on both benchmarks. In closed-loop driving on 420 HUGSIM scenarios, VLG-Drive outperforms all compared planners. These results indicate that a lightweight planner trained on fused features of frozen vision-language and geometric foundation models is an effective alternative to adapting either model for planning.
  </details>

- **[G0.7: Scaling Up a Fast World Action Model with Long Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31822)**  `arXiv:2611.31822`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Tianyuan Yuan, Baijun Ye, Shicheng Cao, Xing Nie, Xiaopeng Yu, Ke Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) adapt pretrained video generators for robot control by jointly predicting future camera frames and actions, transferring physical priors learned from video into manipulation policies. However, generating future frames before every action makes test-time inference too slow for closed-loop control, while most existing policies remain restricted to short observation histories. Building on Fast-WAM, which showed that video prediction shapes policy representations during training so that future frame synthesis can be skipped at deployment, we present G0.7, a generalist world action model scaled along two axes: extending the context window from a single observation to up to five minutes of history, and scaling pretraining to a large-scale corpus of robot teleoperation and human egocentric (ego) video. Across public simulation suites and real-robot deployments, direct action decoding (Fast mode) matches imagine-then-act (IDM) inference from the same checkpoint while running in 60 ms per 16-step action chunk on a single RTX 5090 (7.6x faster than IDM) and supporting pause-free real-time chunking. Performance scales consistently with pretraining data, with human ego video driving the largest gains in out-of-distribution generalization: G0.7 achieves a 70.8 mean score across five simulation benchmark suites, 48% and 54% zero-shot pick-and-place success on Galaxea R1 Pro and R1 Lite, 96.7% in-distribution and 93.3% out-of-distribution success on video-conditioned in-context learning tasks, and 86.9% mean task progress across seven real-world post-training tasks including multi-minute box folding, drink preparation, and product bagging.
  </details>

- **[LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25595)**  `arXiv:2611.25595`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Anh Dao, Xinxin Liu, Hang Yu, Jeffrey A Chan Santiago, Yuzhang Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, a compact WAM that recurrently applies a shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains a single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using $7.4\times$ less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracy-latency operating points from a single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks.
  </details>

- **[BioLoom: Scaling Biomedical Vision-Language Models by Learning from the Structure of Scientific Articles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33151)**  `arXiv:2611.33151`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuwei Yang, Xiaoxiao Sun, Alejandro Lozano, Yuan-Sen Ting, Yuhui Yuan, Liang Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The development of biomedical vision-language models (VLMs) with contrastive image-text pretraining has been driven by scaling to increasingly large and diverse image-text datasets. Beyond scaling, further progress can come from learning richer representations from existing data. We hypothesize that the structure of scientific literature itself provides an additional source of supervision: figures, panels, captions, and accompanying article text encode rich relationships across modalities. Yet, treating these as independent image-text pairs overlooks their relationships. To address this limitation, we incorporate these structural relationships into contrastive image-text supervision. We preserve exact image-text matches while introducing weaker positive supervision for related pairs, such as a panel and the caption of a sibling panel. We upweight cross-article negatives of the same content type to encourage finer discrimination. We also enrich panel descriptions using visual content, original figure captions, and mentioning paragraphs. Pairing panels with these descriptions and whole figures with their original captions yields Loom52M, a dataset of 52.4 million image-text pairs spanning scientific charts and biomedical images. Using Loom52M, BioLoom (DFN-2B) achieves 56.82% domain-balanced mean accuracy across 67 zero-shot classification tasks from 43 datasets in 10 domains, compared with 48.77% for MedPMC-CLIP. On BioLoomBench with concise captions and BioMedFlickr, it achieves bidirectional mean Recall@1 of 63.20% and 10.59%, versus 46.37% and 7.55% for the respective strongest evaluated baselines, BiomedCLIP and MedPMC-CLIP. Code, datasets, benchmarks, and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Relational Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32227)**  `arXiv:2611.32227`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Shin'ya Yamaguchi, Kosuke Nishida_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving both interpretable and high-performance inference is a key challenge in deep learning. While Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide inherent interpretability, they are constrained by task-specific training and rely on isolated visual concepts that fail to capture higher-order visual semantic relationships. Conversely, Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate zero-shot capabilities through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, standard CoT lacks a strict causal dependency between the generated rationale and the final output, compromising faithfulness. To bridge this gap, we propose Relational Bottleneck Models (RBMs), a novel training-free framework that transforms any off-the-shelf LVLMs into inherently interpretable models in a plug-and-play manner. RBMs consist of two stages: (i) Structured Rationale Generation (SRG), which explicitly models semantic interactions between visual concepts as subject-predicate-object triplets, and (ii) Rationale-Grounded Decoding (RGD), which enforces a strict causal dependency between the rationales and the final output. RGD aggregates rationale-conditional next-token distributions via an importance-weighted product-of-experts strategy, strictly excluding the image inputs from the decoding context. Extensive zero-shot evaluations across diverse benchmarks and LVLM backbones demonstrate that RBMs achieve superior interpretability while improving task performance over CBM baselines, entirely without task-specific fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[LoopZip: Visual Token Compression for Efficient Looped Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22728)**  `arXiv:2611.22728`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Zhicheng Liu, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped vision-language models (VLMs) deepen computation by reusing shared layers, approaching larger models' capabilities with fewer parameters and easing the storage and deployment burden of stacking independent layers. However, repeatedly processing long visual sequences still incurs substantial compute and GPU memory costs. Visual token compression reduces these costs by shortening sequences. Existing methods mainly select original tokens directly or combine selection with aggregation. In looped models, the former may overlook changing token importance across calls and discard information needed later; the latter lack joint consideration of output changes across calls, limiting information preservation. We propose LoopZip, a training-free visual token compression algorithm addressing both issues. Visual aggregation builds a compact candidate pool before the looped backbone, and cross-invocation selection averages instruction attention from the first two existing calls of the same shared attention layer to retain tokens needed by both states. Weight-tied value correction then jointly fits shared visual-value adjustments to both pre-selection layer outputs, reducing output deviations across states. To validate LoopZip's effectiveness, we conduct extensive experiments covering two open-source looped VLMs, eleven benchmarks, and three pruning ratios. On LoopVL-1B, LoopZip retains 97.16% of the original model's performance at 50% pruning ratio and exceeds the strongest baseline by 5.36% at 87.5% pruning ratio. Across Hugging Face Transformers, vLLM, and SGLang, LoopVL-1B with LoopZip reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 58.36%-58.98% and achieves 2.01-2.47x end-to-end speedups over the original model.
  </details>

- **[ARS-VLM: Visually Aligned Augmented Random Search for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32147)**  `arXiv:2611.32147`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiangwei Wang, Jianyuan Lin, Peng Wang, Saman Halgamuge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  First-order reinforcement learning, such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), is widely used to post-train Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for multimodal reasoning. However, it incurs heavy memory and system overhead from backpropagation, optimizer states, and a reference model. Forward-only zeroth-order (ZO) methods, such as Evolution Strategies (ES) and its top-k variant Augmented Random Search (ARS), avoid these overheads via reward-guided weight perturbations, yet remain unexplored for VLM reasoning. We find that directly applying standard ES or vanilla ARS to VLMs suffers from two fundamental failure modes: (i) patch-grid overfitting, fitting fragile grid-specific visual token artifacts while ignoring spatial invariants, and (ii) catastrophic forgetting, degrading general perception and out-of-distribution (OOD) tasks due to unbounded high-rank parameter and policy drift. We propose ARS-VLM, a visually aligned Augmented Random Search framework for forward-only VLM post-training. First, ARS-VLM extracts a contrastive visual basis from bilinearly paired cross-grid views, concentrating perturbations on shared visual signals while suppressing patch-shift artifacts. Second, ARS-VLM anchors shallow visual-fusion layers with a soft subspace prior while freeing deep reasoning layers, restraining policy drift without a reference model. Third, ARS-VLM scores perturbations on paired views to discard view-fragile directions during top-k selection. We evaluate ARS-VLM on Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B on multimodal reasoning tasks. On 8B, ARS-VLM reaches 61.0% validation accuracy in 20 steps versus 150 for GRPO (7.5x fewer optimization steps) and avoids the late OOD collapse of vanilla ARS and ES. Moreover, ARS-VLM achieves 75.41% single-view micro accuracy across seven OOD vision reasoning benchmarks, compared with 75.31% for full-parameter GRPO, using forward-only post-training.
  </details>

- **[When Repair Accuracy Misleads: Training a VLM on Its Own Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32982)**  `arXiv:2611.32982`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Ang Qi, Yue Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning a vision-language model on its own failures is a direct way to repair it, but accuracy on held-out failures, the obvious measure of success, misleads. Many mined failures are not model errors: a human audit of 100 failures of Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct on questions generated from COCO and GQA found label errors in 37. And mined failures share wording regularities: question-only classifiers get both answers right on 73.2% of held-out failure pairs. We study both traps with verified failure-driven repair (VFR): typed minimal-pair failure mining, verification by three LLM judges that answer without seeing the stored labels, answer-first corrective targets, and LoRA fine-tuning with replay. VFR repairs 82.8% of held-out failures (base model: 10.0%), and on independently labeled, unconditioned pairs it raises overall accuracy from 87.4% to 92.4% through spatial relations, whose accuracy rises from 66.7% to 96.8% even where the wording points to the wrong answer. Yet on negation, of which it repairs 88.9% of held-out failures, its accuracy on unconditioned pairs falls from 88.7% to 79.7%: it breaks negations about absent objects, rare in its training set. Without verification, accuracy on unconditioned pairs falls to 80.8%, below the base model's 87.4%, through a negation shortcut our development benchmarks miss; without failure selection, it stays at 92.0% (one seed) while repair falls to 73.4%. Training also lowers two-image relation accuracy (74.3% to 49.6%; replay data alone: 48.4%); multi-image replay partly and unevenly reverses this. Paraphrasing the spatial questions keeps most of the gain (87.3% vs. the base model's 62.0%), though rewording lowers spatial repair on held-out failures from 92.9% to 76.8%. On What'sUp's independent photos of objects under others, caption choice stays perfect while accuracy on our yes/no pairs falls from 84.5% to 26.4%.
  </details>

- **[STAR: Counterfactual Dual-World Rollouts and Spatial Target Adaptive Reconstruction for Visual On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32472)**  `arXiv:2611.32472`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yi Zou, Zhuang Yan, Yudong Zhang, Xu Wang, Yang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models often rely on global context and language priors while overlooking answer-determining local details. Privileged visual on-policy self-distillation supervises full-image student rollouts using a frozen teacher that views high-resolution evidence crops. Yet our cross-scale diagnostics show that models retain over half of their full-image accuracy when local evidence is completely masked, indicating that single-image correctness does not guarantee reliance on local evidence, while the need for visual correction also varies across tokens with their spatial attention states. We propose STAR, a visual on-policy self-distillation framework combining independent counterfactual dual-world rollouts with spatial-attention-guided target reconstruction. Local image editing constructs paired worlds with identical questions and backgrounds but distinct answer-determining facts, where a shared student samples trajectories independently to expose contextual shortcuts under image-consistent prefixes. Within each world, the teacher contrasts real and mean-color crops to determine the visual correction direction, while the divergence between student image attention and a teacher-centroid spatial reference adaptively controls token-wise target log-probability reconstruction. Without auxiliary attention losses or test-time tool overhead, STAR improves Qwen3.5 across scales by up to 8.04 percentage points in average accuracy over initialization across six fine-grained benchmarks, reaches OPSD's final accuracy $4.7\times$ faster, and generalizes to out-of-domain reasoning tasks.
  </details>

- **[Embodied AI Fails the Mirror Test: How a Simple Mirror Breaks Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33010)**  `arXiv:2611.33010`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Xuanchen Liu, Hejun Zhang, Boxuan Dong, Ying Wu, Heshuai Li, Wen Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a simple mirror expose the limitations of today's robotic manipulation policies? Inspired by the mirror self-recognition test in animal cognition, we investigate whether robot policies can reliably manipulate physical objects in scenes containing their reflections. We introduce RoboDojo-Mirror, a benchmark built on RoboDojo that preserves task objectives, success criteria, and policy checkpoints while comparing a mirror-free baseline with single-mirror, dual-mirror, and mirrored-tabletop conditions. Across five policies spanning Vision-Language-Action models, World Action Models, and agentic policies, every mirror condition reduces aggregate success rates: averaged over the three conditions, the drop ranges from 10.8 to 56.7 percentage points across policies, and it reaches 62.5 points on the mirrored tabletop. Because a mirror is also a physical obstacle, we compare it against geometrically matched non-reflective panels; 50--88\% of mirror-specific failures occur without recorded robot--panel contact, so direct collisions alone do not explain the degradation. Trajectory and contact analyses instead reveal recurring behaviors consistent with phantom targeting of reflected objects and spatial mislocalization of real ones. Targeted diagnostics further characterize grasping near reflections, policy-dependent pushing progress, and physical-target interaction under main-camera occlusion. Real-robot experiments with $\pi_{0.5}$ and FastWAM also show lower overall success rates under the upright-mirror and mirrored-tabletop conditions. These findings reveal a shared vulnerability to reflective environments across diverse robot policies and motivate reflection-aware evaluation of embodied intelligence. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ICLWorld: Benchmarking In-Context Learning of Agents from Digital to Physical Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32649)**  `arXiv:2611.32649`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Qingle Liu, Jieming Li, Pei-lin Li, Shenghan Zheng, Ruize Yang, Sunqi Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning (ICL) lets agents acquire new rules from context and adapt to user-specific requirements at inference time. The same frontier agents now operate both computers and robots, yet their ICL remains insufficiently assessed: existing benchmarks are largely offline, rely on text or static images, and risk training-data contamination, while robot evaluations are limited to method-level ablations. We present ICLWorld, an online benchmark that evaluates the ICL of agents from digital to physical worlds, covering computer use and robot use, together with UIverse, a unified harness that turns every environment into a user interface. We build 30 environments, record 630 demonstration videos (10+ hours), and design 160 tasks with customized, often counter-intuitive rules that cannot be satisfied without learning from the context. Each task comes with three rule versions, given as text or video, and we test generalization at three levels, from replaying the demonstration to new instances and unseen environments. Across six frontier models, the strongest agents, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, succeed on 82% and 76% of computer-use runs but only 27% and 26% of robot-use runs, and a no-rule control reduces success to at most 8%. GPT-6 Astra succeeds under all three rule versions on only 38% of computer-use tasks, half its rate under a single rule, and answers 98% of robot rule questions correctly yet completes only 20% of these tasks. Frontier agents learn rules comparably well from video and from text and transfer them across environments, whereas weaker agents rely on text and lose most of their video-based success once the demonstrated situation is resampled. These findings reveal substantial gaps between the digital and the physical world and between identifying a rule and acting on it.
  </details>

- **[Text-to-CAD Evaluation with CADTests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07807)**  `arXiv:2605.07807`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Dimitrios Mallis, Marco Wang, Ahmet Serdar Karadeniz, Elisa Ricci, Anis Kacem, Djamila Aouada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-CAD has recently emerged as an important task with the potential to substantially accelerate design workflows. Despite its significance, there has been surprisingly little work on Text-to-CAD evaluation, and assessing CAD model generation performance remains a considerable challenge. In this work, we introduce a new evaluation perspective for Text-to-CAD based on automated testing. We propose CADTestBench, the first test-based benchmark for Text-to-CAD, based on CADTests, executable software tests that verify whether a generated CAD model satisfies the geometric and topological requirements of the input prompt. Using CADTestBench, we conduct comprehensive benchmarking of recent Text-to-CAD methods and further demonstrate that CADTests can also guide CAD model generation, yielding simple baselines that surpass performance of current methods. CADTestBench code and data are available.
  </details>

- **[VIDE : Training-Free Video Anomaly Detection via Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32138)**  `arXiv:2611.32138`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Kevin D. Nguetche, Atif Belal, Lilian Hollard, Eric Granger, Marco Pedersoli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In embedding-based video anomaly detection (VAD), normal clips can have high similarity to anomalous descriptions, an anomaly bias that leads to false positives. Methods such as PRISM mitigate this bias by measuring deviations from a normality reference along anomaly directions. Both the normality reference and the anomaly directions are derived from text and shared across videos, although normal activity and the appearance of anomalies vary across scenes. The resulting scores are also sensitive to prompt selection. To address these limitations, we propose Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation (VIDE), a training-free framework that adapts text-derived scoring to each test video using frozen clip embeddings. VIDE estimates normal scene content from clips with low initial anomaly scores and uses this estimate to partially correct the textual reference. It then rescales category responses, penalizes clips with strong responses across categories, and refines the anomaly directions using high-scoring clips. VIDE averages scores across prompt views to reduce sensitivity to prompt selection and fuses scores from two frozen encoders. With both encoders, VIDE improves AUC over the best PRISM result by $1.5$, $1.9$, and $3.5$ points on UCF-Crime, XD-Violence, and MSAD, respectively, and AP by $6.5$ points on XD-Violence and $9.3$ points on MSAD. VIDE yields the highest AUC among the compared training-free methods on all three benchmarks and the highest reported AUC among all compared methods on XD-Violence and MSAD. Our code is included in the supplementary material and will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[EventSSM: Causal Spatial-State Modelling for Asynchronous Events Event-Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31720)**  `arXiv:2611.31720`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Ghosh, Hadfield Simon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Event cameras report asynchronous brightness changes, yet metric-depth supervision is typically available only at sparsely sampled reference timestamps. Aggregating events into frame-like representations simplifies learning but can obscure the fine-grained temporal structure of the original event stream. We introduce EventSSM, a causal spatial-state framework for event-associated metric-depth estimation using a recurrent memory updated by chronological event packets. To bridge asynchronous predictions and frame-time supervision, predicted event depths are geometrically transported using calibrated camera poses and differentiably rendered at reference timestamps. Benchmark evaluations demonstrate improvements over established event-depth methods across multiple depth-error measures. Controlled ablations further examine cross-modal initialization, temporal recurrence, individual state, readout, and supervision components, and packet subdivision. Together, these findings establish EventSSM as an effective framework for causal event-associated metric-depth estimation, combining persistent spatial context with physically timed state retention.
  </details>

- **[Find the Fungible: Variable-Resolution Tokenization that Compacts Semantics with a Granularity Dial as a VLM "Catalyst"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18690)**  `arXiv:2610.18690`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianhao Fu, Austin Wang, Charles Chen, Roby Aldave-Garza, Victor Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient vision-language models should preserve a useful representation of the whole image, even under tight budgets. However, aggressive pruning or indiscriminate merging can erase critical details and disrupt the spatial structure. We thus propose Varest, which maximizes semantic fungibility: tokens describing coherent content can be summarized together with minimal information loss. Object boundaries distilled from a visual foundation model guide grouping, while text localization protects details needed for reading. At lower budgets, Varest merges the image into coarser semantic regions, while maintaining full spatial coverage. After early language decoder layers process this representation, a learned selector prunes visual tokens that later layers no longer need, reducing repeated access to already integrated information. Across three LLaVA backbones and 13 benchmarks, Varest outperforms 10 training-free and 5 trained baselines in mean performance retention at every evaluated budget, preserving 86.10-92.33% of uncompressed performance using only 5.56% of tokens. This SOTA performance further generalizes to modern VLMs like Qwen3.8-27B.
  </details>

- **[ASTERISM: Do Multimodal LLMs Actually Look at All the Images?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32316)**  `arXiv:2611.32316`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Chanseul Cho, Dongwoo Kim, Noori Bae, Seungjae Moon, Seongsu Lee, Youngmin Ro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-image benchmarks aim to test whether a multimodal large language model (MLLM) combines evidence across images, but many of their items can be answered from a single image. Across about 33k items from 19 public benchmarks, our audit and a two-stage single-image filter find that one image or the question alone settles 24.2% of them. On these items, MLLMs recover from one image a median 78% of the accuracy that all images add over the text alone, so these items reward answering from one image, which we call the single-image shortcut. MLLMs take this shortcut even on items where one image is not enough. On such items, most of their wrong answers repeat an answer they give from one image, at a rate well above chance. We build ASTERISM, to our knowledge the largest audited multi-image benchmark, from these 19 benchmarks by keeping their items that need more than one image. Its 18.7k items, 2.0k of them corrected or rewritten, share one format, one scorer and seven task categories. We also release ASTERISM-Train with 49k training items and ASTERISM-Lite, a hard subset whose items we manually verify one by one. On ASTERISM-Lite, the strongest proprietary MLLM scores 68%. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), now common in MLLM post-training, rewards the shortcut as well, because a correct answer read off one image earns as much reward as one that combines images. In line with this, existing RLVR objectives add little to the ASTERISM score. To stop rewarding the shortcut in training as ASTERISM does in evaluation, we propose cross-image credit assignment (CICA), an extension of RLVR that moves the credit toward the tokens that need more than one image. On three open-source MLLMs, CICA raises the ASTERISM score by 2.6 to 6.0 points, more than twice the gain of each general and vision-specific RL objective trained on the same data.
  </details>

- **[MMGenBench: An Automated Pipeline for Evaluating LMMs from a Text-to-Image Generation Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.14062)**  `arXiv:2411.14062`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hailang Huang, Yong Wang, Zixuan Huang, Huaqiu Li, Tongwen Huang, Xiangxiang Chu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing benchmarks often rely on predefined questions or reference captions, limiting their flexibility for evaluating detailed descriptions on new image collections. We present MMGenBench, an automated benchmark framework for evaluating Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) from raw images without per-image questions or reference captions. The pipeline generates descriptions, reconstructs images with a frozen text-to-image model, and compares the original and reconstructed images using a frozen image encoder. It produces reusable per-image scores and inspectable reconstructions, supporting both model comparison and diagnosis of reconstructible visual information. We construct MMGenBench-Domain with 10,000 generated images and MMGenBench-Test with 1,284 images spanning 13 overlapping visual patterns. Evaluation of 68 LMMs reveals pattern-specific differences in reconstruction quality that overall scores can obscure. On 1,000 human-labeled caption pairs from six models, the default score achieves 90.00\% sample-level agreement. Factual perturbations and component analyses characterize the score's sensitivity under the evaluated pipeline, while a COCO study provides initial validation on natural photographs. MMGenBench complements existing benchmarks by linking automated comparisons to visual evidence of the information preserved in LMM descriptions. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ThinkMorph-SSL: Scaling Interleaved Multimodal Reasoning with Pretext Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32183)**  `arXiv:2611.32183`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiawei Gu, Linjie Li, Yiming Liu, Yunzhuo Hao, Huichen Wang, Guanzheng Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  People reason with pictures as well as words. Interleaved multimodal reasoning gives models a similar way to solve problems by creating intermediate images and using them to guide further reasoning. Training models with this ability usually relies on task-specific demonstrations, which are difficult to collect. We introduce ThinkMorph-SSL, a two-stage training recipe that uses images themselves as a source of supervision. The first stage, pretext reasoning, converts visual pretext tasks, such as image colorization and denoising, into instruction-free interleaved traces. Models describe a corrupted or transformed image, generate a restored or canonical image, and interpret the result. The second stage uses automatically constructed demonstrations for question-driven instruction tuning. At the same instruction-tuning budget, models trained with pretext reasoning outperform those trained with instruction tuning alone. The full recipe improves over the base model by an average of 11.33 accuracy points across twelve benchmarks. These results show that supervision derived from images can build the visual skills needed for interleaved reasoning and improve learning from task-specific demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[HuluOPD: Unifying Heterogeneous Medical Specialists into One Lightweight Model with Unlabeled Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32021)**  `arXiv:2611.32021`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuncheng Zhang, Songtao Jiang, Yitian Tang, Zijie Meng, Zihan Xu, Xuxiang Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Specialist medical vision-language models (VLMs) are strong within their specialties but hard to unify: their training data are often private, and independently developed specialists cannot be assumed to share an architecture. Deploying these large models separately is costly. We present HuluOPD, an on-policy distillation recipe that unifies heterogeneous specialists into one lightweight VLM using only unlabeled prompts, without requiring reference answers or the specialists' training data. The lightweight model answers each prompt, and the specialist of the prompt's modality supervises its response; a continuation-aligned cross-tokenizer loss makes this possible even when they differ in architecture and tokenizer. We apply HuluOPD to five specialists, for ultrasound, pathology, optical coherence tomography, fundus photography, and dental imaging. To distill and evaluate them, we build HuluOPDCorpus, 914k unlabeled prompts, and HuluOPDBench, 47k held-out test items, from over 90 public datasets, and score all models under one protocol on 132k test items, including third-party benchmarks. A single model with 10% of the five specialists' combined parameters retains 94% of their question-answering performance, and one with 2% retains 89%. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of unifying heterogeneous medical specialist VLMs without requiring their training data or a shared architecture.
  </details>

- **[CrossProjection: Benchmarking Spatial Grounding across Projection and Section Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00473)**  `arXiv:2608.00473`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kaho Li, Zekai Lin, Pengyu Zeng, Yuqin Dai, Jun Yin, Tianjing Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plans, elevations, and sections describe a three-dimensional structure through incomplete projections that change how an entity is drawn, not only where it is seen from. Reading them means deciding which entities correspond across views and recovering where they lie. We introduce CrossProjection, a benchmark that uses architectural drawings to test whether vision-language models (VLMs) can turn correspondence decisions into geometric answers. Its tasks cover cross-view matching, projection registration, and novel-section prediction, with outputs ranging from judgments and candidate selection to free region boxes and directed section cuts. For inverse section localization, we introduce, to our knowledge, the first VLM benchmark protocol that accepts any cut within an expert-adjudicated band of compatible positions and checks the viewing direction separately. Manually annotated drawings and BIM-derived views provide 33,788 questions from 46 buildings, and we evaluate 22 VLMs on a common set of 2,763. Strong selection or reproduction does not imply cross-view localization: on 100 matched region targets, the best model redraws 98 marked boxes but localizes only 59 across views. Section cuts remain far from expert level, with the leading model trailing two architects by 51.9 and 67.4 percentage points on shared Natural and BIM line questions. Incorrect choices favor mirrored positions, and many cuts face the correct way but violate local geometry. Curriculum fine-tuning on BIM supervision teaches a 9B model to localize regions it could not localize before, raising box accuracy from 0% to 25.3% on held-out BIM buildings and from 0.3% to 30.8% on real drawing sets not used for fine-tuning, whereas section cuts remain largely out of reach.
  </details>

- **[PruneGround: Plug-and-play Spatial Pruning for 3D Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31148)**  `arXiv:2606.31148`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Duc Cao Dinh, Khai Le-Duc, Florent Draye, Chris Ngo, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Bernhard Schölkopf, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Visual Grounding (3DVG) aims to localize target objects in 3D scenes given natural language descriptions. Existing approaches typically perform reasoning over the entire scene, leading to ambiguous predictions and high computational cost, especially in cluttered environments. We observe that many referential expressions rely on local spatial context and often correspond to restricted spatial regions rather than the full scene. Motivated by this insight, we propose PruneGround, an effective plug-and-play framework for 3DVG built upon three key components. First, we introduce Language-Guided Spatial Pruning (LGSP), which leverages a frozen Vision Language Model (VLM) to identify language-relevant regions, thereby reducing spatial computation and grounding candidates in the narrower search space. Second, we propose MultiView-Conditioned Description Reformulation (MCDR), which decomposes complex expressions into simplified target--anchor relations and augments missing spatial cues through multi-view reasoning. Finally, we propose LLM-Grounder, which repurposes a detection-pretrained spatial LLM into a language-conditioned grounding model by aligning point cloud and linguistic representations within the pruned region. Extensive experiments on the three most popular point cloud benchmarks demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art results on all three ScanRefer settings and on 9 out of 10 Nr3D/Sr3D settings. Code and models are publicly available:this https URL
  </details>

- **[One, No One and One Hundred Thousand CTs: Testing Anatomical Control in Text-to-CT Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33003)**  `arXiv:2611.33003`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Francesca Pia Panaccione, Eugenio Lomurno, Matteo Matteucci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in deep learning and the public availability of computed tomography (CT) datasets with paired reports have enabled many Text-to-CT generators. However, their evaluations almost always differ in preprocessing and metrics, which hinders comparison. Moreover, their protocols almost never test how editing the report changes the CT, not even at the same seed, so the report is often assumed to control the anatomy without evidence of how much and how consistently it does. We therefore propose CtrlCT, a toolkit that tests two properties of a generator, Credibility and Control. Credibility tests compare generated CTs, as a population, with real CTs and reports; Control tests are metamorphic and need no reference CT: at a fixed seed, editing one finding should change it as requested, keep negated findings absent and shift distant anatomy no more than a new seed, and the change should hold across seeds. A Report Card summarises all measures and insights for each generator. We benchmark five public generators on 940 chest CTs and 687 edits of pleural effusion and cardiomegaly, holding out a sixth to test the toolkit. Among them, the most credible generators barely respond to an added effusion, while the most responsive produce less realistic and less diverse CTs. The analyses proposed in the literature do not reveal this. We release CtrlCT, its edits, seeds and 30,525 synthetic CTs after acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Evidence Sufficiency in Vision-Language Models for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32964)**  `arXiv:2611.32964`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Nhat Ha Nguyen, Sudipta Chowdhury, Lokesh Das_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable driving VLMs must do more than answer correctly or localize relevant scene actors: they must determine whether the available visual evidence is sufficient to support an answer. Existing evaluations largely treat grounding and answerability as separate measurements, making it difficult to distinguish well-supported predictions from those localized to relevant but insufficient evidence. We introduce DriveAnswer, a benchmark of 20,151 questions across seven reasoning families, that operationalizes this distinction using matched strong- and weak-evidence configurations. Each pair preserves the underlying scene fact and semantic answer while changing whether the observations support answering, making abstention the appropriate response under weak evidence. On the six-family evaluation, the strongest zero-shot baseline reaches 67.31% strong-evidence accuracy, yet only 21.55% of matched questions satisfy both answer-abstain behavior and complete grounding. With controlled adaptation, Gemma-4-12B reaches 63.29% on this joint capability, nearly tripling its zero-shot performance. Qwen3-VL-8B exhibits the same broad improvement in evidence-sensitive answering and grounding. These results show that semantic correctness, evidence sufficiency, and grounding are distinct capabilities that should be evaluated jointly.
  </details>

- **[Motion Conditioned World Modeling for Embodied Visual Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32900)**  `arXiv:2611.32900`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiahao Zhang, Xuan Yao, Junyu Gao, Changsheng Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models support embodied decision-making by predicting future environment states. In embodied visual tracking, uncertain target motion and robot-induced viewpoint changes jointly affect visual observations, making future-state prediction challenging. We propose Motion-Conditioned World Modeling, which incorporates predicted target-motion distributions and robot ego-motion into latent state transitions. A Geometry--Progress module jointly models geometric paths and single-step travel distances through shared motion modes and path-conditioned progress distributions. This formulation captures uncertainty in route selection and motion progress while preserving their dependence. Action-induced coordinate transformations express predicted paths in the next robot frame, providing geometrically grounded conditions for future visual-state prediction. Future visual feature reconstruction supervises the motion predictor through the conditioned transition branch, linking explicit motion representations to subsequent observations. The model is jointly optimized with motion likelihood, feature reconstruction, and expert-action supervision, enabling the tracking policy to combine visual context with predictive motionthis http URLon three embodied tracking benchmarks demonstrate improved tracking performance and cross-environment generalization with a lightweight $0.5$B backbone.
  </details>

- **[Reliable and grounded reporting of any computed tomography scan with FORA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32823)**  `arXiv:2611.32823`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Sezgin Er, Utku Turkbey, Alperen Gur, Omer Faruk Durugol, Suprosanna Shit, Murong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence systems for computed tomography (CT) remain largely restricted to specific anatomical regions, examination types or predefined abnormalities, limiting their ability to provide comprehensive and spatially grounded interpretations. Here we present FORA, a general-purpose CT model that recognizes and spatially localizes 1,028 hierarchically organized radiological concepts and generates reports across the 28 CT protocols represented in our cohort. Trained on 304,962 routine CT examinations supervised by their accompanying reports, FORA learns to localize abnormalities without manually annotated lesions. Its architecture combines multiple-instance learning with an interpretable concept bottleneck that connects spatial evidence, radiological findings and report generation. We first developed and benchmarked the architecture on chest radiographs, achieving state-of-the-art performance, before training a separate model on three-dimensional CT examinations. On 36,619 held-out CT examinations, FORA achieved a macro area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.918 for radiological concept recognition, exceeding the strongest of 18 existing CT models by 0.21. FORA also ranked first on three public benchmarks, outperforming Merlin on its own test set without training on that institution's data (AUROC 0.907 versus 0.826). On independent localization benchmarks for pulmonary emboli, renal masses and rib fractures, FORA's strongest spatial evidence fell within annotated lesions at rates up to 348 times greater than chance. FORA achieved more than threefold higher macro-averaged finding recall than the strongest existing report generator. These results show that a single model can combine broad abnormality recognition, spatial localization and report generation across 12 CT examination types, using routine reports without lesion-level supervision.
  </details>

- **[Are We Rewarding the Wrong Module? Module-Specific Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32687)**  `arXiv:2611.32687`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _HuiSeong Cho, Jun Yeong Park, Yu Rang Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has been widely adopted to enhance the multimodal reasoning of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing methods share a common limitation of Module-Level Credit Entanglement, in which the vision encoder, projector, and LLM modules are jointly optimized using the same advantage signals even when perceptual correctness and final-answer correctness differ. To address this limitation, we propose Module-Specific Policy Optimization (MSPO), a framework that separates perceptual and outcome-based learning signals across model modules. Specifically, MSPO uses responses to perception checklists comprising auxiliary questions about visual information to derive an advantage for training the vision encoder. From these responses, MSPO computes the Confidence-aware Perception Reward (CaPR), using a perception sign obtained by thresholding checklist accuracy to determine reward direction and posterior confidence from a Beta distribution over latent perception accuracy to scale reward magnitude. We then derive the vision advantage through group-relative normalization of CaPR and scale it using main-task success rates. This advantage guides updates to the vision encoder, while the projector and LLM retain outcome-based GRPO updates. Extensive experiments on seven mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that MSPO outperforms existing RL-tuned models on average at both the 3B and 7B scales.
  </details>

- **[Progressive Reliable Supervision for Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32474)**  `arXiv:2611.32474`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jing Li, Zhe Zhang, Huateng Sun, Yuhui Yang, Wanli Xue, Xu Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation (B$^2$-UniDA) aims to adapt a target model to an unlabeled target domain under arbitrary label-space relationships, while neither source data nor source-model parameters are accessible during adaptation. Under such restrictive information access, reliable target supervision is unavailable, while predictions from the black-box source model can be unreliable due to source bias and domain shifts. To address this challenge, we propose Progressive Reliable Supervision (PReS), which progressively establishes and refines reliable target supervision by coordinating complementary knowledge from the black-box source model, foundation models, and the evolving target model. PReS first identifies reliable known samples by exploiting semantic consistency under diffusion-based perturbations and cross-model consensus between the black-box source model and CLIP. It then estimates the sample-wise reliability of the two predictors and adaptively integrates their complementary predictions to construct reliable pseudo labels. The resulting supervision is exploited asymmetrically to encourage confident and discriminative predictions for reliable known samples while maintaining uncertain yet locally consistent predictions for potential unknown samples. As adaptation proceeds, the emerging target-domain knowledge is further leveraged to refine both the pseudo labels and the known/unknown sample partition, forming a closed-loop process in which supervision construction and target adaptation mutually reinforce each other. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets under diverse label-space relationships demonstrate that PReS achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive performance under the restrictive B$^2$-UniDA setting.
  </details>

- **[Describe, Don't Ask: Caption-Driven On-Policy Distillation for Fine-Grained Visual Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32463)**  `arXiv:2611.32463`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haozhan Shen, Liangbo He, Jun Lan, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, Tiancheng Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) often recognize fine-grained details more accurately in a relevant crop than in the full image. Multimodal on-policy distillation (OPD) methods exploit this by transferring the advantage of a crop-view teacher to a full-image student. However, their supervision relies on region-specific questions curated through an elaborate synthesis pipeline, which brings three weaknesses: (1) every region needs a question--answer pair written exclusively for it, locking training into costly QA curation that is hard to scale to unlabeled images; (2) all rollouts of a region answer the same question, so the teacher supervises only the details it asks about, leaving other visual elements in the region unsupervised; and (3) every question presupposes that its target exists, so the student becomes prone to answering even when the target is absent. In this paper, we study how to turn unlabeled images into broad visual supervision through multi-aspect self-description and privileged-view distillation, and propose CaptionOPD, which replaces tailored questions with a fixed set of image-agnostic caption prompts, ranging from general descriptions to focused captions of parts, colors, counts, text, and layout. The rollouts of a region receive different prompts, so the teacher supervises various aspects of the region. Moreover, because caption prompts mask the model to describe what is visible instead of querying a specific target, they avoid presupposing what the region contains. Using the model itself as the crop-view teacher, CaptionOPD requires no questions, no answers, and no larger model annotations: only images and region boxes. Experiments show that, trained on the same images and regions, CaptionOPD outperforms prior OPD methods across multiple fine-grained and general perception benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[NRG-Twins: Pixel-Level Ground Truth for Rigidity Violations in Generated Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32431)**  `arXiv:2611.32431`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenrui Fan, Qiyang Hu, Luca Scharr, Paolo Favaro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generators now produce realistic clips of scenes and objects, which makes generated video an attractive source of 3D content. Yet generated video is not 3D consistent: static content slowly deforms or slides, 3D reconstruction degrades, and the errors are too subtle to label by hand. To make generators 3D consistent, or to build rewards and guidance that push them there, we need data that say where and by how much a generated video breaks rigidity, and these data must come from generated video itself. We obtain per-pixel ground-truth labels from real-generated twins: a real clip paired with its re-creation by a keyframe-conditioned video generator. After inserting real keyframes so that the twin camera roughly follows the real one, we measure per pixel, in degrees, how far the twin departs from a rigid scene seen along its own camera path. We release Non-Rigidly Generated Twins (NRG-Twins), 871 twins, the code that builds them for new generators, and a benchmark with an official test split. A detector trained on these data, the Non-Rigidly Generated Pixel Detector (NRG-PD), predicts the per-pixel angle from RGB alone. It separates inconsistent from consistent pixels with AUROC 0.85, against 0.40-0.74 for seven existing consistency and motion-segmentation methods, and its predictions improve 3DGS of video that contains generated content.
  </details>

- **[Part-Set Prototypes for Explainable Mountain Gazelles Sex Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32343)**  `arXiv:2611.32343`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Tali Boneh Shitrit, Amir Kedem, Ilan Shimshoni, Anna Zamansky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-explainable visual models for fine-grained recognition are often hindered by rigid geometric constraints that cannot capture diverse semantic shapes in unconstrained "in-the-wild" imagery. Real-world camera-trap data introduces challenges, including non-optimal poses, lighting variations, occlusions, animal camouflage, and environmental shortcuts. To address this, we introduce set prototypes, a general architecture where both part cardinality and unconstrained spatial layouts are mined directly from data. Our joint-matching formulation places parts simultaneously on a frozen DINOv3 token grid via truncated Sinkhorn competition, allowing unsupported parts to abstain without spatial collapse. We evaluate our approach on Mountain Gazelle sex classification, a camera-trap benchmark, where our model achieves 0.878 macro-F1--outperforming prior architectures and at par with an unconstrained linear probe. The strongest prototypes localize to distinctive biological areas such as horns, neck and body, allowing ecologists to visually verify decisions before trusting the metric. This formulation generalizes naturally to diverse ecological monitoring challenges beyond a single species.
  </details>

- **[SolarFilNet-V4: Multi-Scale Transformer Vision with Physics-Informed Adaptive Post-Processing for Solar Filament Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32193)**  `arXiv:2611.32193`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Indra Agustian, Ruvita Faurina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Solar filaments are cool, dense chromospheric plasma structures held by magnetic field lines above polarity inversion lines. Their destabilization triggers coronal mass ejections and severe space weather hazards. Segmenting individual filaments from ground-based GONG H-alpha observations (2048 x 2048 pixels) is challenging due to low contrast, turbulent fibrils, delicate barb structures, and annotator boundary variance. In this technical report, we describe SolarFilNet-V4, a targeted segmentation framework developed for the MAGFiLO 1.0 benchmark (Solar Filament Segmentation Challenge 2026). Our method couples a lightweight Pyramid Vision Transformer (PVT-v2-B1) backbone with anisotropic curvilinear strip convolutions (k=15) and dual-frequency feed-forward blocks (DFFN) to follow extended flux ropes while capturing thin central spines. A shallow full-resolution convolutional branch operating at 1024 x 1024 preserves high-frequency edge details and mitigates transformer downsampling. We train the network using multi-task supervision over masks, centerline spines, barb boundaries, and pixel-contrastive representations. At test time, we apply 8-fold dihedral test-time augmentation (D4), bilinearly upscale predictions to the native 2048 x 2048 grid, and apply an activity-adaptive post-processing routine that calibrates morphological joining parameters for quiet versus active solar disks. On the official benchmark, SolarFilNet-V4 achieves an out-of-fold validation Panoptic Quality of 0.4340 and a Public Leaderboard score of 0.380. The model contains 15.46M parameters and requires 224.34 GFLOPs per forward pass, reducing parameter count by 36.8% compared to standard ResNet-34 U-Net baselines.
  </details>

- **[DocPristine: Content-Aware Document Appearance Enhancement under Real-World Mixed Degradations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32176)**  `arXiv:2611.32176`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihan Huang, Junle Liu, Zhonghao Ye, Yuyi Zhang, Peirong Zhang, Hanlin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Camera-captured documents often exhibit mixed degradations, such as highlights, shadows, and blur, that distort backgrounds and obscure text details. Document appearance enhancement (DAE) aims to mitigate these degradations and improve readability. Existing document appearance enhancement methods rely either on input-derived visual cues that can become unreliable under severe degradation or on LLM-based degradation diagnosis that lacks spatial guidance. Notably, we find that pretrained document visual encoders continue to attend to text regions in degraded documents, providing content-aware guidance. Based on this insight, we propose DocPristine, which adaptively fuses multi-layer features from a frozen document visual encoder and injects them into a pixel restoration network. To integrate these features into a pixel restoration network, DocPristine combines Adaptive Multi-Layer Feature Fusion (AMFF), Hierarchical Gated Conditioning (HGC), and Shifted-Window Cross-Attention (SWCA). Specifically, SWCA draws on structural cues from neighboring locations across window boundaries to recover fine textthis http URLalso introduce DocACE, with a synthetic mixed-degradation training set (DocACE-Synthetic) and a real-world test set (DocACE-Real) containing manually restored ground-truth images and document parsing annotations. Comprehensive experiments across three benchmarks demonstrate that DocPristine achieves state-of-the-art appearance enhancement quality and consistently improves downstream document parsing. Code, models, and data will be publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Mind the Band: The Hidden Frequency Variable in Geometric RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32171)**  `arXiv:2611.32171`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Seonghyun Jin, Youngmin Kim, Jong Chul Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometric rotary position encodings (RoPE) incorporate 3D camera geometry into multi-view attention by rotating token features according to viewing rays, depths, and reprojected image coordinates. Each coordinate is rotated across a progression of frequencies--its frequency band--yet existing implementations leave this band to inherited defaults, either retaining standard 2D RoPE's top frequency or letting the frequency span grow exponentially with the attention head dimension. We show that this unstandardized setting can reverse benchmark rankings. In novel view synthesis on RealEstate10K, RayRoPE matches the strongest baseline in the 47M model (144-channel heads), yet trails by 1.2 dB in the 25M model (48-channel heads), and exhibits the same reversal when varying head size alone at fixed model width. Populating a 243x range with four frequencies instead of the 3x default at 48 channels recovers 0.9 dB of that gap. Across view synthesis and two-view depth estimation, overly narrow bands consistently blur correspondence, whereas all three projection-based encodings perform reliably when their top frequency lies within 0.3--3 rotations per 8-pixel patch (10--90 rotations across 256x256 RealEstate10K). At the opposite extreme, overly wide bands degrade RayRoPE through a forward-backward asymmetry: averaging rotations over a predicted depth interval damps high frequencies in the forward pass, yet leaves their gradients with respect to predicted depth undamped. Blocking only those high-frequency depth gradients recovers 90--93% of the lost PSNR, showing that forward smoothing does not guarantee backward stability. Guided by this analysis, we distill practical guidelines--setting bands in image coordinates, populating spans in small heads, and avoiding unconditional same-view damping--and advocate reporting and matching frequency bands in future multi-view benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[An Automated Framework Integrating Deep Learning and Anatomical Reasoning for Cardiac Phenotyping From CCTA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32114)**  `arXiv:2611.32114`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hien Minh Tran, Thi-Thu-Hien Pham, Le Duy Tan, Hieu Xuan Nguyen, Tuong-Anh Mai-Phan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coronary computed tomography angiography (CCTA) depicts coronary anatomy and cardiac fat, but segmentation alone does not ensure anatomically consistent measurements. We propose an automated framework that combines deep learning with anatomical reasoning for quantitative cardiac phenotyping. Its anatomical measurement module uses graph geometry, branch identity, and surrounding structures to define vessel-specific pericoronary adipose tissue (PCAT) sampling and a surrogate epicardial adipose tissue (EAT) compartment. The latter also generates training targets without requiring expert pericardium contours. A companion module quantifies coronary calcium on non-contrast CT. Component-wise benchmarking was conducted on five public datasets: ImageCAS, ImageCAS-X, COCA, Visual Lab, and MultiD4CAD. On ImageCAS-X Test-160, lumen segmentation with validation-selected post-processing achieved a Dice score of 0.9133 and a clDice score of 0.940, exceeding the corresponding published CAS-Net point estimates. In the four-split ImageCAS evaluation, the four-member ensemble outperformed the single ResEnc-L model. Analysis of 800 paired scans showed that PCAT attenuation measurements differed between whole-vessel and lumen references. External EAT evaluation showed strong volume correlation and good spatial overlap. Explicit anatomical rules make the resulting measurements interpretable and inspectable. In practice, the framework provides a standardized pipeline for processing cardiac CT cohorts and quantifying coronary calcium, PCAT, and EAT for population-level analysis and comparison across studies. The source code is publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Clinical3D-Bench: Evaluating and Improving Spatial Intelligence of Multimodal LLMs in Hospital Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32094)**  `arXiv:2611.32094`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Bai, Yingzhi Xia, Ling Lin, Jun Cheng, Lin Fu, Huazhu Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning in hospitals requires tracking look-alike equipment, interpreting patient-relative directions, and judging passage widths, yet existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of these abilities. We introduce Clinical3D-Bench, a simulated benchmark with 3,368 questions of eight types on egocentric walkthroughs of 373 hospital-room layouts. Layouts follow hospital design guidelines, answers are computed from the 3D scenes, and paired scene states test whether models change their answers when relevant evidence changes. Videos are photorealistic, and ten physicians screened the layouts. Among 35 multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the best open and proprietary models reach 45.7% and 53.6% macro accuracy, against 40.8% for an answer prior and 79.0% for physicians on a subset, and 27 of 32 open models score below 40% on two-way patient-relative directions. Fine-tuning on Clinical3D-45K, our 44,750-question training set, lifts two MLLMs 16-20 points above the best proprietary model. 3D maps given to the fine-tuned MLLM as input go largely unused: replacing them with another video's, even when they are ground truth, barely changes its accuracy.
