The proposed GeoJeV instead lets the MLLM compute with 3D: it reconstructs a scene memory from each video, selects 3D operations and their arguments from the MLLM's question state, and adds their evidence to its option scores. It achieves the highest accuracy of all evaluated models, narrowing the gap to physicians.
  </details>

- **[Do Occupancy World Models Really Follow Actions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32029)**  `arXiv:2611.32029`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jongseong Bae, Ha Young Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Occupancy world models predict future 3D scenes conditioned on ego actions, yet their predictions may not faithfully follow the specified trajectory under counterfactual actions. Because driving datasets provide sparse coverage of alternative trajectories for each scene, existing models learn the spatial effect of ego motion implicitly from observed scene--trajectory combinations, which may entangle action effects with scene--trajectory correlations. We propose ReCast, an ego-trajectory-guided transformer that grounds future prediction in action-induced geometric correspondence across ego-centric frames. ReCast decouples historical encoding from future decoding and aligns deformable interactions using relative ego transformations, while retaining flexibility for non-rigid scene evolution. We also introduce a counterfactual action-faithfulness benchmark covering in-distribution, tail, and out-of-distribution trajectories. On Occ3D-nuScenes, ReCast achieves competitive standard forecasting performance while improving counterfactual forecasting, with particularly clear gains under out-of-distribution trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Before-and-After Images: Evaluating Temporal Grounding in a Lightweight VLM for Disaster Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32022)**  `arXiv:2611.32022`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shubh Garg, Pratham Arora, Debabrata Ghosh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bi-temporal satellite imagery is central to disaster assessment: deciding what was damaged requires comparing a pre-event scene with its post-event counterpart. A vision-language model (VLM) can nonetheless appear to answer change questions while relying mainly on the post-event image or on the prompt's temporal framing. We present a controlled diagnostic of temporal grounding and apply it to one lightweight VLM, InternVL3-2B, on DisasterM3: 1,659 optical and SAR image pairs from 41 events and nine disaster types, in event-disjoint partitions, with 9,031 reasoning questions. We compare post-only input, the true pre/post pair, the post image repeated twice, reversed image order, and an unrelated pre-event image. The true pair scores $+0.5$ accuracy points above the repeated-post control (95\% paired bootstrap CI $[-0.2, +1.2]$), and reversing the order changes accuracy by $-0.2$ points ($[-0.8, +0.5]$). Under this protocol we therefore find no measurable benefit from the content of the true pre-event image and little sensitivity to temporal order. The model is not blind to the first image, however: the true pair scores $2.5$ points above a pair whose pre-event image comes from another event ($[+1.6, +3.4]$). State-transition and multi-hop questions remain hard, and optical and SAR inputs show different profiles. We outline temporal scene graphs as a testable next step, not a validated solution, and argue that temporal benchmarks for Earth observation should report whether answers depend on the intended evidence, not only how often they are right.
  </details>

- **[Your VLM Knows What to Inspect: Semantic Probe-Guided Adaptive Focusing for GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31909)**  `arXiv:2611.31909`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruixiao Shi, Fu Feng, Yucheng Xie, Jing Wang, Xin Geng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding requires resolving fine visual details within the context that gives them meaning. Additional views can improve localization, but their value depends on what evidence they expose and how much context they preserve. We explore a capability of vision-language models (VLMs): even when their coordinate predictions are incorrect, they can describe visual evidence worth inspecting. We introduce \textbf{SPARK}, \textit{\textbf{S}emantic \textbf{P}robe-guided \textbf{A}daptive \textbf{R}egion see\textbf{K}ing}, a training-free framework that turns this semantic guidance into targeted visual observations. Exploration probes describe relevant context and distinguishing target details, while verification probes identify evidence needed to assess emerging hypotheses. Their spatial attention guides view placement, contextual responses determine crop extent, and observation history encourages complementary inspection. The resulting predictions are combined through uncertainty-weighted voting. On ScreenSpot-Pro, SPARK improves grounding accuracy over single-view inference by 13.9, 25.2, and 16.1 percentage points with Qwen3-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, and UI-TARS, outperforming all compared test-time methods for each backbone. Consistent gains across four additional benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness across diverse interfaces and grounding tasks. These results establish semantic probing as an effective mechanism for turning a frozen VLM's understanding of what to inspect into more accurate GUI grounding.
  </details>

- **[Loupe: Reinforcing Knowledge-Intensive Video Generation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31895)**  `arXiv:2611.31895`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kai Zeng, Ruichuan An, Zhen Fang, Xi Fang, Han Lyu, Moran Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge-intensive video generation remains challenging because prompts often omit the facts, appearances, and temporal relationships required for accurate generation. While search-based workflows can retrieve missing evidence, manually designed strategies limit adaptive planning, and conventional training suffers from costly video-level feedback and coarse trajectory-level credit assignment. In this paper, we present Loupe, a video generation agent that retrieves and verifies textual and visual evidence to construct evidence-grounded temporal video prompts with reference images. We further introduce Rubric-Based Credit (RBC), which leverages prompt-specific factual, visual, and temporal requirements to evaluate intermediate search and planning decisions before video rendering. Building on RBC, RBC-SFT reweights assistant-token learning by step quality, while RBC-GRPO incorporates step-level credits alongside a final planning reward, without rendering videos during RL. To address the lack of dedicated search-oriented evaluation, we construct Search-VBench, which contains 150 held-out prompts across seven categories. Experiments show that Loupe improves all four evaluation dimensions across three video generators, raising the overall score of MiniMax-H3 from 32.80 to 70.67 and outperforming an untrained search workflow. Ablation experiments show that full RBC training outperforms standard SFT followed by trajectory-level GRPO by 5.27 points. Human evaluations and results on Sci-VBench and KIVI-Bench further demonstrate improved generation quality, scientific grounding, factual precision, and helpfulness. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Gaze World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31836)**  `arXiv:2611.31836`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawen Zhang, Houze Yang, Xu Cao, Yifan Shen, Zhongyi Zhou, Steve Wong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Where a person looks indicates their focus of attention, and how their gaze shifts between targets reveals how that attention responds to a changing scene. Existing gaze target estimation methods largely operate frame by frame and do not model these shifts. Gaze shifts are often driven by dynamic changes in the scene, which is the kind of information world models are designed to encode. This relationship is subtle, however, since an event far across the frame may produce only a slight movement of the person's head or eyes. We therefore introduce the Gaze World Model (GWM), which co-trains latent scene prediction with person-conditioned gaze supervision on a frozen V-JEPA 2.1 encoder; in our ablations, the latent prediction objective improves both shift detection and gaze localization. Given a video clip and the person's head box, GWM estimates gaze locations and detects gaze shifts in the observed clip, and forecasts future gaze and shifts from predicted scene features. To train and evaluate these tasks, we augment VideoAttentionTarget and ChildPlay with event-level gaze-shift annotations. GWM achieves state-of-the-art shift detection on these benchmarks, about 10 points higher in four-class AP than the strongest baseline, while remaining comparable to the strongest image-based models in gaze target estimation, and outperforms the compared baselines on future gaze localization and shift forecasting.
  </details>

- **[VISOR: A Vision-Language Selector for Multimodal Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31780)**  `arXiv:2611.31780`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Seunghan Lee, Jun Seo, Junhyeok Kang, Jaehoon Lee, Sangjun Han, Sungdong Yoo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent time-series foundation models (TSFMs) forecast a distribution over futures, and multimodal time-series (TS) forecasting further exploits other modalities to improve forecasting accuracy. However, existing works have two limitations: 1) TSFMs reduce the predictive distribution to its median or mean, flattening diverse futures into a calm line, and 2) multimodal TS forecasting models incorporate text into the forecaster through TS-language alignment, which is substantially weaker than vision-language alignment. To address these limitations, we propose the VIsion-language SelectOR (VISOR), a multimodal TS forecasting framework that selects one plausible future instead of averaging across candidates, leveraging the alignment capabilities of vision-language models (VLMs) to select the most suitable candidate based on textual information. VISOR consists of two components: 1) a text-free stochastic predictor that samples diverse candidate futures, and 2) a VLM selector that reads the candidates as a single chart image and selects one based on the text. To avoid overly calm forecasting paths, we train the selector with a shape-aware target that evaluates each candidate based on both point-wise error and path shape. Across three benchmarks covering ten datasets, VISOR achieves the best average ranking in both point and volatility errors compared with TSFMs and multimodal TS forecasting models. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[RVLM-X: Towards a Universal VLM Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31755)**  `arXiv:2611.31755`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Aadi Palnitkar, Dmytro Kurdydyk, Xiaomin Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harnesses for vision-language models (VLMs) have grown with the models' capabilities, notably for images far larger than those seen in training, where downscaling loses small details. Most of them wrap the VLM in a fixed procedure, such as a zoom tree, that decides in advance where it looks and how the evidence is combined. These procedures make up for the weaknesses of older models by keeping them on a task-specific path, but with frontier models that reliably plan and use tools, they can lower accuracy. We propose RVLM-X, a task-generalizable recursive VLM harness that runs unchanged across photographs, charts and maps. Following the recursive inference of Recursive Language Models, the VLM programmatically controls its own visual context in a code REPL that holds the full-resolution image and calls itself recursively on regions that it chooses. With a cost-efficient frontier model as the base of every method, RVLM-X is more accurate than a single call on all 7 public benchmarks and has the highest pooled accuracy. Because existing benchmarks mostly test whether a model finds one small detail, we also introduce MapGenBench, procedurally generated maps whose questions combine fine-detail perception with multi-step reasoning. There, RVLM-X answers 71.4% of held-out questions against 55.5% for the strongest baseline. Overall, our findings suggest that as VLMs plan and use tools more reliably, letting the model write its own visual search works better than fixing one in advance.
  </details>

- **[GRUN: Generalized Retinex Unfolding Network for Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31690)**  `arXiv:2611.31690`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Rihan Zhang, Fengyang Xiao, Yuhan Chen, Chunming He, Sina Farsiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image restoration remains a fundamental challenge in low-level vision, requiring methods that handle diverse degradations while preserving scene structures and details. We revisit classical Retinex theory and propose a Generalized Retinex formulation that extends the illumination-reflectance decomposition into a Retinex-inspired content-environment factorization prior for image restoration. The key idea is to restore images using two learned factors: a content-dominant response (generalized reflectance) capturing scene properties and an environment-side response (generalized illumination) encoding task-dependent variations associated with the observed degradations. Based on this formulation, we develop GRUN (Generalized Retinex Unfolding Network), a MAP-guided deep unfolding framework that alternates reflectance restoration, illumination estimation, and progressive degradation inference across K stages with degradation-conditioned proximal operators. We further introduce a Degradation-aware Coupling Regularizer (DCR) that combines mutual information minimization, gradient decorrelation, and scale anchoring to promote complementary content-environment factor representations. Experiments on seven image restoration tasks, including denoising, dehazing, deraining, low-light enhancement, deblurring, underwater enhancement, and backlit enhancement, demonstrate competitive results on multiple benchmarks using the same architecture with task-specific weights. Moreover, the proposed Generalized Retinex design can be integrated into existing restoration methods, demonstrating its cross-architecture applicability. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[AdProducer: Towards Agentic Advertising Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31466)**  `arXiv:2611.31466`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kunyang Li, Chaoyang Wang, Mubarak Shah, Yuzhang Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in video generation have made automated advertising production increasingly feasible. However, an effective advertisement must faithfully represent the product and communicate its selling points through compelling visual storytelling, which requires more than visual synthesis: it calls for deeper product understanding and strategic decisions about how to present product value. Crucially, product research and creative design are closely intertwined, as the information gathered about a product determines which features can be faithfully demonstrated and how they should be presented. In this paper, we introduce AdProducer, the first end-to-end agentic framework for advertising video creation, transforming concise creative briefs into finished advertisements. Our multimodal agent autonomously researches products through web and image search and translates its findings into selling-point-driven storyboards that guide a frozen video generator. To achieve this, we develop a dedicated data construction pipeline that yields two training datasets, AdProducer-SFT and AdProducer-RL, and train the agent through supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning with joint optimization of storyboard- and video-level rewards. We further introduce AdCreation-Bench, comprising 305 advertising briefs across eight domains, with two complementary metrics: PG-Score for product correctness and creative execution, and Ad-Score for overall advertising effectiveness assessed by an independent judge. Experiments demonstrate that AdProducer improves the PG-Score of MiniMax H3 from 17.3 to 51.3 and generalizes to Veo 3.1 without additional training, improving its score from 28.0 to 35.9, with consistent gains in Ad-Score, VideoScore2, and human evaluations. Code, models, and data are released.
  </details>

- **[Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation: Pixel as Code for Agentic Visual Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31358)**  `arXiv:2611.31358`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Guo Chen, Fuxiao Liu, Haoqin Tu, Yiyang Zhou, Andrew Tao, Bryan Catanzaro, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual creation demands expressive imagery together with precise control over text, layout, and structure. We introduce Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation, an agentic framework that connects these capabilities through Pixel as Code. Raster images are represented by learned discrete visual tokens, while structured artifacts use explicit programs. A shared generation-and-rendering interface connects these representations to visual feedback across images, vector graphics, webpages, and documents. A Nemotron-native harness coordinates planning and raster candidate selection, with targeted repair for editable structured and hybrid artifacts. To support this interface, we develop Nemotron-Labs-Image-Tokenizer, the autoregressive raster generator Nemotron-Labs-Image-30B-A3B, and the structured-code model family Nemotron-Labs-VisionCode. On approximately 178K images spanning six visual domains, our tokenizer improves reconstruction PSNR from 26.08 to 29.57 dB over Emu3.5 IBQ. It also provides 7.13x faster encoding and 10.93x faster decoding at batch size one on A100. In standalone evaluation, one Nemotron-Labs-VisionCode variant surpasses Kimi K3.1 Pro on the aggregate across Chart2Code, MMSVG, Design2Code, and Image2Struct. At the system level, the Nemotron-native configuration raises GenEval2 Soft-TIFA GM from 31.71 to 52.56 using the same raster generator. With our best setting, Nemotron-Labs-VisualCreation achieves performance competitive with GPT Image 2 and Nano Banana Pro across most benchmarks with available comparisons.
  </details>

- **[PixelOne: From Options to Pixels in a Single Forward for GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30235)**  `arXiv:2611.30235`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kanghui Tian, Shuai Dong, Yuhan Zhu, Congcong Wang, Ji Zhao, Tianxiang Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. However, screenshot downsampling removes detail needed to locate small controls, while fixed bins cover larger pixel ranges at higher resolutions. We introduce PixelOne, which transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student that predicts clicks directly from screenshots at native resolution. The teacher uses VideoJEV's text-option scorer to select rows and columns and progressively zoom into candidate regions. The student inherits the teacher's parameters and matches its distributions over explored regions through spatial distillation alongside box supervision. At inference, the same decision head selects a visual token, and a lightweight pixel head predicts the click within it, all in one forward pass. A click-aligned loss encourages pixels inside the target box to score above those outside. Across four benchmarks, PixelOne-Pro-35B-A3B achieves 83.8% mean accuracy, the highest among the evaluated single-forward grounders, with a mean latency of 0.43 s per target.
  </details>

- **[Seeing vs. Knowing: Counterfactual Supervision and Correction for 3D-Aware Object Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29542)**  `arXiv:2611.29542`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jian Zahng, Zhijun Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rotating, translating, or rescaling an object in a photograph requires revealing unseen surfaces, preserving visible appearance, and updating shadows and reflections. Reconstructed 3D assets, rendered at the target pose as a 3D state, provide explicit geometric guidance, but their imperfect textures may conflict with the photograph, while standard paired training does not explicitly teach the editor which source to trust. To address this, we propose counterfactual state supervision, which constructs controlled photo--state conflicts through hidden-surface repainting, hidden geometry with re-rendered shadows and reflections, and visible-surface texture replacement. This teaches the editor to follow the 3D state for unseen content while preserving photograph-visible appearance. However, learning this distinction does not ensure that it is maintained during sampling. We therefore introduce symmetric counterfactual correction, which uses state-side and photo-side twins, which share the photograph and the state respectively, to construct directed latent perturbations toward condition-inconsistent targets and train recovery toward the target specified by the current condition. Both strategies operate only during training, requiring no architectural changes or additional inference steps. Experiments on OBJect and SpatialEdit-Bench demonstrate strong spatial editing performance and content preservation, while controlled ablations show improved source selection and sustained responsiveness to conditioning during sampling.
  </details>

- **[VMSA: Video Memory Skill Agent for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27381)**  `arXiv:2611.27381`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Changin Choi, Jungwon Park, Jungmin Ko, Jimyeong Kim, Wonjong Rhee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video question answering requires retrieving and integrating relevant evidence from videos spanning hours or even days. Recent agentic approaches organize video information into external memories and iteratively retrieve evidence to answer questions. However, effective retrieval and reasoning remain challenging, as agents must determine what information to search for, which memory sources to access, and how to connect evidence across distant temporal intervals. To address these challenges, we propose Video Memory Skill Agent (VMSA), a framework that jointly designs structured video memories and memory skills for long-video understanding. VMSA constructs three complementary memory components. Segment Caption Memory and Entity Event Memory serve as searchable evidence sources, while Entity-Aware Episode Memory provides broad yet detailed video context to guide evidence retrieval and reasoning. To further support effective memory utilization, VMSA induces memory skills from successful agent trajectories, incrementally refining or extending a compact skill library to guide iterative evidence retrieval, reasoning, and verification. Across seven long-video question-answering benchmarks, VMSA achieves the highest accuracy on all three week-long egocentric benchmarks, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 9.4% on Ego-R1 Bench, and the best or competitive performance on four additional benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of all three memory components and memory skills, with memory skills also improving temporal evidence localization.
  </details>

- **[dKV Forcing: Separating Generation and Memory Representations through a Differentiable KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01648)**  `arXiv:2611.01648`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqi Li, Bo Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A causal video generator writes every segment it produces into a key--value (KV) cache, and later segments know the past only through it. Self-rollout training stops the gradient at this cache, and Self Gradient Forcing (SGF) restores it, but applies it to the same weights that generate the current segment. We find that writing and generating are different roles: in a pretrained generator the written KV already differs from the KV used for denoising, and on shared weights the gradient from future reads is negatively aligned with the gradient of generation. We propose dKV Forcing (dKV denotes differentiable KV), which gives the write its own parameters, a low-rank residual used only when a segment enters the cache, and trains them with the gradient of the later segments that read what they wrote. Opening this gradient through the cache in common use exhausts GPU memory. We trace this to checkpoint replay re-writing the cache, not to the gradient path, which removes the need for the second reconstruction pass of SGF. dKV Forcing keeps the cache as persistent differentiable pages and computes the exact gradient in one temporal traversal, with an $11\%$ lower memory peak and $28\%$ less resident memory. With pages that store hidden states, it trains 15\,s clips on which the two-pass reconstruction runs out of memory. Trained on 5\,s clips, dKV Forcing reaches the highest VBench-Long quality score at 60\,s and 240\,s and outperforms SGF by $3.6$ and $6.1$ points of aesthetic quality, with a higher dynamic degree.
  </details>

- **[One Evaluation Is Enough: Inversion-Free Image Editing with Trajectory Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30827)**  `arXiv:2610.30827`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Lifan Jiang, Taiyang Chen, Senlin cheng, Bolei Xu, Xiaobo Li, Can Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-image editing commonly relies on inversion and iterative sampling, while efficient one-step editors often stabilize a large update by estimating and averaging editing fields at multiple noise levels. We ask a stricter question: is a single temporal field evaluation sufficient for reliable inversion-free editing? Our answer rests on a previously underused duality in a variance-preserving (VP) one-step model: one source-target query at a shared noisy state simultaneously determines a local angular semantic tangent and a clean endpoint displacement. We integrate the tangent over the analytically known remaining VP angle to obtain a nominal one-step transport, and use the endpoint displacement to correct its long-horizon extrapolation through a constrained projection. This transport-first, correction-second construction yields Endpoint-Calibrated Transport (ECT), a closed-form, training-free editor requiring only one noise level, two text conditions packed into one denoiser batch, and one latent update. ECT requires no image inversion, trajectory integration, second temporal observation, or iterative optimization, and can be directly applied to pretrained epsilon-prediction VP one-step generators. Across the reported benchmark settings, ECT delivers stable gains in editing quality within an extremely short editing time.
  </details>

- **[SIEVES: Selective Prediction Generalizes through Visual Evidence Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25855)**  `arXiv:2604.25855`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hector G. Rodriguez, Marcus Rohrbach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As the accuracy of Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on traditional visual question answering benchmarks approaches saturation, reliable deployment requires satisfying low error tolerances in real-world out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. Selective prediction aims to improve coverage, i.e. the share of inputs the system answers, while adhering to a user-defined risk level. This is typically achieved by assigning a confidence score to each answer and abstaining on those that fall below a certain threshold. Existing selective prediction methods rely on text-only reasoning and learn to predict confidences simply based on whether the answer was correct or not. In contrast, we introduce SIEVES (Selective Prediction through Visual Evidence Scoring), which first requires the answering model to provide visual evidence supporting its answer, by returning crops of the image alongside their textual reasoning and answer. Second, a lightweight selector is trained to predict the quality of this evidence, assessing how well the provided crops localize the relevant visual elements and if the final answer is coherent with this evidence. We show that SIEVES improves coverage by up to six times on challenging OOD benchmarks (V* Bench, HR-Bench-8k, MME-RealWorld-Lite, VizWiz, and AdVQA), compared to non-grounding baselines. Beyond better generalization to OOD tasks, the design of the SIEVES selector enables transfer to proprietary reasoners without access to their weights or logits, such as o3 and Gemini-3-Pro, providing coverage boosts beyond those attributable to accuracy alone. We highlight that SIEVES generalizes across all tested OOD datasets and reasoner models, without benchmark- or reasoner-specific training or adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Think, Act, Build: An Agentic Framework with Vision Language Models for Zero-Shot 3D Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00528)**  `arXiv:2604.00528`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haibo Wang, Zihao Lin, Zhiyang Xu, Lifu Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Visual Grounding (3D-VG) aims to localize objects in 3D scenes via natural language descriptions. While recent advancements leveraging Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have explored zero-shot possibilities, they typically suffer from a static workflow relying on preprocessed 3D point clouds, essentially degrading grounding into proposal matching. To bypass this reliance, our core motivation is to decouple the task: leveraging 2D VLMs to resolve complex spatial semantics, while relying on deterministic multi-view geometry to instantiate the 3D structure. Driven by this insight, we propose "Think, Act, Build (TAB)", a dynamic agentic framework that reformulates 3D-VG tasks as a generative 2D-to-3D reconstruction paradigm operating directly on raw RGB-D streams. Specifically, guided by a specialized 3D-VG skill, our VLM agent dynamically invokes visual tools to track and reconstruct the target across 2D frames. Crucially, to overcome the multi-view coverage deficit caused by strict VLM semantic tracking, we introduce the Semantic-Anchored Geometric Expansion, a mechanism that first anchors the target in a reference video clip and then leverages multi-view geometry to propagate its spatial location across unobserved frames. This enables the agent to "Build" the target's 3D representation by aggregating these multi-view features via camera parameters, directly mapping 2D visual cues to 3D coordinates. Furthermore, to ensure rigorous assessment, we identify flaws such as reference ambiguity and category errors in existing benchmarks and manually refine the incorrect queries. Extensive experiments on ScanRefer and Nr3D demonstrate that our framework, relying entirely on open-source models, significantly outperforms previous zero-shot methods and even surpasses fully supervised baselines.
  </details>

- **[Taming Hallucinations: Boosting MLLMs' Video Understanding via Counterfactual Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24271)**  `arXiv:2512.24271`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhe Huang, Hao Wen, Aiming Hao, Bingze Song, Meiqi Wu, Jiahong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made remarkable progress in video understanding. However, they suffer from a critical vulnerability: an over-reliance on language priors, which can lead to visual ungrounded hallucinations, especially when processing counterfactual videos that defy common sense. This limitation, stemming from the intrinsic data imbalance between text and video, is challenging to address due to the substantial cost of collecting and annotating counterfactual data. To address this, we introduce DualityForge, a novel counterfactual data synthesis framework that employs controllable, diffusion-based video editing to transform real-world videos into counterfactual scenarios. By embedding structured contextual information into the video editing and QA generation processes, the framework automatically produces high-quality QA pairs together with original-edited video pairs for contrastive training. Based on this, we build DualityVidQA, a large-scale video dataset designed to reduce MLLM hallucinations. In addition, to fully exploit the contrastive nature of our paired data, we propose Duality-Normalized Advantage Training (DNA-Train), a two-stage SFT-RL training regime where the RL phase applies pair-wise $\ell_1$ advantage normalization, thereby enabling a more stable and efficient policy optimization. Experiments on DualityVidQA-Test demonstrate that our method substantially reduces model hallucinations on counterfactual videos, yielding a relative improvement of 24.0% over the Qwen2.5-VL-7B baseline. Moreover, our approach achieves significant gains across both hallucination and general-purpose benchmarks, indicating strong generalization capability. We will open-source our dataset and code.
  </details>

- **[One Take, Many Cameras: Audio-Grounded Shot Synthesis for Dialogue Re-Cinematography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33147)**  `arXiv:2611.33147`  `cs.CV`  
  _Anthony Chen, Naomi Ken Korem, Matan Ben-Yosef, Tavi Halperin, Urska Jelercic, Ofir Bibi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Creating a dialogue scene typically requires cutting between multiple views (e.g., close-ups, medium shots, and over-the-shoulder shots) of the actors. Yet capturing such coverage is costly and in many settings unavailable. We study dialogue re-cinematography: synthesizing additional shots from a single wide-angle dialogue video. Existing video generators can synthesize new shots, but rely on visual cues alone, so when the source video barely shows the lips, the generated lip motion fails to match the speech. Our key observation is that the speech audio is view-invariant: it constrains lip motion in any target view. Building on this insight, we adapt a pretrained audio-visual diffusion model to generate the new shot from a target frame that specifies the view, a source video that contains the performance, and the speech audio that drives lip motion. In multi-person scenes, however, pretrained audio-visual models drive every visible face with the same speech, causing speech leakage, where silent listeners mouth the speaker's words. We therefore introduce speaker-aware audio gating, a training-free module that lets the audio drive only the faces that speak in the target shot. On a cinematic paired-view benchmark spanning diverse viewpoints and framings, our method lowers perceptual error by 59%, landmark error by 56%, and speech leakage by 97% relative to the strongest baseline, Wan 3.0. Our method also remains robust on in-the-wild scenes, highlighting its potential for film post-production.
  </details>

- **[Lost on Campus: Evaluating Embodied Spatial Reasoning of Vision-Language Models in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33145)**  `arXiv:2611.33145`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zehan Zheng, Yanyuan Chen, Deming Li, Yutao Tang, Jianwen Xie, Alan Yuille, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied Spatial Reasoning (ESR) is central to deploying Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in real-world embodied tasks, yet remains inadequately evaluated. Existing benchmarks primarily assess spatial reasoning in static images or synthetic indoor environments, where semantic shortcuts often suffice, making it difficult to faithfully evaluate spatial reasoning capabilities in the wild. In light of this, we present Lost on Campus, a benchmark for evaluating ESR in large-scale real-world outdoor 3D environments reconstructed by 3D Gaussian Splatting. Our benchmark introduces a unified reasoning-action evaluation framework that seamlessly integrates diagnostic QA for isolated reasoning with closed-loop interactive navigation for active reasoning under multimodal instructions. To enable fine-grained diagnosis, we evaluate six capability aspects of ESR: action grounding, spatial foresight, metric awareness, goal-directed planning, ego-allocentric localization, and spatio-temporal consistency. Extensive experiments reveal that: (i) Compared to indoor settings, real-world outdoor environments impose substantially higher demands on spatial reasoning, where existing models exhibit poor performance; (ii) VLMs still struggle with fine-grained visual alignment, with failures in spatial foresight and ego-allocentric localization emerging as primary bottlenecks; (iii) Improving multimodal interaction capabilities and long-term spatial reasoning is crucial for advancing embodied intelligence. These findings highlight that faithful evaluation of ESR demands benchmarks tightly coupling perception, reasoning and action within realistic and continuous environments.
  </details>

- **[Physicl Scenes: An Artist-created 3D Dataset for Learning Perception and Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33137)**  `arXiv:2611.33137`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yueh-Cheng Liu, Snehal Jauhri, Matthias Nießner, Angela Dai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Physicl Scenes, a high-quality artist-created and curated dataset of 6,499 3D scenes spanning household rooms, public interiors, and outdoor spaces. The dataset combines detailed geometry, physically based materials, and carefully composed environments with object- and part-level semantic annotations, providing rich, joint supervision for visual and embodied learning. We demonstrate its value as training data across inverse rendering, 3D reconstruction, scene understanding, and mobile manipulation. Across these tasks, training state-of-the-art methods with our scenes improves performance over alternative synthetic datasets. In addition, we introduce a mobile manipulation benchmark, evaluating vision-language-action models performing physical pick and place in diverse 3D scenes with realistic layouts, and using our part annotations to also score where each object is grasped. Our dataset will be released publicly to facilitate research in visual and embodied AI.
  </details>

- **[GRAPE-Bench: When Vision Degrades, Does Video Reasoning Hold?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33020)**  `arXiv:2611.33020`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ce Zhang, Junyi He, Tianshi Ming, Jinxi He, Jianshu Zhang, Xiyuan Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite strong performance on standard benchmarks, video reasoning models remain vulnerable to visual degradation, raising concerns about their reliability in safety-critical scenarios. Existing benchmarks address this concern in part by simulating visual degradation or probing model reasoning with challenging questions, leaving open how well models handle naturally degraded video and whether they can make the simpler judgments that matter for safe driving. To this end, we introduce GRAPE-Bench, which jointly evaluates video reasoning across visual degradation conditions and question difficulty. It combines a synthetic set spanning twelve corruption types across daily-life and driving videos with real-world footage of driving at night or in rain, snow, or fog. Each video is paired with three progressively challenging questions, from fundamental judgments with potential safety implications to demanding reasoning stress tests. Across open-weight and frontier models, we observe ranking reversals between synthetic and real-world conditions and level-specific weaknesses obscured by overall accuracy. Synthetic-data fine-tuning improves Qwen3.5-9B's real-world accuracy from 76.7% to 81.1%, while clean-privileged on-policy distillation reaches 80.4% in a separate reasoning-enabled setting. Through preference steering, we further demonstrate continuous control over a frozen model's trade-off between safety-relevant judgments and challenging reasoning, with little change in overall accuracy. Together, GRAPE-Bench provides a unified testbed for evaluating, improving, and controlling video reasoning under degraded visual conditions.
  </details>

- **[E-MAPS: Event-Based Promptable Video Segmentation with Memory-as-Prompt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33017)**  `arXiv:2611.33017`  `cs.CV`  
  _Adonay Demewez Gebremedhin, Mohamad Alansari, Wessam Shehieb, Muzammal Naseer, Hasan AlMarzouqi, Sajid Javed, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RGB-based video object segmentation (VOS) remains brittle under fast motion and adverse illumination. Event cameras preserve signal under blur and challenging exposure, but provide little evidence when motion slows. Interaction can restore object state for subsequent propagation, yet promptable event methods merely address single windows. We formalize event-based Promptable Video Segmentation (ePVS), where prompts at arbitrary windows initialize, correct, or introduce objects and predictions. We present Event Memory-As-Prompt Segmentation (E-MAPS), which transfers a pretrained promptable segmenter to event streams and learns propagation and correction. Event-to-Token (E2T) aligns recurrent event features with pretrained representations while preserving prompt-conditioning priors. Memory-Aware Prompting (MAP) converts object-conditioned history into sparse prompts, allowing memory and user inputs to share one decoder pathway efficiently. We construct Event-View Masklets (EViM) from 206K aligned RGB-event pairs and 620K masks for two-stage representation transfer and native event-video learning, and establish three training-disjoint benchmarks. E-MAPS leads prompt-once and interactive ePVS across all three benchmarks while being more efficient. It further demonstrates strong performance on zero-shot object tracking benchmarks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[RePix-VFI: Re-rendering Observed Pixels for Video Frame Interpolation with Generative Priors from One-Step Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33016)**  `arXiv:2611.33016`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dayeon Kim, Jeonghyeok Do, Munchurl Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-frame video frame interpolation (VFI) aims to reconstruct dense video from sparse keyframes, requiring accurate intermediate motion and faithful appearance. As temporal gaps grow, flow-based methods can retain observed textures but misplace them under ambiguous motion, whereas iterative latent-diffusion models can synthesize plausible content but incur substantial sampling costs and may alter details during reconstruction with a variational autoencoder (VAE). We present RePix-VFI, named for Re-rendering observed Pixels, a multi-frame VFI framework that couples draft-conditioned one-step video diffusion with reconstruction from observed keyframes. A pretrained video diffusion model refines a flow-rendered draft of the whole window in a single forward pass. For each target frame, our generative readback matches its two bounding keyframes to the generated prediction using an optical-flow estimator. The resulting correspondences guide re-rendering of original keyframe RGB pixels outside the VAE. A lightweight appearance fusion head combines these renderings with generated appearance. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate significant improvements in reconstruction and perceptual quality. On SNU-FILM at $\times$8 and $\times$16, RePix-VFI achieves the highest PSNR and the lowest LPIPS, FloLPIPS, and tOF among the compared methods, exceeding the best evaluated diffusion-based baseline by 1.71 and 1.48dB in PSNR, respectively. It also runs 32--42$\times$ faster than the state-of-the-art multi-frame VFI diffusion model.
  </details>

- **[PAVE: Preference-Aligned Visual Egocentric Learning for the Blind and Low-Vision Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32996)**  `arXiv:2611.32996`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nan Li, Shaofeng Liang, Shiyun Zhao, Keni Shi, Zhencheng Huang, Bowen Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Egocentric video question answering enables blind and low-vision (BLV) users to query their immediate surroundings through natural language. However, existing benchmarks and models primarily optimize for semantic agreement with reference answers, reducing visual assistance to a purely factual matching task. In real-world scenarios, a factually correct response remains practically unhelpful if it lacks the spatial orientation, actionable detail, or conciseness required by the immediate task of the user. Drawing upon real BLV egocentric videos and authentic user queries from EgoBlind, we construct the PA-EgoQA, a preference-aligned dataset that reconstructs reference answers according to empirical BLV communication criteria. Based on this dataset, we introduce Preference-Aligned Visual Egocentric Learning (PAVE), a unified learning and inference framework that couples structured answer-policy fine-tuning with deterministic question-conditioned routing to adapt response formulation directly to the surface form of the query at inference time. We further align the model via Filter-GRPO, which pairs a hierarchical correctness-quality reward with contrast-based candidate-group filtering to extract learning signals from both factual accuracy contrasts and fine-grained quality variations among correct answers. Extensive experiments across three vision-language backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in factual accuracy alongside substantial gains in assistive response quality. These results establish a concrete paradigm for translating human-centered response requirements into egocentric supervision, generation policies, and post-training objectives. Code will be provided atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Decision-Native Multimodal Clinical Modeling via Anatomical Grounding and Confidence Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32993)**  `arXiv:2611.32993`  `cs.CV`  
  _Juntao Jiang, Wenqi Zhong, Guangyu Guo, Dexia Chen, Yilong Hu, Yunhao Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured clinical decisions from volumetric computed tomography (CT) require anatomically grounded predictions and calibrated confidence estimates. However, existing 3D medical multimodal models often suffer from uncalibrated autoregressive decoding and shortcut reliance on irrelevant anatomical regions. We introduce Radev, a decision-native multimodal model that integrates anatomy-guided visual compression, direct candidate prediction, and counterfactual confidence supervision. To bridge global context with fine-grained slice details, a frozen 3D encoder extracts reusable organ-level features, which are dynamically selected by the query to attend to 2D slice features within predicted anatomical bounds. The resulting compact visual tokens enable the language backbone to estimate candidate probabilities in a single forward pass without autoregressive decoding. We further propose Counterfactual-Calibrated Training (CCT) to compare predictions under intact and intervened organ representations. CCT encourages confidence to decrease when removing target-organ evidence degrades decision quality, while penalizing excessive confidence drops under unrelated-organ removal. To facilitate rigorous evaluation, we construct CT-Verdict, a comprehensive benchmark spanning binary finding presence, multiple-choice questions, and ordinal severity assessment across both in-distribution and external cohorts. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Radev significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both decision accuracy and confidence calibration, while generalizing robustly under distribution shifts. Project page atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[NEST: Uncovering and Nesting Unknown Objects in Open-World Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32981)**  `arXiv:2611.32981`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Yuan Gao, Sebastian Schmidt, Johannes Betz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-world object detectors aim to localize objects of classes not covered by their training annotations, but most label them only as "unknown". This label tells a system that an object is present but not what it is, while its broader category would allow a more informed decision. We therefore address both uncovering unknown objects and assigning them to the broader categories of a given class taxonomy. For uncovering, we find that most unknown objects are already localized and labeled "unknown" in our base detector, but are discarded during final top-k selection because background receives higher scores. To mitigate this, we propose a class-agnostic ranker that estimates how well each predicted box fits an object and uses these scores to select the final predictions. Across three benchmarks, our detector improves unknown recall by 3.8% to 195.1% relative to the strongest baseline in each task, while maintaining competitive known-class detection. For categorizing, we draw on Euler diagrams, in which the region of a category contains the regions of its members. Our categorizer builds each category's region from the known classes and learns how much room to leave for members it has never seen. On the same detections, it categorizes unknown objects more accurately than existing hierarchical methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Teaching Time Without Losing General Retrieval: Adapting Multimodal Embeddings for Time-Sensitive Video Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32971)**  `arXiv:2611.32971`  `cs.CV`  
  _Toya Oyama, Rainer Lienhart, Shin'ichi Satoh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In text-to-video retrieval, MLLM-based embedding models do well when the text describes the scene (general retrieval), but poorly when it describes actions and their direction (time-sensitive retrieval). TARA, a pioneering work, fine-tunes such a model on text triplets with temporal hard negatives; it improves time-sensitive retrieval but lowers general retrieval (mean R@1 over six benchmarks in our rerun on Qwen3-VL-Embedding-8B: 58.4->44.8), and its released model keeps general retrieval only when ensembled with Qwen3-VL-Embedding. Our goal is strong general and time-sensitive retrieval with one model and one vector per clip. Three findings lead to three simple components. Keep loss: general retrieval follows how far the output embeddings drift from the base model, not how far the weights move; we therefore keep them near the base model, which keeps most general retrieval even without general-domain data. Pair loss: the text embeddings of an action description and its reversed-direction version are well separated, but the video embeddings of a clip and its reversed copy are close; we therefore widen the gap between the video embeddings of each clip and its reversed copy, in the direction of the difference between their two text embeddings. Middle-layer LoRA: frames are combined in the middle layers, and temporal information peaks in or just after them; we therefore train LoRA only on these layers, which nearly matches training all weights on most time-sensitive benchmarks with 0.15% of the parameters and keeps general retrieval better. General R@1 stays at 58.4, while R@1 with each clip's reversed copy in the gallery rises from 55.5 to 63.2; the gains carry over to unseen benchmarks and InternVL2-8B-RA. The keep loss is also a remedy for the TARA recipe (general R@1: 44.8->57.8), and ensembling TARA with Ours gives the highest scores on RTime and unseen CiA among the compared models.
  </details>

- **[FocusAnchor: Zero-Shot Metric Depth by Anchoring a Depth Foundation Prior to Focus Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32970)**  `arXiv:2611.32970`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ashkan Ganj, Hang Su, Tian Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing depth-from-focus methods can struggle with weak focus cues, new cameras, or fewer focal slices. We present FocusAnchor, a recurrent model that uses focus-volume lookups to refine metric depth and repeatedly aligns a relative depth prior from a frozen foundation model to the current depth estimate. The aligned prior guides subsequent refinement in a feedback loop, carrying metric scale recovered from textured regions into regions where focus cues are weak while preserving scene structure. To support transfer across cameras, we synthesize training stacks with randomized camera parameters and focus distances. However, stack synthesis blurs existing image noise differently across slices, creating artificial depth cues even on textureless surfaces. We add independent per-slice noise to suppress these cues. We also introduce variable-stack training to improve robustness with fewer focal slices and support more practical mobile capture. Compared with FOSSA ViT-B, FocusAnchor reduces AbsRel by 36% on NYU Depth V2 and by 9% on DDFF-12 after fine-tuning. In zero-shot evaluation, FocusAnchor and its variable-stack variant, FocusAnchor-flex, achieve the two lowest AbsRel values on all five benchmarks. On ZEDD test, FocusAnchor achieves 9% lower AbsRel than the best competing method, FOSSA ViT-B, while FocusAnchor-flex achieves lower AbsRel with 40% fewer focal slices on ZEDD validation.
  </details>

- **[OmniHarness-RSI: Modality-Aware Harness Evolution for Omnimodal Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32951)**  `arXiv:2611.32951`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shilin Ma, Xingming Shui, Jinglin Chen, Zetong Zhao, Jingqi Tian, Deyi Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in harness-based recursive self-improvement (RSI) have improved video understanding by evolving executable agent workflows without updating foundation model parameters. However, extending this paradigm to omnimodal understanding remains challenging, as the heterogeneous and interdependent nature of multimodal perception and reasoning creates a complex evolution space, making targeted harness optimization difficult. To address these challenges, we propose OmniHarness-RSI, a modality-aware framework for evolving agent harnesses. Specifically, we factorize the evolution space into three capability dimensions: modality routing and collaboration, modality-aware evidence localization, and modality-specific perception. Based on this factorization, we construct a Factorized-Harness Agent integrating specialized perception experts and audio-video retrieval tools. We further introduce modality-guided harness evolution, which identifies deficiencies through structured modality-capability attribution and refines corresponding harness strategies through interactive code revisions and execution feedback. Validation-guided selection and held-out evaluation assess generalization within and across datasets. Experiments on WorldSense, Daily-Omni, and OmniVideoBench demonstrate test accuracy improvements of 8.86, 8.13, and 6.23 percentage points, respectively, consistently outperforming modality-agnostic RSI. The evolved harnesses also demonstrate cross-dataset transfer without adaptation, while reducing planner calls and viewed frames.
  </details>

- **[IMPACT-Seg: Consistent Anatomical Segmentation and Representation Learning Across CT, CBCT, and MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32903)**  `arXiv:2611.32903`  `cs.CV`  
  _Cédric Hémon, Valentin Boussot, Matthew McCormick, Lukas Zimmermann, Christopher Kurz, Guillaume Landry_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computed tomography (CT), cone-beam CT (CBCT), and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) provide complementary representations of the same underlying anatomy, yet substantial differences in image appearance challenge consistent segmentation and deformable registration in adaptive radiotherapy. We investigate whether dense anatomical segmentation supervision can promote cross-modality correspondence without explicit feature alignment. We introduce IMPACT-Seg, which segments 126 structures across CT, CBCT, and MRI using shared network parameters. A collection of 8,290 volumes was assembled from public cohorts through label harmonization, model-assisted pseudo-label completion, iterative refinement, and 2,864 physics-based CBCT simulations with voxel-aligned CT labels. Unpaired images were used to jointly train 2.5D and 3D residual-encoder networks.On 139 real CBCTs, IMPACT-Seg 3D achieved a mean Dice of 0.842 versus 0.671 for the strongest comparator. On 497 voxel-aligned CT-simulated-CBCT pairs, the CT-to-CBCT Dice decrease was 0.022 versus 0.153-0.178 for competing methods. On independent pancreatic radiotherapy CBCTs, gastrointestinal Dice was 0.735 versus 0.634 for TotalSegmentator. Prediction agreement reached 0.881 on registered CT-CBCT and 0.790 on CT-MRI pairs. Adding MRI training data reduced CT-MRI encoder feature retrieval error from 36.8 to 3.2 mm. Registration pipelines incorporating IMPACT-Seg features ranked first on two multimodal Learn2Reg benchmarks. The 3D model segmented a whole-body CT in 15 s.These findings show that dense anatomical segmentation supervision can support consistent anatomical representations across modalities, with applications to both segmentation and registration. Code, datasets, and pretrained models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DualSpec: Spectral Reconstruction with Optimized Stereo Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32896)**  `arXiv:2611.32896`  `cs.CV`  
  _Luca Cogo, Marco Buzzelli, Simone Bianco, Raimondo Schettini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spectral imaging captures information that ordinary RGB sensors cannot, but dedicated spectral cameras can be expensive and bulky. Recovering dense spectra from RGB images offers an accessible alternative, but distinct spectra can produce the same camera response, generating an ambiguity that even more advanced recovery algorithms cannot always resolve. Therefore, increasing the spectral information captured at acquisition is essential. We propose DualSpec, a stereo acquisition framework that jointly optimizes two external optical filters and a reconstruction network. Each filter is placed in front of a standard RGB camera, and the two are designed to produce six responses that provide complementary information for the network. Experiments across reconstruction architectures and spectral ranges show that DualSpec reduces mean spectral angle error by 36-72% relative to a single RGB camera and by 11-29% relative to stereo pairs selected from commercial filters. Cross-dataset evaluations, including a new benchmark of spectral images captured from displaced viewpoints, show that the method generalizes and remains robust to viewpoint misalignment, while analyses of thin-film approximations support optical feasibility.
  </details>

- **[3D Tracking Any Point with Events and Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32883)**  `arXiv:2611.32883`  `cs.CV`  
  _Roberto Pellerito, Zijia Dai, Laurent Kneip, Davide Scaramuzza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking a fast-moving point in 3D is prohibitive when RGB frames are blurred or too far apart to reveal its motion. Event cameras record brightness changes between frames, providing measurements inside these temporal gaps. We introduce TrackAnyMotion, which combines monocular RGB frames and events to track arbitrary points in 2D and 3D, including at times when no RGB frame is available. The motion expected for a static point, given scene geometry and camera motion, provides a reference for identifying independently moving objects. An independent-motion decoder combines geometric motion cues with event guidance and 2D tracks, feeding its predictions back into iterative 2D and 3D refinement. We also introduce HyperEvent, a synthetic RGB--Event dataset with high-frequency 3D trajectories, geometry, and independent-motion labels. At RGB frame timestamps on this benchmark, TrackAnyMotion reduces 3D and 2D endpoint errors by 70\% and 32\%, respectively.
  </details>

- **[MemHand: Memory-Enhanced World-Space Hand Motion Reconstruction from Egocentric Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32876)**  `arXiv:2611.32876`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyi Ma, Zhangshuo Qi, Erhang Zhang, Mingjie Li, Hesheng Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering the continuous world-space motion of both hands from egocentric video is essential for applications such as human-to-robot policy transfer and augmented reality. Achieving this goes beyond reconstructing the hands from each individual frame, as the context that a frame needs is often spread over time, especially around occlusions or when a hand moves out of sight. Recent works have explored how to exploit temporal context, yet they mostly draw it from fixed-length windows. Such windows cannot guarantee that every frame has the context required to reconstruct the hands in world space with accurate poses and metric scale. To address this limitation, we introduce MemHand, a memory-enhanced framework for world-space hand motion reconstruction. MemHand maintains two complementary forms of memory. The Short-Term Event Memory keeps the context of the well-observed hand around each occlusion or out-of-sight event and supplies it to frames with weak or missing visual evidence. In addition, since the metric scale holds over the whole recording, the Long-Term Scale Memory retains the most reliable observations across the video as scale anchors within a bounded state. Across benchmarks featuring severe occlusions and frequent out-of-sight intervals, MemHand achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and generalizes well to unseen domains. Benefiting from the synergy of both memories, it cuts W-MPJPE by 57\% on ARCTIC and 70\% on HOT3D relative to StableHand, delivering continuous and metrically accurate world-space hand poses and trajectories. We will release our code and models at the project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Aqua2CAD : Observation-Guided Point Cloud Correction and Completion for CAD Reconstruction from Underwater Stereo Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32835)**  `arXiv:2611.32835`  `cs.CV`  
  _Weizhao Huang, Jiayi Zhang, Pangkit Fong, Chongrong Fang, Jianping He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering editable computer-aided design (CAD) models from underwater stereo images requires accurate and complete geometry, yet stereo reconstruction produces displaced points and missing regions. The original views retain cross-view disagreement and ray evidence lost during point-cloud fusion. Aqua2CAD exploits this evidence in two stages: view-conditioned correction and quality filtering establish reliable geometric support, while multi-view observations condition a learned shape prior to infer missing surfaces in the original metric frame. The recovered points support surface fitting and solid assembly with manually revised face groups. On an OceanSim-based synthetic benchmark, Aqua2CAD reduces point-cloud Chamfer distance by 60% relative to the stereo reconstruction. Compared with a matched point-only completion backbone, it improves missing-region recovery at 5,mm from 16.27% to 24.16%. With a shared CAD backend, exterior acceptance increases from 6/19 to 14/19. Qualitative results on selected real underwater captures demonstrate recovery of the main structures of six 3D-printed objects.
  </details>

- **[PhysMend: Verification-Guided Program Refinement for Physics-Aware Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32821)**  `arXiv:2611.32821`  `cs.CV`  
  _Meilong Xu, Ziyi Zhao, Bhagyashree Puranik, Shayda Moezzi, Yixiao Zhang, Kushan Thakkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generative models synthesize realistic scenes, yet their interactions often violate physics. A ball dropped onto sand should displace grains and settle into a crater, whereas a generated video may show it passing through the surface or leaving the sand flat. Post-hoc methods typically score generated videos with a vision-language model (VLM), but such scores offer limited guidance on which object or parameter to modify, and each correction costs another synthesis. Simulation-based pipelines instead run fixed stages before synthesis, so simulation errors reach the video. To address these limitations, we present PhysMend, a training-free framework for verification-guided program refinement. PhysMend describes the target event as an editable physical program of grounded objects, events, and required relations, paired with tunable execution parameters. Our key insight is to verify and repair the event on its simulated rollout and on the depth video that conditions the generator (depth control) rather than on generated videos, enabling quantitative violation measurement, targeted editing, and fast re-verification before synthesis. A hybrid verifier evaluates semantic event consistency, physical plausibility, solver stability, and visibility in the depth control. VLM judgments are confined to event semantics and cannot override failed physical measurements, which limits the impact of VLM hallucinations. Each failed check localizes the program fields eligible for repair, and its measured violation scales a bounded update of their parameters. Only accepted depth control reaches the generator, which runs once per input. Experiments on the commonly used benchmarks Physics-IQ, PhyGenBench, and VBench-2.0 demonstrate substantial gains across different generator backbones; for example, PhysMend raises the overall Physics-IQ score of Wan-VACE from 27.33 to 39.40.
  </details>

- **[SpatialArena: Where Do Geometric Foundation Models Stand?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32816)**  `arXiv:2611.32816`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu Chen, Haosen Yang, Su Ee Tan, Zhensong Zhang, Jifei Song, Jiankang Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D perception is shifting from task-specific pipelines to geometric foundation models: monocular depth networks, feed-forward 3D/4D reconstruction models, and vision--language models that predict geometry. However, their progress cannot be measured reliably. Published numbers are not comparable, because every paper samples frames, sets resolutions, and selects test splits differently. They are not robust because the alignment required for every up-to-scale prediction can outweigh the gap between models: fitting depth in the wrong space nearly triples its error, and standard trajectory alignment understates the error of failing methods by up to $2.6\times$. And they rarely cover long recordings, dynamic scenes, or aerial views. We present SpatialArena, an open arena that scores geometric models with the same metrics and rules, with robust and deterministic alignment. SpatialArena spans five tasks, from monocular depth to SLAM. It covers 23 public datasets with 418,929 images across indoor, outdoor, aerial, object-centric, and dynamic scenes, together with Spatial Arena, our own captures with accurate poses and depth. Evaluating 52 models, we find that single-image depth is close to saturation on public benchmarks but not on large, uncontrolled scenes, and that progress now comes from consistency across frames and views, where feed-forward 3D/4D models match or surpass monocular specialists and overtake video depth models. The benchmark, toolbox, and leaderboard will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[Qwen-Image-HD: Autoregressive Diffusion for High Definition Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32736)**  `arXiv:2611.32736`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihao Liu, Zekai Zhang, Shengming Yin, Zikai Zhou, Donghao Zhou, Chenyang Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning a generator on a complete high-resolution canvas requires long image sequences and substantial computation, yet local regions can still exhibit unnatural repetitive stripes when training resources are limited. We introduce Qwen-Image-HD, which adapts Qwen-Image 2.0 to high-resolution synthesis through local training and blockwise autoregressive generation. Each prediction covers a target tile at 1K scale, conditioned on high-resolution upper and left neighbors and a compressed historical thumbnail. These conditions provide adjacent fine-scale structures and an overview of the scene generated so far. To integrate these complementary cues, we map both forms of history and the target to a shared canvas coordinate system to express their spatial relationships and scale differences. On Qwen-Image-Bench at 4K, Qwen-Image-HD achieves stronger overall evaluation performance than direct 4K generation with FLUX-3-Image-Klein. Compared with full-image 4K supervised fine-tuning of the same backbone, it achieves better generation quality with lower training compute and higher inference efficiency, while exhibiting fewer unnatural repetitive stripes in original-resolution comparisons. We further introduce a Wavefront-AutoRegressive inference schedule that generates tiles on the same anti-diagonal concurrently across GPUs to reduce single-image latency.
  </details>

- **[SplatSeg3D: Efficient Zero-Shot 3D Segmentation via Point Cloud Surfelization and Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32716)**  `arXiv:2611.32716`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sandeep Inuganti, Mahdi Chamseddine, Marcel Rogge, Martin R. Oswald, Didier Stricker, Jason Rambach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary 3D segmentation methods rely on 2D foundation models applied to registered images captured alongside the 3D point clouds. However, such images may not be available in many cases, while direct 3D point cloud segmentation methods require semantic class supervision and are challenging to deploy in a zero-shot manner. To address these issues, we present SplatSeg3D, which segments a bare colored point cloud in an open-vocabulary manner without requiring the accompanying images, camera poses, class supervision, or instance mask proposals. To achieve this, we first surfelize the input point cloud and obtain oriented 2D Gaussians. We then render views of the scene by splatting, with a view selection strategy that maximizes scene coverage with as few views as possible. Next, we apply text-prompted segmentation on the rendered views, giving each 3D point a set of multi-view semantic cues, fused to produce a 3D segmentation of the scene. We evaluate SplatSeg3D on semantic segmentation and novel-class instance segmentation on 4 standard benchmarks: Scannet, Scannet++, Replica, and S3DIS. Our pipeline not only performs better or on-par with the state-of-the-art, but we also achieve these results efficiently across runtime and compute requirements. Code will be published.
  </details>

- **[Knocked Off Course: Tracing Uncommanded Motion from Perception to Action in Embodied VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32705)**  `arXiv:2611.32705`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dan Zlotnikov, Nadav Alali, Ohad Ben-Shahar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) enable simultaneous processing of egocentric video and textual target descriptions to produce action commands for embodied tracking platforms. However, these platforms rarely move exactly as commanded, in part due to unexpected environmental disturbances such as wind, unleveled terrain, and road bumps. Here we study how such models handle uncommanded egomotion. First, we explore performance across diverse disturbances by benchmarking three tracking VLAs on three datasets. We find that models differ in which environmental disturbances hurt them most, but all are sensitive to sudden target loss. Tracing egomotion from pixels to emitted actions, we identify two gaps. In representation, motion is reliably recovered through explicit relations between frames, but aggressive compression of past frames weakens small-motion readouts. In action, large rotations do reach the decision state, yet the decoded direction carries only a few percent of a mirrored rotation's effect on the turn. Targeting both gaps, we fine-tune one model, Uni-NaVid, on data amounting to just 1.7% of its original training set in a ladder of analysis-motivated variants. These gain 5-22 points after target-loss without degrading clean success; after a smooth loss, the best one improves success by 72% (relative), recovering 76% of its gap to clean. We also show that targeted fine-tuning improves recovery partly via a text-mediated action dependency, without new architecture, large-scale data collection or training from scratch.
  </details>

- **[RestPose: Canonicalization for Open Object Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32696)**  `arXiv:2611.32696`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sandeep Inuganti, Weihang Li, Benjamin Busam, Didier Stricker, Jason Rambach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In object pose estimation methods, predicted poses are expressed with respect to a canonical frame, defined by a reference model of that object. In learning-based methods, an arbitrarily chosen modeling convention defines this canonical frame, which is then used to generate the ground-truth pose-annotations for training. Object pose estimation methods are therefore bound to such arbitrary conventions, restricting generalization. In this work, we propose that such a canonical frame can instead be derived for any object, and present RestPose, an approach towards open object pose estimation that does not require reference models or canonical frame conventions during training. From a single input image, we generate an object mesh and canonicalize it through a geometric and physical analysis of the object, then use foundation model-based dense matching to estimate the pose. We choose category-level benchmarks for our experiments, which contain instances across various object categories and enable us to measure the effectiveness of our canonicalization. Across HouseCat6D, NOCS, and Omni6DPose datasets, we improve on the supervised state-of-the-art methods by 18.3 points in absolute metrics and 13.8 points in scale-agnostic metrics, without requiring task-specific training. Code will be published.
  </details>

- **[Text Is Just Another Shot in Vision-Language Models: Simple Probes on Frozen Encoders Do It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32672)**  `arXiv:2611.32672`  `cs.CV`  
  _Reda Bensaid, Ismail Ben Ayed, François Leduc-Primeau, Vincent Gripon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot adaptation of vision-language models has produced increasingly elaborate methods built on CLIP and on one shared principle: the zero-shot text classifier is the anchor, and labeled images only correct it. We argue that this principle reflects CLIP's visual features rather than a property of the task. We provide a simple Bayesian interpretation, in which a class name is worth exactly kappa = sigma^2 / tau^2 labeled images, the ratio between the visual spread of a class and the misalignment of its name. The Bayes classifier of this model counts text as kappa more samples. With modern frozen encoders, few-shot adaptation then reduces to a well-regularised convex probe. Specifically, we introduce TASP, a simple convex logistic regression on frozen features that incorporates class-name embeddings as weighted training examples and as a regularization prior. On the standard 11-dataset benchmark, TASP with frozen PE-Core-L features achieves 87.1% accuracy at 1 shot and 91.4% at 16 shots, outperforming state-of-the-art few-shot vision-language adaptation methods by 4.6 to 11.0 percentage points. While stronger representations explain much of this improvement, controlled comparisons on matched features show that existing frozen-encoder adaptation methods do not materially improve on this probe. We further find that the weight assigned to text matters more than how it is incorporated, and that combining self-supervised visual features with vision-language representations is most beneficial for weaker visual encoders. Together, these findings suggest that progress in few-shot vision-language adaptation depends more on representation quality and the appropriate use of text supervision than on increasingly complex adaptation mechanisms.
  </details>

- **[HazeFlux: Few-Step Generative Priors for Real-World Image Dehazing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32632)**  `arXiv:2611.32632`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yunong Liu, Danna Xue, Javier Vazquez-Corral_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world single-image dehazing suffers from severe domain shifts, as paired real data is scarce and models trained on synthetic haze generalize poorly to real-world haze. While pre-trained generative editing models offer powerful image priors to bridge this generalization gap, applying them to image dehazing poses two major challenges: standard generative models prioritize distribution alignment over strict pixel-level fidelity, and operating in compressed latent spaces inherently discards fine-grained structural details. In this paper, we propose a novel two-stage framework that adapts the generative prior of a few-step flow-matching backbone to real-world dehazing. In the first stage, we fine-tune the latent backbone using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), supervised not only on a flow-matching objective, but also on pixel-level supervision, thus enabling direct hazy-to-clear translation. In the second stage, we introduce structure-preserving skip connections directly to the VAE decoder, bypassing latent compression artifacts to recover precise spatial edges. Experiments on four realworld benchmarks demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing dehazing methods in real conditions.
  </details>

- **[On the Capabilities and Limitations of Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Cloud Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32625)**  `arXiv:2611.32625`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jose Sosa, Thomas Gillet, Rim Sleimi, Albert Abello_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in vision-language foundation models have enabled open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) without task-specific training, offering new opportunities for Earth Observation (EO) applications. However, their capabilities for cloud detection remain insufficiently explored. In this work, we evaluate twelve OVSS approaches across six publicly available EO datasets in a zero-shot setting, i.e., without fine-tuning or adaptation to the target data. We investigate both binary cloud detection and multiclass segmentation, distinguishing between thick clouds, thin clouds, cloud shadows, and background. Our results show differences in performance across models and datasets, with SegEarth-OV3 achieving the highest binary cloud detection performance on four of the six datasets. While several approaches demonstrate promising performance in binary cloud detection, multiclass segmentation remains more challenging, particularly when distinguishing between fine-grained cloud categories. Furthermore, our controlled prompt sensitivity analysis reveals model-dependent responses to alternative textual descriptions, with appearance-oriented prompts benefiting certain approaches while degrading the performance of others. Overall, our findings demonstrate both the potential and current limitations of zero-shot OVSS for cloud detection, establishing a comparative benchmark for future research on generalisable cloud segmentation. Code will be available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ShapeQuest: Towards Granularity-Controlled Edit-Conditioned 3D Asset Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32621)**  `arXiv:2611.32621`  `cs.CV`  
  _Faris Abu-Ali, Sankalp Sinha, Didier Stricker, Muhammad Zeshan Afzal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a reference asset and an instruction such as "remove one armrest," a retriever must find variants that make the edit and preserve the other parts. This search is well established for images but remains underexplored for individual 3D assets. To study this problem, we introduce ShapeQuest and ShapeQuester, a new benchmark and model for edit-conditioned 3D asset retrieval. ShapeQuest's open-source pipeline edits textured meshes through part additions, removals, and material and colour changes.
