  _Alexandre Le Mercier, Chris Develder, Thomas Demeester_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collectives of AI agents can outperform any single agent on complex tasks, yet remain vulnerable to imposters that deliberately mislead their peers. Because even one such imposter can sabotage the collective, reliable detection is essential. We examine two strategies: embedding detection inside the participating agents, or employing a dedicated external detector that observes the deliberation without joining it. On a deliberately small but challenging chess-based testbed, we find that simply warning agents of possible imposters is counterproductive. It degrades decisions even in the absence of adversaries, triggers unhelpful social dynamics, and leaks information to the imposter. An external detector avoids these pitfalls, yet must continually adapt as imposters evolve new strategies. We show that a lightweight meta-trained detector can rapidly recalibrate to unseen attacks from only a handful of examples, far more efficiently than conventional fine-tuning. To enable continued study of robust multi-agent systems under adaptive adversaries, we release the accompanying benchmark of labeled deliberations spanning a wide range of evolved imposter strategies. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SD-Cache: Learnable Memory Consolidation with State-Guided Dynamic Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31265)**  `arXiv:2611.31265`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuanhong Chen, Yiping Ji, Cameron Gordon, Emily Hackett-Jones, Hemanth Saratchandran, Anton van den Hengel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as persistent services that must reason over large volumes of external knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds model responses in relevant documents, but the retrieved content must still be encoded and stored in the key-value (KV) cache. As this content accumulates, the cache grows linearly, increasing memory consumption and inference cost. Existing memory-compression methods address this problem either by retaining a subset of cached states or by learning context compressors that map documents to compact latent representations. Despite these advances, consolidating information under tight memory budgets while preserving its usefulness for future queries remains challenging. We investigate how the model's native KV states can be selectively consolidated into a fixed-size working memory without access to future queries. To this end, we introduce SD-Cache (State-guided and Dynamically updated Cache), a query-agnostic KV-memory consolidation framework that operates alongside a frozen generator LLM. SD-Cache learns to consolidate layer-wise KV states into fixed-size memory through selective anchor-based pooling and state-guided refinement, processing new contexts in a single forward pass without context-specific optimisation. Experiments on retrieval-augmented question answering and synthetic long-context benchmarks show that SD-Cache maintains competitive downstream performance under constrained memory budgets.
  </details>

- **[Untangling the Mechanisms of Misleading Context in Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02754)**  `arXiv:2609.02754`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Robin Linzmayer, Noémie Elhadad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models now answer medical questions with expert-level performance. However, the context these systems act on can be misleading, and misleading context can corrupt a model's medical judgment. To understand how misleading context corrupts this judgment, we examine the model's susceptibility to the context, disclosure of it, mechanism of corrupted reasoning, and monitorability of the decision. On the medical reasoning subset of MedMisBench, a clinician-reviewed question-answering benchmark of 8,627 questions, we inject two types of misleading context cues, fabricated evidence and a bare assertion. We test three reasoning models, two that expose their full reasoning trace and one frontier model that exposes only its response. All three are more susceptible to the assertion than to the fabricated evidence, adopting the asserted answer 10 to 27 points more often. The misleading cues are disclosed in 81 to 98% of traces but only 7 to 90% of responses, and the assertion is disclosed less often than evidence based cues. Resampling from reasoning traces without disclosure shows the two cues corrupt reasoning differently, evidence entering early and accumulating while the assertion redirects the conclusion near its end. An LLM monitor catches 78% of corrupted decisions at 5% false positives when reading an open model's trace with guidance, against at most 32% from any response. The misleading context that models are most susceptible to is disclosed least, and was caught reliably only from an open reasoning trace, which frontier providers withhold.
  </details>

- **[Structured Prompt Optimization Advances Globally and Locally Interpretable Learning from Complex Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29076)**  `arXiv:2605.29076`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianyang Zhou, Wenbo Chen, Pierre Jinghong Liang, Leman Akoglu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs have advanced text classification, yet existing paradigms face a trade-off: supervised (label only) fine-tuning is scalable but offers limited reasoning on complex text and lacks broader model transparency, while discrete prompt optimization offers human-readable instructions but struggles with performance and scalability. We introduce eXTC (eXplainable Text Classifier), centered on a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP, or rulebook) learned in natural language, in three stages: (1) learning the SOP via a new Structured Prompt Optimization algorithm; (2) SOP-grounded reasoning distillation from a large teacher LLM into a compact LM; and (3) expanding reasoning capabilities beyond the SOP via reinforcement learning. This design enables eXTC to provide (i) fast inference via a compact LM, with (ii) inference-time local reasoning traces, alongside a global, modular explanation of its learned domain rules, while (iii) significantly outperforming existing paradigms across diverse benchmarks in both classification performance and explanation quality, with stage-by-stage gains.
  </details>

- **[AIChipBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Automated AI Accelerator Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33093)**  `arXiv:2611.33093`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Guangyuan Wang, Chengran Li, Minchen Luo, Jichen Feng, Mengdi Wang, Yifan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language-model decoder carries state between decoding steps, and in hardware a wrong state update can leave the current token correct and corrupt the output several tokens later, so a design can pass output tests and still be wrong. AIChipBench asks what LLM coding agents need to build such hardware correctly. Agents implement quantized Qwen3.5, Gemma 3, Gemma 4, and Llama 3.2 decoders in synthesizable register-transfer-level (RTL) code against an executable integer reference that specifies the outputs and the stored state at every decoding step. They debug against public tests under fixed budgets; their frozen designs are graded on held-out token sequences, resets, memory stalls, and synthesis, and a second track asks them to speed up a correct design at nearly fixed area. Across 9,136 attempts by four coding agents, the difficulty lies in stored state. Feedback that points to the first state mismatch raises acceptance over output-only feedback in all 12 agent--task cells of a matched study, by 25.0 to 46.9 percentage points, and halves the agent-hours per accepted design. Regraded on new sequences and conditions, 45 of 69 accepted designs fail within the 32 steps their tests required. More than a third of attempts to speed up a correct design end without one. An overall score ranks Sol 5.6 and Fable 5.1 above Astra 6 and Opus 5.5, and both halves of the instances give this order in all 200 random splits. Hardware agents therefore need debugging feedback that exposes stored state, and their benchmarks need final tests well beyond the development cases.
  </details>

- **[ASCENT: A Benchmark for Evaluating and Advancing Stepwise Diagnostic Reasoning in Large Language Models on Common Clinical Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32734)**  `arXiv:2611.32734`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yera Choi, Yeong Hwa Kim, Jaedeok Lee, Taekang Kim, Sangdoo Yun, Seong-Eun Moon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel at medical question answering yet are rarely evaluated on the stepwise diagnostic reasoning that defines real clinical workflows, where impressions are revised as information accumulates. We build Annotated Stepwise Clinical rEasoning for NaturalisTic Diagnosis (ASCENT), a clinician-annotated benchmark and training resource of 3,078 stepwise problems derived from MedQA-USMLE that decomposes each vignette into EMR-aligned steps (Findings, Impression, supporting Rationale), enabling evaluation of intermediate reasoning under incomplete information.Experiments and training with ASCENT revealed insights into how current LLMs handle stepwise diagnostic reasoning. Even strong reasoning models that perform well on MedQA-USMLE leave substantial headroom on ASCENT, and general-purpose frontier models trail further, exposing a persistent gap between fully informed and stepwise diagnosis. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-7B and 32B on ASCENT yields measurable F1 gains over both pre-trained and HuatuoGPT-o1 CoT-trained baselines, with gains driven primarily by precision. Complementary robustness analyses (counterfactual perturbation, format-vs-content control, judge agreement, and rollout) further show that ASCENT-fine-tuned models rely on the diagnostic content of prior impressions rather than imitating their output format, while error propagation under rollout remains a key challenge for clinical deployment.
  </details>

- **[REBEAL: Human References Are No Longer the Best Translation, and Metrics Do Not Notice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32623)**  `arXiv:2611.32623`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ahrii Kim, Chanjun Park, Seong-heum Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-based MT evaluation assumes the human reference is correct and better than the systems it scores. Both assumptions now fail, since the human reference ranks first in none of the 2024 WMT MQM campaigns and carries annotated errors in 19% to 87% of its segments. What a metric does with a flawed reference is a question about the metric, and it would have been worth asking before reference quality came into doubt. We introduce REBEAL (Reference Bias EvALuation), a benchmark that probes how MT metrics respond to seven types of controlled reference contamination, drawn from the error categories those campaigns record. Across eight widely used metrics and three language pairs, recent learned metrics (xCOMET, MetricX) prove more robust than their predecessors, but each family fails in its own way. Surface metrics show no length sensitivity, so contamination hides in longer segments, while learned metrics accept references in unrelated languages as nearly correct. The failure they share is named-entity contamination, which no metric detects and which flips up to 8 of 36 system pairs when ranking nine MT systems.
  </details>

- **[RightDiff: Learning Generative Reward Models to Judge by the Right Differences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32166)**  `arXiv:2611.32166`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiyuan Chen, Hongsen Huang, Jiahe Chen, Renjie Hua, Hongxia Xu, Chuan Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative reward models typically rely on generic query-level rubrics or unconstrained rationales, overlooking the fact that pairwise preference is driven by response-specific differences. Moreover, optimizing solely for outcome correctness encourages models to exploit superficial shortcuts such as verbosity and formatting, triggering rationale drift. To address this, we propose RightDiff, which structures pairwise evaluation into an evidence-grounded evaluation trajectory executed in a single autoregressive generation. RightDiff explicitly discovers contrastive differences between candidates, categorizes them into material or immaterial factors, and induces pair-specific rubrics before final adjudication. To train this process without post-hoc rationalization, we synthesize demonstration data via response-isolated prompts and optimize the reward model with a difference-alignment reward regularized by budget-constrained one-to-one matching and multiplicative gating. RightDiff achieves state-of-the-art preference modeling performance on RewardBench and RM-Bench, surpassing all open-source reward models and leading proprietary LLM judges. Downstream, it provides reliable supervision for direct preference optimization on Arena-Hard and AlpacaEval while effectively resisting length shortcuts.
  </details>

- **[Making Evaluation Explicit Is Not Enough: Supervising the Evaluation Process in Generative Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32161)**  `arXiv:2611.32161`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiyuan Chen, Yuze Zhao, Jiahe Chen, Hongsen Huang, Hongxia Xu, Yingda Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative reward models (GRMs) make language-model evaluation more explicit by generating critiques, rubrics, or intermediate analyses before predicting a final preference. However, explicit evaluation does not necessarily imply a reliable evaluation process: under outcome-only supervision, a model may reach the correct final preference while producing weak or unsupported intermediate judgments, turning the evaluation into a post-hoc rationalization. We introduce STEP-GRM, a framework that directly supervises the intermediate evaluation process of rubric-based GRMs. In addition to conventional preference supervision, STEP-GRM provides structural guidance for rubric construction and local supervision for criterion-level judgments, encouraging the model to learn not only the correct final preference but also a more reliable process for reaching it. Extensive experiments show that STEP-GRM achieves strong performance on standard reward-modeling benchmarks while improving the consistency and correctness of intermediate evaluations. Moreover, reward signals produced by STEP-GRM lead to better downstream policy optimization, demonstrating that more reliable evaluation processes can translate into practical gains beyond reward-model accuracy.
  </details>

- **[SPARQL-Planned Single-Hop Traversal over a Live Knowledge Graph for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31771)**  `arXiv:2611.31771`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Priyesh Vakharia, Masud Moshtaghi, Pratyay Banerjee, Nicholas Jedema, Amita Misra, Ankit Chadha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic retrieval struggles with multi-hop question answering on both recall and precision: gold passages are buried among distractors that degrade the reader's answer, and retrieving them requires reasoning over the query rather than matching it. GraphRAG techniques expand semantic retrieval by connecting passages to graph nodes and performing one or more hops of expansion after initial retrieval. This improves recall, but multi-hop expansion becomes expensive and brings additional noise into the retrieved set. We break this tradeoff by \emph{interleaving} dense retrieval with knowledge-graph traversal for complex multi-hop questions. Dense retrieval locates the first relevant regions of the search space at good recall, while traversal of a pre-existing external KG (Wikidata) restores precision and improves recall by confirming retrieved passages against a reasoning path through the KG. To keep traversal both question-adaptive and reliable, we have an LLM plan it as code generation task for graph traversal in SPARQL format but \emph{parse rather than execute} the query. On full 1{,}000-question splits under a shared corpus and reader, and with a single configuration applied identically to every dataset, we reach \textbf{62.5 / 75.1} EM/F1 on HotpotQA, \textbf{73.5 / 81.0} on 2WikiMultiHopQA, and \textbf{37.1 / 50.0} on MuSiQue, ahead of reproduced HippoRAG2, T\textsuperscript{2}RAG, and IRCoT on all three benchmarks. A second, token-matched setting stays ahead of T\textsuperscript{2}RAG on HotpotQA and 2Wiki while spending roughly its per-query budget. Executing the generated SPARQL instead of parsing it costs up to 11.4 EM, mostly because raw queries return empty results, not because of syntax errors.
  </details>

- **[AhaPro: A Process-level Rebus benchmark for probing Aha moments in LLM reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31268)**  `arXiv:2611.31268`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Guo, Lang Qin, Yundi Gai, Qinghua Xu, Zhi Wu, Wenjian Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing research on Aha moments in large language models has largely focused on reflective expressions or final revision outcomes, leaving their role in the visible reasoning process insufficiently characterized. Rebus puzzles encode answers through character forms, spatial arrangements, and homophony. Reinterpreting key details may trigger Aha moments and shifts in the reasoning process and candidate answers. We introduce AhaPro, a process-level benchmark that uses rebus puzzles to examine how models reorganize evidence and revise answers through Aha moments. The framework represents CoT as sequences of candidate answers, evidence, and verification states, using hierarchical metrics to assess conflict detection, state improvement, and subsequent adoption. Across eleven models, we observe frequent failures to adopt better states discovered during Aha moments, alongside adopted revisions that leave state quality unchanged or reduce it. Models also detect conflicts without subsequently achieving an effective revision. Further paired interventions suggest that prior reasoning context may hinder effective revision after conflict detection, while multi-branch search highlights answer selection as a bottleneck between correct-answer discovery and successful correction.
  </details>

- **[How Persona Conditioning Changes Open-Ended Task Execution: A Paired Evaluation of Behavioral Gains and Reliability Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31264)**  `arXiv:2611.31264`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yugyeong Ji, Changho Lee, Yerin Hwang, Taegwan Kang, Jinsik Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are often prompted with role- or persona-based instructions, yet persona conditioning has mainly been evaluated through character fidelity or aggregate benchmark accuracy. We study persona conditioning as a task-execution steering signal that changes how models interpret, structure, and sustain responses. Using paired instances derived from real user role-play logs and expanded into simulated multi-turn continuations, we compare with-persona and no-persona conditions while fixing task content, constraints, user simulation, and evaluation criteria. Across eight models, persona conditioning improves expert content depth and procedural fidelity, and these gains survive length control. At the same time, it lowers strict instruction-following accuracy by 17 to 30 points in every model evaluated, and increases unsupported real-world specificity through persona-induced elaboration. These results suggest that persona prompting is not a general-purpose performance improvement technique, but a selective task-execution policy shift with both behavioral gains and reliability risks.
  </details>

- **[RegioZH: Unveiling Comprehension Disparities across Regional Chinese Varieties in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31262)**  `arXiv:2611.31262`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Weihua Zheng, Wu Kui, Thong T. Doan, Lin Jingxia, AiTi Aw, Nancy F. Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current evaluations often treat Chinese varieties as monolithic, obscuring failures for regional users. We introduce RegioZH, a 2,607-item multiple-choice benchmark for regional lexical reasoning across Mainland China, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore, and Malaysia. Built by region-specific annotators and expert-validated, it targets localized idioms, slang, code-mixing, politeness conventions, and sociocultural expressions. Zero-shot results on eleven LLMs show large disparities, including a 29.4-point average accuracy gap between Taiwan and Singapore. Persona and structured chain-of-thought prompts for five models yield selective gains, suggesting that some errors stem from retrieval or framing rather than only missing knowledge. Representation, attention, calibration, and surface-feature analyses show a dissociation between marker attention and semantic grounding, overconfident forced-choice errors, and localized lexical density as a consistent difficulty predictor. Qualitative analysis finds terminology misunderstandings, sociocultural inference gaps, and cross-regional semantic divergence. RegioZH demonstrates that apparent Chinese proficiency can mask uneven understanding across regional varieties, motivating intra-language robustness evaluation alongside multilingual generalization.
  </details>

- **[Does Item Response Theory Generalize Across Languages? Evidence from Cross-Lingual LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31261)**  `arXiv:2611.31261`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruchira Dhar, Qiwei Peng, Anders Søgaard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The application of Item Response Theory (IRT) for data selection in benchmarks has mostly been limited to monolingual settings. In this work, we ask: how does IRT generalize when the same benchmark is translated across languages? We conduct experiments on Multilingual GSM and Global MMLU, covering over 100 language models and 11 languages. Fitting 1PL, 2PL, and 3PL IRT models per language, we assess cross-lingual consistency of parameters and whether IRT-filtered subsets preserve model rankings across languages: item difficulty emerges as the most stable parameter followed by guessing and then discrimination. A differential item analysis also shows that lack of stability can be tracked to specific items shifting under translation across languages. Our findings suggest IRT-based selection in multilingual settings must be both parameter-aware and item-aware, validating model choice and screening individual items before assuming cross-lingual generalization.
  </details>

- **[RecipeADQA: A User-aware Question-Answering Dataset for Multi-Constraint Recipe Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31259)**  `arXiv:2611.31259`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Li, Shuai Jiang, Sayaka Kamei, Yasuhiko Morimoto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Question-answering recipe recommendation tasks lack datasets with ground-truth labels. Existing methods often generate datasets from simple combinations of ingredient or tag constraints, leading to unnatural expressions and unreasonable constraints that are hard to reflect users' real preferences. This paper proposes a construction method for a benchmark-style QA dataset for natural-language recipe recommendation. Driven by real user behavior, our method integrates external user query and review data to extract fine-grained preference signals by LLMs and maps them onto a knowledge-graph-based tag schema, thereby constructing QA data consisting of preference and ingredient constraints. Besides, a greedy strategy for constraint selection is also used to control the answer size, while tag-based re-ranking is applied to improve data quality. The generated dataset RecipeADQA contains natural-language questions, structured constraints, and answer sets, resulting in 128,465 instances. Experiments compare RecipeADQA with baseline datasets from two perspectives: data quality and retrieval-recommendation performance. The results show that ours outperforms existing methods on the same recipe knowledge graph in coverage, higher question quality in naturalness and constraint rationality, and better queryability. Moreover, we design a noise-augmented retrieve-and-rerank model, in which a lightweight parser first retrieves candidate recipes, and a small language model then selects and ranks relevant recipes from a noisy candidate pool. The model trained on RecipeADQA outperforms the same model trained on the baseline datasets. The relevant construction code and data have been open-sourced at a GitHub repositorythis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RECAP: Training Long-horizon LLM Agents with Context Compaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31258)**  `arXiv:2611.31258`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yulun Jiang, Maria Brbic, Michael Moor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Context compaction enables large language model agents to tackle long-horizon tasks within limited context budgets by replacing interaction histories with compressed summaries. However, learning effective compaction jointly with the agent policy remains challenging, as summaries affect task success over many subsequent interactions. We introduce RECAP, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework based on the principle that a summary should preserve the behavior induced by the original interaction history. We formalize this principle with a behavior distortion objective that measures the divergence between the agent's subsequent behavior under compressed and original contexts. We show that the gradient of the distortion objective decomposes into two complementary learning signals: distortion-based reward for summary generation and self-distillation for subsequent actions. Experiments on Qwen3.5-4B show that RECAP consistently outperforms the task-reward-only RL baselines on both environments, leading to an absolute improvement of 4.6% and 4.3% on BrowseComp-plus and SWE-bench Verified, respectively. Ablations further highlight the value of jointly supervising summary generation and post-compaction behavior for RECAP.
  </details>

- **[Directly Maximizing On-policy Expected Accepted Length for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31256)**  `arXiv:2611.31256`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziying Zhang, Yaqing Wang, Yong Liu, Quanming Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by using a lightweight draft model to propose tokens for verification by a target model. Draft training commonly optimizes token-level distribution alignment, but this does not directly optimize the length of the accepted prefix: once a draft token is rejected, all subsequent draft tokens are discarded. We propose FreDraft, an on-policy training method that directly maximizes the expected accepted length of drafts sampled from the current draft model. This objective accounts for first-rejection truncation and induces prefix-aware credit assignment, weighting each token by its contribution to the acceptance of the current and subsequent tokens. To optimize the objective efficiently, we analytically marginalize token-level acceptance rate, using the target model's vocabulary distribution to reduce gradient variance. We further redistribute part of this distribution-level gradient into an explicit penalty for sampled tokens whose probabilities are overestimated by the draft model, while preserving the expected gradient. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and target models show that FreDraft improves accepted length and decoding speedup over strong baselines, achieving faster on-policy training convergence.
  </details>

- **[INCLUDE-2.0: Disentangling Knowledge Gaps in Multilingual LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31255)**  `arXiv:2611.31255`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Angelika Romanou, Maria Grandury, Clara Meister, Negar Foroutan, Jabez Magomere, Anna Sotnikova, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current multilingual benchmarks cover only a fraction of the languages represented in modern pretraining corpora and often conflate language proficiency with the ability to answer questions grounded in specific regions and communities. We introduce INCLUDE-2.0, a large-scale benchmark for region-aware multilingual factual evaluation, which covers 91 languages from 86 countries (127 total language--country pairs), comprising over 220K curated samples drawn from native regional sources. Unlike existing benchmarks that often treat language as a proxy for region, IINCLUDE-2.0 distinguishes between questions whose answers are independent of context, vary by country, concern widely documented facts about a place, or depend on knowledge primarily expressed through local linguistic and cultural sources. Each item is annotated with its regional knowledge type and domain, and paired with an English translation, enabling controlled analysis of the relationship between language proficiency and possession of regional knowledge.Evaluating frontier and open-weight models on IINCLUDE-2.0, we find that aggregate multilingual accuracy masks substantial variation across regional knowledge types, languages, and regions. We find that frontier models perform worse on locally embedded knowledge than on widely documented facts about regions, with an average drop of ~14.5 points. We also show that for open-weight models, scale improves accuracy but does not reliably make performance invariant to whether a question is asked in English or its original language. INCLUDE-2.0 is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[DAGGER: Deployment-Free Quality Estimation for Generative AI Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31252)**  `arXiv:2611.31252`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Dimitrios Liakopoulos, Neeraja J. Yadwadkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating the quality of generative AI workflows is challenging because these workflows comprise multiple stages, and errors can propagate across them, making end-to-end performance depend not only on the accuracy of individual models but also on how their outputs affect downstream stages. Each stage can use one of many candidate models. Estimating end-to-end workflow quality by deploying and evaluating every resulting model configuration is therefore prohibitively expensive. As a result, existing approaches face a tradeoff between estimation cost and accuracy: inexpensive methods often produce unreliable quality estimates, while accurate methods require deploying and evaluating many workflow configurations. In this paper, we introduce DAGGER, a framework for estimating the quality of generative AI workflows without exhaustive deployment and profiling. DAGGER represents a workflow as a graph, in which nodes correspond to stages and edges capture how outputs from one stage affect subsequent stages. It then decomposes workflow quality into per-node accuracy terms and per-edge dependency terms. Using a novel analytical formulation and SVD-based matrix completion, DAGGER estimates workflow quality from sparse observations without executing every candidate workflow. We evaluate DAGGER on three generative AI workflows across three datasets. DAGGER estimates workflow quality with a median error of 6.8 percentage points while reducing profiling cost by 10.2$\times$ relative to BenchPress, a state-of-the-art baseline, and selects workflow configurations competitive with state-of-the-art frameworks such as LLMSelector.
  </details>

- **[Grammar-as-a-Drafter: More Tokens per Speculative Decoding Step on Grammar-Constrained Structured Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28349)**  `arXiv:2611.28349`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _John Zhang, Silky Arora_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding speeds up generation by letting a lightweight drafter propose tokens that the target model verifies in one forward pass. Under structured outputs (JSON schemas, regular expressions, tool-call formats), the drafter works against a constraint it does not know, so its proposals for keys, delimiters, and indentation are often rejected or invalid, even though the grammar engine already knows the next tokens.We present Grammar-as-a-Drafter (GaaD), which makes the grammar a second drafter. Before each verify step, the scheduler merges the drafter's proposal with grammar-forced tokens (out-degree 1 in the grammar automaton) into one draft row of width $K$ (which can exceed the drafter's proposal length $K_M$): matching tokens are promoted to GRAMMAR, the proposal is truncated where it disagrees, and grammar tokens fill the remaining slots. The verify step accepts GRAMMAR drafts without computing their logits, composing with any drafter without adding kernels or compilation on the critical path. Because grammars fix text rather than tokens, accepting canonical GRAMMAR drafts with probability 1 preserves grammar validity but can shift the output distribution relative to grammar-masked decoding.We implement GaaD in vllm-torchtpu and an independent Rust serving runtime, and evaluate it on TPU v7x with MTP, DFlash, and n-gram drafters on a JSON character-profile benchmark. GaaD raises average acceptance length from 2.06 to 5.43 for MTP (Qwen3.5-0.8B; 1.99 to 5.31 on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B), 1.57 to 4.13 for DFlash, and 1.30 to 3.64 for n-gram (Qwen3-4B), decoding 2.66-2.81x faster at batch size 1 and 1.14-1.70x faster at batch size 64. In the Rust runtime, MTP with GaaD is 2.23x faster on Qwen3.5-0.8B and 2.02x on Qwen3.5-397B-A17B-FP8 (1.22-1.69x at high concurrency). Under mixed traffic, GaaD finishes workloads 1.03-1.23x sooner while modestly raising plain-text TPOT.
  </details>

- **[Exploring the Hidden Cost of the World Around AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38469)**  `arXiv:2609.38469`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Nusrat Jahan Lia, Shubhashis Roy Dipta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent benchmarks test what agents can do in environments that are built for their tasks. Deployed agents work in the real world where other people and tools can change the state. An agent booking a flight may receive a message from someone else to change the destination, or a booking tool may fail without saying whether the ticket was purchased. We present BACKDROP, which asks how much of an agent's capability in a clean world survives in a non-cooperative world. BACKDROP takes a task along with the agent's execution environment, and plants four everyday hazards in its environment, one at a time and all together. Each hazard tests a different behavior. Authority: does a non-author actor instruction, from anyone who did not author the task, override the user's goal? Injection: does text planted in a record redirect the agent? Boundary: does the agent make changes in an app the user's task did not require? Fault: after a write fails without saying whether it landed, does the agent check before it retries? Across 3,678 variants and 16 models, the average pass rate falls from 69.5% to 31.3% once all four hazards are present; the strongest models fall furthest (Claude Fable 5.1 from 96.6% to 56.0%). The gap is consistent throughout all 16 models. BACKDROP formalizes these gaps and shows how an agent's score in a task's world is a ceiling on real-world performance.
  </details>

- **[Linguistic and Argument Diversity in Synthetic Data for Function-Calling Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17829)**  `arXiv:2601.17829`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Dan Greenstein, Zohar Karnin, Chen Amiraz, Oren Somekh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The construction of function calling agents has emerged as a promising avenue for extending model capabilities. A major challenge for this task is obtaining high quality diverse data for training. Prior work emphasizes diversity in functions, invocation patterns, and interaction turns, yet linguistic diversity of requests and coverage of arguments (e.g., \texttt{city\_name}, \texttt{stock\_ticker}) remain underexplored. We propose a method that generates synthetic datasets via optimizing general-purpose diversity metrics across both queries and arguments, without relying on hand-crafted rules or taxonomies, making it robust to different usecases. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our technique via both intrinsic and extrinsic testing, comparing it to SoTA data generation methods. We show a superiority over baselines in terms of diversity, while keeping comparable correctness. Additionally, when used as a training set, the model resulting from our dataset exhibits superior performance compared to analogous models based on the baseline data generation methods in out-of-distribution performance. In particular, we achieve an $7.4\%$ increase in accuracy on the BFCL benchmark compared to similar counterparts.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Dimensional Prompt Chaining to Improve Open-Domain Dialogue Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01037)**  `arXiv:2601.01037`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Livia Leong Hui Teng, Jing Yang Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models (SLMs) offer significant deployment advantages but often struggle to match the dialogue quality of larger models in open-domain settings. In this paper, we propose a multi-dimensional prompt-chaining framework that integrates Naturalness, Coherence, and Engagingness dimensions to enhance human-likeness in open-domain dialogue generation. We apply the framework to two SLMs, TinyLlama and Llama-2-7B, and benchmark their performance against responses generated by substantially larger models, including Llama-2-70B and GPT-3.5 Turbo. We then employ automatic and human evaluation to assess the responses based on diversity, contextual coherence, as well as overall quality. Results show that the full framework improves response diversity by up to 29%, contextual coherence by up to 28%, and engagingness as well as naturalness by up to 29%. Notably, Llama-2-7B achieves performance comparable to substantially larger models, including Llama-2-70B and GPT-3.5 Turbo. Overall, the findings demonstrate that carefully designed prompt-based strategies provide an effective and resource-efficient pathway to improving open-domain dialogue quality in SLMs.
  </details>

- **[CoT-Proxy: Cross-Format Transfer for Distilling Long-Horizon Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33148)**  `arXiv:2611.33148`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dawid J. Kopiczko, Tijmen Blankevoort, Yuki M. Asano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning models become long-horizon agents through reinforcement learning or, more cheaply, through fine-tuning on a stronger teacher's trajectories, which contain the teacher's chain-of-thought as well as its actions. The standard fine-tuning approach imitates both, yet the strongest teachers are closed models whose chain-of-thought is hidden behind an API. We show that the chain-of-thought can be left out altogether and that the long-horizon policy can be distilled from the teacher's actions alone. The student is trained on these trajectories with its reasoning block left empty, while a KL loss on a replay buffer of its own generations limits drift from its initial model. Surprisingly, the policy learned from these trajectories with an empty reasoning block carries over to inference with thinking enabled, where the student reasons in its own style while acting like the teacher, an effect we call cross-format transfer. Distilling the actions of Qwen3.6-27B raises the task success of Qwen3-4B on AppWorld from 7% to 18% and that of Qwen3-32B from 12% to 25%. We further introduce CoT-proxies, short summaries of the teacher's reasoning placed after the empty reasoning block, which raise Qwen3-4B further, to 35%. Since neither actions nor CoT-proxies need the hidden chain-of-thought, the recipe applies unchanged to closed teachers. Distilling GPT-5.6 Luna from its visible outputs and the reasoning summaries its API returns raises Qwen3-4B from 7% to 48% on AppWorld and from 3% to 28% on SWE-bench Verified.
  </details>

- **[Leto: Utility-Centric Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33005)**  `arXiv:2611.33005`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shuguang Chen, Salman Paracha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents rely on external memory to retain and reuse information across interactions. Existing memory systems treat memory as a record of the past and evaluate it by fidelity to the conversation. However, the value of memory lies in whether it improves the agent's response. A memory system decides what to memorize and what to serve before that response exists, and fidelity checks neither decision against it. Faithful memory can therefore fail to improve the response. We instead view memory as a prediction problem and present \textbf{\emph{\leto{}}}, a memory system for LLM agents that focuses on the utility of memory to the future rather than on its fidelity to the past. Because future requests are unknown, \emph{\leto{}} preserves access to conversation details through memory indexing, allowing information to remain available through its sources. When a request arrives, memory replay draws on this index to anticipate and supply the context the agent needs beyond what the request explicitly states. The utility of the served context remains uncertain until the response is generated, motivating a utility loop that uses response outcomes to refine future memory selection. We evaluate our system against six memory systems on five benchmarks across three scenarios. In personalization, it outperforms the strongest compared memory system by 16.3 percentage points in accuracy on PrefEval and by 3.1 points in task completion on PERMA. For long-horizon tasks, it achieves the best overall performance on MemoryArena averaged over five environments with a gain that increases with the chain depth. In long-context understanding, it is on par with retrieval over the raw history while serving fewer context tokens.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Fixed-Context Likelihood: Zero-Shot AI-Generated Text Detection via Context Perturbation Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32778)**  `arXiv:2611.32778`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yifan Tang, Jingran Li, Yihao Wang, Zhen Yang, Jianyi Liu, Gongshen Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The same increase in token log probability from earlier context can be typical at one position and unusually large at another. We introduce Context Perturbation Discrepancy (CPD), a zero-shot detector that measures this gain relative to what a single pretrained language model expects. Keeping the passage fixed, CPD scores each observed token under its full prefix and under a short window of recent tokens. Under the full-prefix distribution, the expected gain equals the KL divergence from that distribution to the restricted-prefix distribution. CPD subtracts this expectation from each observed gain and scales the sum of the signed deviations by its analytical reference standard deviation. On unedited human and AI texts from GEN, DetectRL-X, and HART, subtracting the expected gain raises AUROC by 32.78-35.81 percentage points at the same variance scale. CPD outperforms seven zero-shot baselines, averaging 94.80% AUROC and 79.60% recall at a 5% false-positive rate. On original and edited HART passages, the same scores separate texts more clearly by draft origin than by the benchmark's final-expression labels (89.15% versus 65.50% AUROC). Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Social Bias Neurons in Multilingual Language Models: A Mechanistic Interpretability Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32685)**  `arXiv:2611.32685`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lance Calvin Lim Gamboa, Yue Feng, Mark Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual models exhibit social bias when processing inputs pertaining to societal stereotypes. We adapt emerging mechanistic interpretability methods to pinpoint and characterize model components that encode this bias. We conduct our analysis across three instruction-tuned models, four languages, and seven bias dimensions. Specifically, we leverage multilingual bias benchmarks in implementing two methods for identifying social bias neurons: activation probability entropy and integrated gap gradients. We find the latter method to be more effective, as the neurons it isolates (which are largely concentrated at the model's deepest layers) lead to sharper decreases in model bias when suppressed. Further analysis reveals neuron distribution patterns differ between high- and low-resourced languages, suggesting that a language's representation in pretraining data influences how its biases are represented within model internals. Our results also indicate that neurons linked to gender, nationality, and social class biases overlap the most with neurons related to other bias dimensions, pointing to the pivotal role these three dimensions play in how models learn and handle bias.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Latent Knowledge Stability in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32566)**  `arXiv:2611.32566`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chuancheng Shi, Shangze Li, Wenhua Wu, Zifeng Cheng, Pengyang Shao, Fei Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Changes in image viewpoint and question formulation can alter latent knowledge representations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, output correctness and overall representational drift provide limited insight into how these changes affect model decisions. We therefore evaluate latent knowledge stability across visual and linguistic elicitation conditions from two complementary perspectives: answer preference and internal support. Specifically, we devise the margin retention score (MRS), which measures the first-order projected degradation of the initial answer's logit margin over competing options across conditions and assesses how changes in latent knowledge representations affect this margin. We also present latent knowledge support-structure stability (KSS), which evaluates reference-correct items by comparing internal support profiles constructed using the margin gradient under the reference condition, which jointly reflects changes in how internal support is distributed and changes in total support magnitude. We further construct MMStableBench, comprising 19,452 knowledge items (facts), each probing the same latent knowledge across varied image viewpoints, semantically equivalent question formulations, and answer-option permutations. Experiments across 15 MLLMs show that MRS and KSS reveal latent knowledge instability not captured by accuracy, while an MRS-inspired regularizer further improves the generalization of edited latent knowledge.
  </details>

- **[Avalon-M1: Reinforcement Learning from Collective Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32539)**  `arXiv:2611.32539`  `cs.CL`  
  _Iliyan Zarov, Henry Course, Anthony Hartshorn, Ran Achiron, Louis Roche, Akshit Sinha, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce reinforcement learning from collective rewards (RLCR), a recipe for jointly training language models to act as agent organisations under latency constraints. A CEO and up to eight specialists share one policy, co-ordinate through a message board and workspace, and are trained with a common outcome-derived advantage. The deadline makes parallel work valuable when it increases the return earned before the cap. We train Avalon-M1-Mini (120B) across 147 environments. For the trained model, eight-hire organisational execution raises IMO-ProofBench-Advanced scores from 23.8% to 39.0% relative to single-agent execution. Across 12 benchmarks, organisations achieve similar average scores at approximately one-third the derived latency of token-matched single-agent execution and just over half that of eight independent attempts followed by selection. The trained model also outperforms Nemotron 3 Super, post-trained from the same base model, across all evaluations when comparing each model's best tested configuration. On EdgeBench, organisations overtake the single agent after approximately 0.4 derived hours. Over the course of training, organisations develop hierarchies to manage communication costs, invest more in peer review, and alter how work is divided. Safety evaluations find near-universal refusal of harmful actions but sustained declines in external reporting in two of three oversight scenarios. These results establish a shared-advantage baseline for studying organisational learning, performance-latency trade-offs, and collective oversight.
  </details>

- **[When Ink Meets Pixel: Intent-Aware OCR with VLMs for Human-Corrected Financial Documents When Ink Meets Pixel: Intent-Aware OCR with VLMs for Human-Corrected Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32446)**  `arXiv:2611.32446`  `cs.CL`  
  _Akshata A Bhat, Daman Deep Singh, Niyati Chhaya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human annotations on documents (such as arrows, carets, strikethroughs, and insertions) express corrective intent yet existing OCR and document understanding systems treat these marks as visual noise rather than semantic edit operations. We introduce intent-aware OCR, the task of generating corrected transcriptions by jointly reasoning over original document content and visual intent cues (handwritten or digital) that convey edit intent. To support this setting, we present DocIntent, a multimodal dataset of 1,600 financial documents with over 5,700 annotated visual intent cues, each paired with layout-aware, intent-resolved OCR targets. We further introduce IntentBench, a 216-document benchmark designed to evaluate model robustness under diverse real-world annotation conditions. Leveraging DocIntent, we fine-tune DocIntentOCR, a suite of 3B and 7B parameter vision-language models, under direct intent supervision on corrected transcriptions. On IntentBench, the fine-tuned 3B and 7B models achieve F1-scores of 0.81 and 0.83, respectively, substantially outperforming commercial closed-source (e.g., GPT-4.1 (0.626)), and open-source models (e.g., LLaMA-3.2-Vision-90B (0.50)) demonstrating that intent-aware supervision enables compact domain-specific models to outperform substantially larger general-purpose systems on this task. This work establishes intent-aware OCR as a novel human-in-the-loop document understanding task and provides foundational resources (dataset, benchmark, models) to advance research in interactive document processing.
  </details>

- **[SkillCore: Internalizing Generalizable Skills via Scaffolding-Removed Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32246)**  `arXiv:2611.32246`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yi Liu, Changqing Ai, Peijie Dong, Xiankun Lin, Yongkang Yang, Zhezheng Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Runtime skills can improve agents' problem-solving capabilities by providing procedural guidance for tasks such as code review and debugging. However, these benefits reach only a minority of users, as most do not proactively invoke skills in practice. We argue that such generalizable procedures should become part of the model's capabilities rather than remain optional external add-ons, making them directly available without explicit skill loading. To this end, we present SkillCore, a data pipeline and supervised fine-tuning recipe for internalizing generalizable skills. We transform real agent trajectories by removing skill-loading scaffolding while preserving the task context and subsequent agent actions, thereby supervising direct execution without external skill content. To preserve access to task-specific skills that provide genuinely external information, we mix these scaffold-removed trajectories with a small proportion of skill-native trajectories that preserve supervision for skill invocation. On a held-out skill-free evaluation, SkillCore improves task completion quality over the base Qwen3-8B and Qwen2.5-72B models by 54.4% and 24.8%, respectively, while preserving task-specific skill invocation on a separate probe and general coding performance on SWE-bench Verified.
  </details>

- **[SecondChance: Benchmarking Reliable Error Recovery in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31586)**  `arXiv:2611.31586`  `cs.CL`  
  _Atahan Dokme, Adar Avsian, Larry Heck_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents make errors while they act, and their reliability depends on what they do afterwards. Existing recovery benchmarks mostly inject faults, involve no user and score whether the task is eventually completed, so they cannot show whether an agent undid its own silent error, told the user, or faced an error that could not be fixed. We introduce SecondChance, an error checkpoint dataset and evaluation harness that profiles and measures error-recovery behaviour of AI agents. The errors are natural: they come from agents' own task attempts in $\tau^2$-bench, BFCL and AppWorld. Different agents then resume from the same checkpoints under one of four conditions: no assistance, an error notice, the error location, or repair guidance. We measure error correction, task success, policy compliance, preservation of environment state, repair durability and cost. We evaluate 12 models under all four conditions in 5,760 continuations. Recovery is rare: across its environments, the best model completes only 16% of recoveries unaided and 27% with repair guidance. Correcting is not recovering: with repair guidance, Llama-3.3-70B corrects 26% of $\tau^2$ errors but completes none, and 40 of 84 successful AppWorld continuations leave the error in place. SecondChance exposes these differences that task success alone cannot capture.
  </details>

- **[PazaBench: Large-Scale ASR Benchmark for Low-Resource Languages in Africa](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31578)**  `arXiv:2611.31578`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mercy Muchai, Nick Mumero Mwangi, Kevin Chege, Samuel Chege Maina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-resource languages remain underrepresented in ASR benchmarks, limiting the ability to reliably assess and compare model performance. We present a large-scale benchmark for automatic speech recognition (ASR) on low-resource languages in Africa. The benchmark harmonizes six public corpora into a unified, reproducible evaluation framework and evaluates 52 state-of-the-art ASR models spanning three model architectural paradigms across 39 African languages on diverse datasets. Models are evaluated using standard accuracy and efficiency metrics, word error rate (WER), character error rate (CER), and inverse real-time factor (RTFx). This benchmark introduces architecture-aware comparison that systematically analyzes both individual models and broader model families. In addition, we quantify linguistic variance across language families, revealing how these factors shape ASR performance across diverse African languages. We also provide a comprehensive accuracy and efficiency trade-off analysis directly relevant to deployment in low-resource settings. Together, the benchmark, evaluation pipeline, and results support transparent, reproducible, and deployment-aware evaluation of ASR models for low-resource languages.
  </details>

- **[Offline-First Quantized AI: Design and Evaluation of an On-Device Maternal-Health Assistant for Low-Connectivity Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20012)**  `arXiv:2611.20012`  `cs.CL`  
  _Okenla AbdulQahhar Abimbola, Maurice Abraham John, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Omolaiye Samuel, Oko-Oba Sandra Chioma, Owoo Oluwamayowa Emmanuel, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline language models could extend access to maternal-health information where internet connectivity is unreliable, but deployment requires both clinically appropriate responses and feasible local execution. This study evaluated an Android assistant using ready-made quantized models, a clinically validated local knowledge package, conversation history, and optional local retrieval. Gemma 4 E2B and Qwen3.5 0.8B were compared on 45 conversations and 312 turns per configuration. Gemma retained context more consistently, whereas Qwen generated faster and used less memory. Clinician assessment of 150 Gemma responses across nine clinical domains yielded a mean quality score of 3.80/4, with 147 responses meeting the acceptance criterion. In 74 matched RAG-on/off questions, 11 RAG-on answers improved, 52 were comparable, four were degraded by returned content, and seven were lower-rated without clear evidence that retrieval caused the difference. Full-cohort mean differences were small and uncertain. In a sensitivity analysis excluding flagged cases, RAG-on had descriptively higher mean usefulness, safety, correctness, and actionability in the conditional 70-pair subset excluding the four flagged pairs from both conditions. Physical- device testing demonstrated offline responses on a 6 GB Android handset, with a 14.3 s median latency, alongside successful reconnection synchronization in the integrated application. Together, the findings characterize the integrated offline maternal-health assistant within the designed and tested benchmark, retrieval pipeline, and handset conditions.
  </details>

- **[GEPA-Curator: Curated Learning for Self Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37651)**  `arXiv:2610.37651`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zack Yu, Nicholas Lee, Minjae Lee, Lakshya A Agrawal, Luca Manolache, Wonjun Kang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in multi-turn agentic workloads that require sustained interaction with complex environments. Existing reflective prompt optimizers such as GEPA learn from failed rollouts, but agentic failures can also come from prompt weaknesses, harness limitations, stochastic execution, or noisy judges. We argue that such failures may not provide useful learning signal, and may even be harmful; and thus it is essential to curate which tasks are provided to the prompt optimizer for better performance. We introduce GEPA-Curator, a framework that augments the prompt optimizer to select which agent experiences should shape prompt optimization. In particular, GEPA-Curator consists of two components: task curation, which filters out tasks that are likely to provide noisy or unreliable learning signals, and prior success retrieval, which selectively retrieves past successful trajectories for a task and provides them as additional context to the prompt optimizer. We evaluate GEPA-Curator on GoBrowse, ALFWorld, AppWorld, and $\tau$-bench, using both Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5 as optimizer models. GEPA-Curator matches or exceeds the mean performance of both baselines, with gains of up to 20.7% over GEPA and 21.2% over ACE.
  </details>

- **[BabelArena: A Large-Scale Multilingual Benchmark for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23490)**  `arXiv:2609.23490`  `cs.CL`  
  _Peng Kuang, Yuchun Fan, Jiangnan Li, Minghao Wu, Jialong Tang, Hao-Ran Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute multi-step workflows through tool use and interaction with users and environments. However, current agent evaluations are largely English-centric, limiting our understanding of agent capabilities in multilingual settings. We introduce BabelFlow, a benchmark-general agentic workflow that adapts existing agent benchmarks to new languages by analyzing runtime dependencies, coordinating structure-preserving translation, and combining multi-layer verification with human review to preserve task and evaluation semantics. Using BabelFlow, we construct BabelArena, a task-aligned benchmark comprising 16,146 instances derived from 702 canonical tasks across four benchmark families, 13 domains, and 23 languages. Experiments with five frontier models show that no single model dominates across benchmark families and that cross-language disparities extend well beyond task success. Lower-resource languages exhibit distinct failure patterns, with larger shares of tool-use and control-flow errors rather than answer-quality errors alone, pointing to gaps in reliable task execution across the resource levels of these languages. On the same tasks, agents in low-resource languages also consume substantially more tokens than in English (up to roughly twice the input) without proportional increases in interaction length, and language consistency degrades further on tasks requiring structured output, where switches are directed overwhelmingly toward English. We believe BabelArena provides a foundation for advancing research on reliable and efficient multilingual agents.
  </details>

- **[Multiscale Aggregated Hierarchical Attention (MAHA): A Constrained and Consensus-Based Approach to Efficient Contextual Modeling in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.14925)**  `arXiv:2512.14925`  `cs.CL`  
  _Caner Erden_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The quadratic computational complexity of Multi-Head Self-Attention (MHSA) remains a fundamental bottleneck in scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) for long-context tasks. While sparse and linearized attention mechanisms attempt to mitigate this, they often compromise the representation of global dependencies or fail to capture multiscale semantic granularity effectively. In this paper, we propose Multiscale Aggregated Hierarchical Attention (MAHA), a novel architectural framework that reformulates the attention mechanism through hierarchical decomposition and explicitly constrained aggregation. Unlike conventional approaches that treat token interactions at a single resolution, MAHA dynamically partitions the input sequence into hierarchical scales via learnable downsampling operators. The aligned scale outputs are combined either by softmax-normalized weights on the probability simplex or by a fixed number of differentiable consensus updates based on scale-to-consensus discrepancy. The latter is a game-inspired update and is not claimed to compute a Nash equilibrium. Implemented within a hybrid dilated-convolutional transformer backbone, MAHA enables end-to-end training. With four compressed scales and a compression ratio of two, MAHA requires 66.8% fewer attention-score computations than full-resolution attention at every sequence length (5.57M versus 16.8M per head at N=4096), and single-run benchmark experiments indicate downstream performance competitive with standard attention. This work offers a scalable multiscale solution for next-generation LLMs.
  </details>

- **[EEG-MedRAG: Enhancing EEG-based Clinical Decision-Making via Hierarchical Hypergraph Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13735)**  `arXiv:2508.13735`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lu Meng, Zan Li, Yi Wang, Yi Ding, Ziyue Zhu, Fang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the widespread application of electroencephalography (EEG) in neuroscience and clinical practice, efficiently retrieving and semantically interpreting large-scale, multi-source, heterogeneous EEG data has become a pressing challenge. EEG-MedRAG addresses this challenge through a three-layer hypergraph-based retrieval-augmented generation framework that unifies EEG domain knowledge, individual patient cases, and large-scale EEG recordings into a traversable n-ary relational hypergraph, enabling joint semantic-temporal retrieval and evidence-grounded clinical reasoning. The accompanying benchmark is the first to pair EEG questions about the same clinical fact across five user roles. Its 700 questions span seven clinical domains and support direct comparisons of answer consistency across roles. Across three LLM backbones, EEG-MedRAG outperforms all four baselines in both overall answer correctness and token-level F1. It achieves up to 86.14% answer correctness on the full benchmark and 97.32% on non-EEG questions, with 97.41% evidence coverage on knowledge questions. In EEG retrieval, EEG-MedRAG improves top-five label agreement by 8.58 percentage points over the time-series baseline. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Perception Is Enough, at the Right Scale: Scale-Adaptive Token Pruning for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31954)**  `arXiv:2611.31954`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Yifeng Lin, Liuxiang Qiu, Yunfeng Chen, Tiesong Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models must process continuous streams of visual observations, and the resulting computational cost limits real-time robotic control. Token pruning reduces this cost, but a fixed semantic scale of perceptual representation can overlook information needed at different stages of manipulation. We show that perceptual semantics can guide effective token selection without relying on internal attention when the selection scale adapts to the scene's evolving semantic structure. Based on this observation, we introduce TauPrune-VLA, a training-free method that controls the scale of the retained perceptual representation through a single perception temperature $\tau$. To adapt this scale as manipulation proceeds, TauPrune-VLA updates $\tau$ online using trends in perceptual semantics, with robot-state changes and action magnitude regulating the adjustment strength. While these semantics evolve over the course of manipulation, they remain relatively stable between consecutive frames. This continuity allows retention indices computed from the current observation to be applied at the next policy invocation, enabling token selection to run asynchronously with inference and reducing pruning overhead. Evaluated across diverse VLA models and simulation benchmarks, TauPrune-VLA achieves up to a $1.86\times$ inference speedup while maintaining near-baseline task success rates. We further validate its effectiveness on two real-world robotic platforms.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Open-Ended Multi-Agent Coordination in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08340)**  `arXiv:2606.08340`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Kale-ab Abebe Tessera, Andras Szecsenyi, Cameron Barker, Alexander Rutherford, Davide Paglieri, Aidan Scannell, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, they must coordinate with others over long horizons in open-ended interactive tasks. Yet existing evaluations rarely test these demands together, focusing instead on single-agent tasks, short interactions, or highly structured multi-agent settings. We introduce $alem$, a JAX-based benchmark for open-ended multi-agent coordination built on Craftax-like dynamics. Alem embeds procedurally generated coordination tasks, soft specialisation, communication, and controllable coordination difficulty into a long-horizon survival world with exploration, crafting, trading, and combat. We evaluate $13$ LLMs zero-shot within homogeneous teams, with trained MARL agents as reference points. Current LLM agents remain far from solving alem, averaging only ~6% normalised return, but their failures are not uniform. On the hardest coordination setting, zero-shot Gemini-3.1-Pro-High approaches MARL agents trained for one billion steps, while GPT-5.4-High achieves strong base-task reward but much lower coordination reward. This contrast shows that individual task competence does not imply coordination competence. Further experiments reveal that communication is the largest contributor to coordination, while memory and reasoning help when used to maintain multi-step plans. In heterogeneous teams, however, combining different models improves base-task progress but lowers coordination reward relative to their homogeneous counterparts. Overall, our results identify coordination as a distinct bottleneck for frontier LLM agents, separate from single-agent capabilities. Alem makes this bottleneck measurable and provides a controlled testbed for developing agents that communicate, allocate roles, and execute shared plans. Code is available atthis https URL, with LLM traces and a leaderboard atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can AI Design a Better nnU-Net? A Hypothesis-Driven Architecture Discovery Loop from Failures to Design Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32384)**  `arXiv:2611.32384`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Songtao Jiang, Yuan Wang, Fucheng Niu, Hualiang Wang, Jian Wu, Zuozhu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural architecture search and LLM-driven evolution often reduce evaluation to a scalar fitness score, discarding failures that could explain why designs succeed or fail. We present a hypothesis-driven architecture discovery loop in which LLM agents diagnose structured error patterns, formulate falsifiable hypotheses, test counterfactual controls under a frozen nnU-Net pipeline, and distill validated mechanisms into executable trigger-to-action rules. Across eight search waves, the loop identifies two principles for 3D medical image segmentation. First, reallocating computation from high-resolution decoders to the mid-encoder preserves thin-anatomy boundaries while reducing cost. Second, support-confined region contrast addresses errors between nested anatomical compartments, with anatomy-dependent triggers selecting when the rule applies. These principles produce LeanNeXt (0.78 MACs and 0.92 training-step time relative to nnU-Net; +1.63 Dice points on four-fold KiTS23 cross-validation) and trigger-routed LeanNeXt+. The latter improves all 15 evaluated CT/MRI settings over nnU-Net and exceeds the 3.3 larger ResEnc-M on 12 of 15 benchmarks at 0.30 its parameter count. Relative to random sampling, one-shot LLM generation, and score-selected agent search, the loop shows how failure analysis can yield reusable design rules across modalities.
  </details>

- **[Conservative Overlap Fuzzy Rough Sets for Incomplete Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31329)**  `arXiv:2611.31329`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Trung Hieu Vuong, Van Thien Nguyen, Nhu Son Nguyen, Thanh Dai Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attribute reduction in incomplete information systems is difficult because missing values break the equivalence-class structure of rough set theory. We propose the Conservative Overlap Fuzzy Rough Set (COFRS) model. Each object is described by its set of present attributes. The similarity of two objects is a weighted overlap on the attributes present in both, divided by the fixed number of attributes |A| instead of the size of their union, so missing values are penalised globally and are not matched as wildcards. From this similarity we define a soft dependency degree based on weighted granule consistency, and we search for reducts by greedy backward elimination in O(|U|^2 |A|^2) time. We show that the relation is symmetric and non-reflexive, that the dependency degree is not monotone, and we give a sufficient condition under which monotonicity holds. On 11 UCI benchmarks with missing rates from 0.15% to 20.46%, COFRS gives the smallest reducts on average (4.1 attributes, against 5.0-11.2 for Tolerance-RS, a range-threshold variant Tol-RS_tau, Zhou-RS and the min-aggregation variant COFRS-min). The difference is significant against Tol-RS_tau, Zhou-RS and COFRS-min (Holm-adjusted Wilcoxon p<0.01) but not against Tolerance-RS (p=0.56), and it is small against the Jensen-Shen selector (adjusted p=0.084). At equal reduct size, COFRS selects more informative attributes than random subsets. With a kNN classifier the methods do not differ significantly in accuracy, F1 or G-mean; with an RBF-SVM they do, and COFRS ranks last. The gain is therefore compactness, not predictive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CADFather: Autonomous CAD Reconstruction through Coordinated Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09127)**  `arXiv:2610.09127`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Gennadiy Savrasov, Maksim Elistratov, Nikita Gavrilov, Albert Garifullin, Oleg Pavlov, Soslan Kabisov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing an editable CAD model from a 3D shape remains a challenging engineering task. Existing methods can propose CAD operations, but no single source of proposals works equally well across different part geometries and stages of reconstruction. We introduce CADFather, an autonomous agentic system that coordinates complementary tools to recover parametric CAD programs from 3D meshes. A vision-language assistant inspects renders of the target and intermediate reconstructions, then decides which candidate CAD programs to extend, which tools to invoke, how many proposals to generate, and when to finish. Learned and algorithmic tools propose CAD operations, while numerical optimization refines the parameters of existing programs. Proposed or refined programs are executed and evaluated to provide feedback for subsequent decisions. The agent maintains alternative candidate programs for each target part and preserves the best valid result throughout reconstruction. CADFather uses pretrained generation and assistant models without additional training. We evaluate reconstruction quality and execution validity on the full DeepCAD, Fusion360, and MCB test sets, as well as on CADENA-Bench, CADBench, and BenchCAD. We additionally analyze computational cost and the trade-off between cost and reconstruction quality.
  </details>

- **[SkillBoost: A Constrained Exploration-Exploitation Process for Mitigating Skill Overfitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26643)**  `arXiv:2607.26643`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hongqiang Lin, Chao Liu, Xiaofan Bai, Xuan Jin, Yuhong Li, Nenggan Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enabling large language model (LLM) agents to accumulate and reuse experience from past interactions remains a central challenge in real-world applications. A promising solution is to treat skills as trainable states and optimize them in the same way as model parameters in neural network training. However, data-driven skill optimization is prone to overfitting to the limited trajectories collected from real environments. Overexploiting these trajectories overfits the current batch, while unconstrained exploration causes regression on previously solved cases. This tension motivates a constrained search view of skill self-evolution, governed by an exploration--exploitation trade-off. We propose SkillBoost, a three-stage framework that mitigates both risks: structured exploitation localizes observed failures to editable skill components, prior-guided exploration draws on prior knowledge in the LLM to generate diverse repair candidates, and verified acceptance commits a candidate only when it improves performance within a regression bound. Experiments across 23 model--benchmark configurations show that SkillBoost achieves state-of-the-art performance while mitigating overfitting, outperforming both human-crafted and LLM-generated skills. Notably, it matches or even surpasses the performance gains typically achieved through post-training. Furthermore, transfer experiments further show that optimized skills can be reused by other agents on similar tasks. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Repairing Reward Functions with Feedback to Mitigate Reward Hacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.13036)**  `arXiv:2510.13036`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Stephane Hatgis-Kessell, Logan Mondal Bhamidipaty, Emma Brunskill_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human-designed reward functions for reinforcement learning (RL) agents are frequently misaligned with the humans' true, unobservable objectives, and thus act only as proxies. Optimizing for a misspecified proxy reward function often induces reward hacking, resulting in a policy misaligned with the human's true objectives. An alternative is to perform RL from human feedback, which involves learning a reward function from scratch by collecting human preferences over pairs of trajectories. However, building such datasets is costly. To address the limitations of both approaches, we propose Preference-Based Reward Repair (PBRR): an automated iterative framework that repairs a human-specified proxy reward function by learning an additive, transition-dependent correction term from preferences. A manually specified reward function can yield policies that are highly suboptimal under the ground-truth objective, yet corrections on only a few transitions may suffice to recover optimal performance. To identify and correct for those transitions, PBRR uses a targeted exploration strategy and a new preference-learning objective. We prove in tabular domains PBRR has a cumulative regret that matches, up to constants, that of prior preference-based RL methods. In addition, on a suite of reward-hacking benchmarks, PBRR consistently outperforms baselines that learn a reward function from scratch from preferences or modify the proxy reward function using other approaches, requiring substantially fewer preferences to learn high performing policies.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Static Charts: Benchmarking Interactive Chart-to-Code Generation for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31345)**  `arXiv:2611.31345`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuhan Bian, Haoyu Liu, Siyu Li, Hanpei Guo, Jingrong Gao, Zhixu Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved significant progress in chart-to-code generation. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on static charts, overlooking interactive charts that enable richer and more expressive data exploration. Unlike static chart-to-code generation, which emphasizes visual consistency, interactive chart-to-code evaluation targets behavioral consistency, requiring chart states to evolve correctly under sequences of user interactions, thereby rendering traditional one-step evaluation paradigms ineffective. To address this gap, we introduce ChartInt, the first benchmark specifically designed for interactive chart-to-code generation. ChartInt consists of 2,779 carefully annotated examples covering common interaction patterns such as hover, highlight, and tooltips. Additionally, ChartInt incorporates editing tasks such as data updates, style adjustments, and cross-chart style transfer to expand real-world application scenarios. To enable reliable evaluation of interaction behavior, we propose a preset state snapshot protocol that predefines target interaction states and deterministically triggers interaction events, transforming dynamic behaviors into reproducible evaluation targets. Based on this protocol, we design a multi-dimensional evaluation framework measuring semantic and stylistic consistency. Experiments show that while current MLLMs perform well on static charts, their performance drops significantly on interactive tasks, highlighting the need for interaction-aware benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory from User Traffic for Graphic Design Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22086)**  `arXiv:2609.22086`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Hongyang Du, Lan Yan, Christian Flores, Asim Kadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed software agents face a continuous stream of user requests while their foundation-model weights remain frozen between releases. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) via external memory offers a practical path to continual adaptation, yet prior methods largely assume programmatic verifiers, cheap retries, or a single global prompt. Creative software breaks these assumptions, as open-ended visual briefs lack ground truth, VLM judges are noisy, and multi-step tool executions are costly and confounded by external assets. We present Designer-RSI, which evolves a modular skill bank from unlabeled design trajectories for an agent spanning over 230 raster, vector, and layout tools.
