Designer-RSI couples widening (acquiring new skills for recurring capability gaps) with deepening (revising failing skills by contrasting successful and failed runs), and screens updates through a Pareto-style matched replay gate that requires strict gains on sampled briefs without local regressions. Across held-out product briefs, eight external benchmarks, and two backbones, traffic-driven evolution outperforms static documentation skills, lifts execution success up to 99.8%, improves requirement fulfillment, and reaches up to a 67.6% blind win rate over the base agent. Ablations further show that skill acquisition and revision are strongly synergistic: neither suffices alone, and reliable gains require evolving coverage and execution quality together.
  </details>

- **[Learning What vs. Learning How: Decomposing Task and Interface Competence in Agentic Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31427)**  `arXiv:2611.31427`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Chaeeun Lee, Hossein A. Rahmani, Aryo Pradipta Gema, Kexin Xu, Emine Yilmaz, Pasquale Minervini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents operate through harnesses that define system prompts, action formats, feedback loops, and resource constraints. A model may possess the knowledge needed to solve a task yet fail to express that knowledge through a particular harness. This raises a basic question: when a policy fails under a given harness, is it missing task knowledge or the competence to operate the interface? We distinguish two components of agentic competence: task competence, the knowledge and reasoning needed to solve a task, and interface competence, the ability to act on that knowledge through a given harness. Across four reasoning and agentic benchmarks, we show that harness-conditioned training yields vastly different outcomes depending on how these interact. Multi-harness distillation aggregates task knowledge from trajectories collected through different harnesses into a shared policy, but this knowledge is realised only when training preserves or improves competence on the inference-time interface. Otherwise, single-harness training can induce interference in interface competence, sometimes catastrophically collapsing performance on a held-out harness despite improvements in underlying task knowledge. To disentangle task and interface supervision, we introduce harness rehearsal: distillation on traces that carry minimal task knowledge while exercising the target interface. Cross-harness task supervision or rehearsal alone yields little gain, but their combination yields gains far larger than either: on BFCL V3 multi-turn with Qwen3-4B, they achieve 8.5% and 32.5% respectively, versus 58.8% together. Finally, on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is more robust than distillation to single-harness training: it does not substantially degrade performance on other harnesses, and interface competence acquired through multi-harness distillation persists through subsequent RL.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Judges Catch Unsafe Clinical Actions? A Fault-Injection Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33012)**  `arXiv:2611.33012`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Neda Ghafouri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As clinical AI agents begin to interact with electronic health records (EHRs), large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate their actions. But how reliably can these judges recognize clinically dangerous mistakes, especially when an agent appears to have completed its task successfully? We investigate this question using MedAgentBench by introducing controlled clinical errors into verified agent trajectories. The experiments include 416 faulty trajectories, eight error categories, and six open-weight LLM judges. By comparing each faulty trajectory with its correct counterpart, we measure whether judges genuinely recognize errors rather than simply flagging more actions as unsafe. The findings show that access to clinical evidence significantly improves judge reliability. When shown only the final answer, judges cannot distinguish errors that leave it unchanged, while flagging 56% of correct trajectories. With full trajectory information, the ability to distinguish unsafe actions from correct ones reaches a score of 0.75, and providing actual EHR responses improves discrimination by 0.39. Surprisingly, including the agent's reasoning does not improve overall performance and reduces detection of incorrectly reported clinical values by 0.18, which remain the hardest errors to detect. MedGemma performed worse than the general-purpose model and was the only judge to miss potentially dangerous medication overdoses. We also find that the benchmark's original grader accepts all 38 trajectories with an intentionally skipped laboratory check, while combining LLM judges with a rule-based checker informed by the full EHR record detects 97% of injected faults.
  </details>

- **[InstructReplay: Replaying Assistant Turns to Preserve General Behavior During Factual Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32918)**  `arXiv:2611.32918`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaofeng Shi, Qian Kou, Hua Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning an instruction-tuned language model on new facts often erodes its general abilities. We find that standard fine-tuning lowers instruction-following, reasoning and coding benchmarks by 35 points on average, and that these losses track a collapse in answer length: fine-tuned models at least halve their responses, and the further a response drifts from the initial model's length, the more often it fails. We propose InstructReplay: the initial model answers a set of general instructions once, and during fine-tuning the model is kept close to the initial model on these answers. Across three model families and two domains, InstructReplay reduces the average decline on five general benchmarks from 28.1 to 2.4 points and keeps answer length close to the initial model's, while learning the new facts as well as standard fine-tuning. It balances learning and retention better than seven adaptation methods in all six settings. Controlled comparisons attribute the gain to replaying the model's own answers to general instructions rather than to the form of the loss.
  </details>

- **[Marketing-Bench: Evaluating Frontier Models and Agents on Real-World Marketing Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32012)**  `arXiv:2611.32012`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sumanth Balaji, Siddharth Gupta, Ameet Deshpande, Vishvak Murahari, Karthik Narasimhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As frontier AI models and agents are deployed more broadly in the real world, marketing is emerging as one of their largest applications. Marketing teams use AI models to produce brand assets like website copy and social media posts, while consumers' agents autonomously discover products, research purchases, and navigate websites. However, evaluating marketing capability is difficult because unlike areas like software development, customer support, or legal review where ground truth is more objectively defined, marketing is subjective, and output quality is judged based on taste and brand. We introduce Marketing-Bench, the first open-source marketing benchmark, which pairs discriminative tasks (detecting brand anomalies) with generative tasks (remediation), while keeping answers verifiable. Marketing-Bench tests five key pillars: factuality, consistency, coherence, structure, and brand voice, all crucial elements for good brand content on the web. We find that frontier models are far from solving the benchmark: the average model corrects only about half of the introduced issues (53.1%), whereas a marketing expert corrects nearly all of them. Even the strongest, GPT-6 Astra, reaches only 74.8%, with Claude Opus and Fable, Gemini Argon, and GPT-6 Sol falling well short. These findings suggest that marketing capabilities are less mature than adoption implies, and that principled evaluation should precede further scaling of usage. Marketing-Bench is a first step toward "frontier marketing models" with superhuman marketing performance.
  </details>

- **[TableRouter: Query-Aware Evidence Construction for Long-Table Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32006)**  `arXiv:2611.32006`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Anahita Bolourani, Xiaofeng Lin, Tung Sum Thomas Kwok, Guang Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs can reason over tables, but when tables outgrow the context budget, retrieving relevant rows is only part of the solution. A retrieved subset may omit information required for an exact aggregate; even complete evidence may remain difficult for an LLM to use. We introduce TableRouter, a training-free framework that analyzes each query, then chooses between two routes: a computation route, which derives question-conditioned statistics from the full table, and a coverage route, which retrieves relevant rows and column summaries. The chosen context is passed to a frozen LLM in one call. Across three LLMs and three large-table benchmarks, TableRouter outperforms the strongest of six single-call baselines in all nine settings by 1.2-18.3 accuracy points, using 77-95% fewer estimated FLOPs than truncated-table prompting, with indexing amortized across questions. The gains extend to long tables from databases not used in earlier experiments, where TableRouter achieves 35.8% with Qwen2.5-7B, versus 16.4% for the strongest external baseline. A controlled experiment shows that explicitly providing computed statistics improves accuracy by up to 40.0 points, even when all answer-determining rows are available. These results distinguish evidence access from evidence use: long-table reasoning depends on how information is represented, not only on which rows are retrieved.
  </details>

- **[What People Choose Not to Say: A Multimodal Benchmark for Fine-Grained Information Manipulation in Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31346)**  `arXiv:2611.31346`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhan Bian, Zhaokai Liang, Jia Wang, Haoyu Liu, Yu Chen, Jianfeng Qu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in MLLMs have improved social understanding, enabling models to recognize emotions and distinguish truthful from deceptive behaviors. However, existing benchmarks largely focus on coarse-grained emotion recognition or binary truth-deception detection, overlooking the diverse ways people manipulate information in conversations, such as withholding, leakage, fabrication, evasion, and defensive counterclaim. Understanding these behaviors requires reasoning about underlying facts, participant knowledge, and conversational context, making them difficult to evaluate reliably. To address this gap, we introduce InfoCon, a multimodal benchmark for fine-grained information manipulation in conversations. InfoCon identifies six fine-grained strategies and derives a coarse consistency label from their factual and interactional relations. We develop an evidence-grounded multi-agent annotation framework to construct and verify reliable labels from rich contextual evidence. We benchmark representative multimodal models and systematically analyze their fine-grained information manipulation understanding, providing a testbed beyond binary truth-deception judgments.
  </details>

- **[LLM-based Personalized Travel Planner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25531)**  `arXiv:2611.25531`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Amandi Nimasha, Oshadha Wijerathne, Dushan Fernando, Nisansa de Silva, Srinath Perera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective travel planning requires handling multiple information sources, managing constraints, and tailoring recommendations to user preferences. While LLMs have introduced dynamic, context-aware decision-making, single-agent models often struggle with complex multi-step reasoning, constraint adherence, and modular execution. To overcome these limitations, we propose a hierarchical multi-agent framework where a supervising chatbot agent coordinates specialized agents for schedule validation, user query formulation, real-time data retrieval, and constraint-aware itinerary generation. By integrating LLM reasoning, external APIs, and iterative refinement mechanisms based on a deterministic, multi-dimensional scoring framework, our approach ensures adaptability, efficiency, and personalization. Evaluations on the well-known TravelPlanner benchmark demonstrate improvements in Budget Constraint planning by 10\%, wider constraints set-based planning by 25\%, and personalized planning results by 45\%. compared to the ReAct baseline. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Speak or Stay Silent: Context-Aware Turn-Taking in Multi-Party Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11409)**  `arXiv:2603.11409`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kratika Bhagtani, Mrinal Anand, Yu Chen, Amit Kumar Singh Yadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing voice AI assistants treat every detected pause as an invitation to speak. This works in dyadic dialogue, but in multi-party settings, where an AI assistant participates alongside multiple speakers, pauses are abundant and ambiguous. An assistant that speaks on every pause becomes disruptive rather than useful. In this work, we formulate context-aware turn-taking: at every detected pause, given the full conversation context, our method decides whether the assistant should speak or stay silent. We introduce a benchmark of over 120K labeled conversations spanning three multi-party corpora. Evaluating eight recent large language models, we find that they consistently fail at context-aware turn-taking under zero-shot prompting. We then propose a supervised fine-tuning approach with reasoning traces, improving balanced accuracy by up to 23 percentage points. Our findings suggest that context-aware turn-taking is not an emergent capability; it must be explicitly trained.
  </details>

- **[Hybrid AI for AI-Native RAN Control: A Functional Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33035)**  `arXiv:2611.33035`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sabrine Aroua, Osama Arouk, Pegah Alizadeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-native radio access network (RAN) control involves interdependent decisions across network entities, protocol layers, and timescales. Understanding how AI and conventional methods can jointly support these decisions requires a common basis for comparing their functional roles. This survey introduces a technique-agnostic taxonomy of RAN decision-making organized around four complementary capabilities: Representation and Reasoning, Prediction and Simulation, Adaptive Control, and Decision Synthesis. These capabilities are defined by their roles in the control process, independently of the techniques used to implement them. Through this perspective, we examine representative AI and model-based approaches, including foundation models, world models, reinforcement learning, and optimization. We then apply the taxonomy to representative RAN control solutions across slow, medium, fast, and cross-timescale applications to identify which capabilities they implement and how these capabilities are combined in hybrid AI systems. We also review datasets, benchmarks, simulators, digital twins, and experimental platforms according to their support for the decision-making process and reproducible evaluation. The review highlights a gap between the availability of resources for closed-loop experimentation and the standardization of tasks, metrics, and evaluation protocols needed for meaningfulthis http URL, we identify research directions for hybrid AI in AI-native RAN, including capability integration, multi-agent and cross-timescale coordination, constraint handling, and reproducible closed-loop evaluation. A curated list of surveyed works and evaluation resources is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RecordRoute: Auditing Agent Failures with Localization-Guided Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33022)**  `arXiv:2611.33022`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Duan, Lihuan Li, Chao Xue, Siyuan Zheng, Du Yin, Flora D. Salim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auditing failed agent executions requires identifying the responsible actor, the earliest decisive step, and the error mode. Representative supervised methods either focus on step localization or predict multiple attribution targets from shared trajectory representations. These methods do not use the localization result to select the text for mode diagnosis. To address this limitation, we propose RecordRoute, which couples comparative localization with local diagnosis. Specifically, it represents each record with word and character features and learns to rank records through comparisons within each trajectory. The selected record supplies the actor and step, while its text and metadata drive mode prediction. On controlled-injection Who&When Pro, RecordRoute outperforms StepFinder and ASCon on all four official metrics. With actor and step predictions fixed, local diagnosis reaches 77.47% pooled joint accuracy versus 69.28% for full-trace diagnosis. On natural failures from TraceElephant and CatchBench POST, it outperforms these baselines and three open LLMs in localization.
  </details>

- **[Lazy Agents Answer Against Their Own Beliefs: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Beliefs Teaches Them to Act on What They Believe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32884)**  `arXiv:2611.32884`  `cs.AI`  
  _Richmond Sin Jing Xuan, Peilin Feng, Wenbin Wang, Soujanya Poria_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents working on long tasks often commit to an answer before they have enough evidence, a behavior called laziness. In our evaluations, they frequently commit even while their stated belief still names several possible answers. An outcome reward at the end of the episode scores only the final answer, so it cannot teach the agent when to commit, because a lucky guess receives the same reward as an answer backed by evidence. During training, however, the simulator can compute the exact posterior over the answer at every step and tell whether the evidence is already enough. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Beliefs (RLVB), which rewards the agent for taking further steps while this posterior still allows several answers and for committing once the evidence is sufficient. The reward needs no judge, critic or reward model, and it never reads the belief line the agent writes before each action. We also introduce Pro Worlds, a benchmark of visual puzzles where the agent views a large world through a small window. We evaluate three vision-language models on three benchmarks, two of which are never seen in training. RLVB improves the base model in all nine settings, by 17.1% on average. Under the outcome reward, GRPO and PPO fall below the base model on average. RLVB also outperforms PPO with a process reward from an LLM judge by 9.3% on average while using 1.6 times fewer GPU-hours. The trained agent answers with sufficient evidence in 51%, 12% and 74% of the episodes on the three benchmarks, against 8%, 0.4% and 36% for the base model. Its accuracy gain therefore comes from answering more often at high levels of evidence. The belief line shows how the agent reaches this evidence: it acts on its own belief. It commits at only 0.1 to 2.2% of the turns at which the evidence is insufficient and its line still names several answers, against 3 to 68% for the base model.
  </details>

- **[Measurement Artifacts in Trustworthiness Evaluations of Quantized Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32848)**  `arXiv:2611.32848`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tanzeel Abbas, Tehseen Zia, David Windridge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluations of quantized language models attribute the change in a benchmark score to quantization. We write the measured change as the true change plus a term contributed by the instrument. Instrument error that is the same at every precision can shrink a paired comparison but cannot create or reverse an effect. Error that interacts with quantization can do both, and it is reported as an effect of quantization. We estimate this interaction for four measurement choices in 8 instruction-tuned models below two billion parameters, at float16, LLM.int8() and NF4, on identical items. Measured against labels from two human annotators, the 4-bit rise in harmful compliance is about half to two-thirds of the rise in non-refusal that a corrected key word detector records. Cloze and lettered multiple-choice scoring disagree about the effect of quantization far more than their noise allows: their estimates correlate at \r{ho} = 0.10, against a ceiling of about 0.77. LLM.int8() answers and generations depend on which items share a batch, and padding alone changes its answers. Rounding activations to 8 bits is enough to produce this dependence. NF4 is batch-invariant. The two largest reported calibration losses are response-bias shifts. We release item-level predictions, option scores and human annotations, and give recommendations for evaluating compressed small models.
  </details>

- **[Learning World Models through Contrastive Identification of Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32776)**  `arXiv:2611.32776`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hayeong Lee, Youngjun Suh. Byung-Jun Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models enable goal-directed planning from offline visual data, but prediction alone does not specify which action-related distinctions their representations should preserve. Inverse dynamics provides auxiliary supervision, yet squared-error action regression rewards additional information only through changes in the conditional action mean. We propose Contrastive Identification of Actions (CIA), which identifies the executed action among alternatives sampled from a history-conditioned behavior proposal. Comparing actions plausible in the same context targets information supplied by the next latent beyond history. Under exact behavior matching, we show that CIA can reward action information that leaves optimal inverse-regression error unchanged, and verify this distinction through a controlled learning example. CIA achieves higher planner-averaged mean success than the base world model and a regression inverse-dynamics baseline across five visual control benchmarks, without modifying the planner.
  </details>

- **[EviCraft: Can Minecraft Agents Find What They Cannot See?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32765)**  `arXiv:2611.32765`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenyou Yi, Lantian Zhang, Guanlin Chen, Xingqiu Xu, Haiyang Sun, Minrui Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Minecraft, a common testbed for embodied agents, semantic tools such as mineBlock pick targets from the simulator's loaded world state without a visibility test, so a buried ore the agent has never seen is one call away, and scores cannot show whether the agent searched. We formalize the missing requirement as evidence closure: every decision of an agent system, including those inside its tools, may depend on the world only through facts for which the agent holds evidence. EviCraft is a runtime and benchmark that enforces closure below the agent and makes the admission rule an experimental variable: a perception-grounded regime (G) admits only what is in line of sight or returned by the agent's own actions, and a loaded-state regime (L) reproduces standard tool stacks, with agents, tools, and worlds held fixed. On Survival, the regime effect exceeds the strongest agent's lead: under G, three mid-sized agents reach the diamond in 32%-40% of runs, against 76%-94% under L, and the strongest agent under G scores below each of them under L. In two models, removing only the line-of-sight test closes 93%-96% of the score gap, and loading state only inside tools recovers 70%-98%, so safeguards on observations and queries leave most of the leak open. In open-ended acquisition, the gain from L concentrates in items that depend on buried resources or mobs. Code and benchmark are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Economic World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32663)**  `arXiv:2611.32663`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qirui Mi, Yujia Ming, Bo Li, Haifeng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can artificial intelligence reproduce the economic dynamics that emerge as households, firms and governments interact and adapt through markets and institutions? To answer this question, we define an economic world model (EWM) as an agent's internal representation of how an economic system works, enabling counterfactual simulation and decision-making under resource constraints. We then propose a general framework for studying EWMs that links three tasks: simulating agent behaviour, learning economic transitions and systematically evaluating the dynamics. As an initial implementation, we learn household policies from microeconomic data and calibrate a structural economic transition model using macroeconomic data, linking individual decisions to aggregate dynamics. Our micro-to-macro evaluation framework compares simulated individual actions and group distributions with observed microdata and assesses macroeconomic dynamics through forecasting and counterfactual experiments. Results show that simulated and observed household consumption distributions overlap by 94.9%, while EWM forecasts closely track real GDP and several other aggregate levels. In government-borrowing counterfactuals, the EWM reproduces the benchmark life-cycle model's short-run transmission from borrowing to consumption. For ex ante planning, the EWM enables an LLM agent to achieve 10.29% higher simulated GDP under the epidemic shock than direct LLM decisions. This initial EWM is only a starting point. We invite economists and AI researchers to build a stronger EWM together. Economists can contribute richer economic models and knowledge, while AI researchers can develop stronger data-driven methods for simulating agent behaviour and learning economic transitions. Together, we aim not only to reproduce real economic dynamics, but also to discover and test new mechanisms that support better decisions and benefit society.
  </details>

- **[GeoSurge: Geometry-Grounded Multimodal Reasoning for Auditable Liver Resection Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32657)**  `arXiv:2611.32657`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenjie Deng, Zijian Tu, Xueyin Zhou, Huayi Wang, Zhuoyun Du, Mingyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical resection planning centers on generating executable resection surfaces from anatomical evidence and operative constraints. Medical multimodal foundation models enable anatomical understanding, and segmentation models predict resection regions, but neither translates these capabilities into explicitly constrained, executable resection surfaces. We present GeoSurge, an anatomy-grounded framework that combines mask-derived metrics with structure-aware point-cloud tokens to generate executable, editable resection surfaces. To make resection geometry learnable and editable, GeoSurge maps actions to parametric geometry through a surface-action grammar. Using this grammar, anatomy-oriented and tumor-centered templates generate candidates, which are filtered by hard constraints and semantic deduplication. Training supervision is then derived from candidate-expert geometric agreement and independently recomputed feasibility metrics. At inference, the reasoner uses patient evidence and surgical requirements to select or revise an action, which an independent geometry engine subsequently materializes and verifies. GeoSurge achieves the best overall scores on DeepTumour Liver and CRLM-QA, improving the constraint satisfaction rate from 73% to 100% on CRLM and from 56% to 99% on GeoSurge-Bench.
  </details>

- **[RecurSciBench: Benchmarking Recursive Self-Improvement in Data-Driven Scientific Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32508)**  `arXiv:2611.32508`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenxi Xu, Chuan Qin, Zihan Wang, Zixing Chen, Le Zhang, Jingbo Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based agents are advancing the automation of data-driven scientific research. Recent advances in recursive self-improvement (RSI) offer the potential for these agents to continuously improve through experience, but evaluating transfer across scientific tasks requires executable, scientifically verifiable tasks and independent assessment of self-improvement gains. We introduce RecurSciBench, a scalable benchmark for evaluating RSI in data-driven scientific research. Its RecurSciPipe framework automatically constructs scientific modeling and data analysis tasks from raw datasets, with execution-based verification and scientific validity checks. RecurSciBench spans four scientific domains, multiple data modalities, and varied scientific objectives. We evaluate four RSI systems by comparing their initial and evolved states on independent tasks after evolution on source tasks, covering within-type, cross-type, and cross-domain transfer. Experiments show that the benefits of self-improvement vary across agent systems and scientific domains. Positive overall gains can coexist with regressions on individual tasks. Experience can transfer across task types and benefit other domains even without measurable gains in the source domain.
  </details>

- **[FigmaEval: A Benchmark for Evaluating AI Agents in Professional UI Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32321)**  `arXiv:2611.32321`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyue Huang, Yongyong Gao, Zhengyuan Pan, Zheng Wei, Xinya Liu, Duanyi Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in AI agents have enabled autonomous UI design using professional tools such as Figma, yet evaluating their design capabilities remains challenging due to the open-ended nature of design tasks and the absence of unique ground truths. We introduce FigmaEval, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on professional UI design tasks, comprising 84 tasks and 783 screens derived from real-world Figma design files. FigmaEval adopts a constrained-specification, open-solution paradigm, standardizing task requirements and design resources while allowing agents to independently determine layouts, visual hierarchies, component composition, and visual presentation. To assess design quality beyond reference similarity, we develop 22 expert-grounded evaluation rubrics across six dimensions and introduce a human evaluation arena where professional designers compare agent-generated designs through blinded pairwise judgments. Evaluating eleven models under a unified Figma agent setup, we find substantial agreement between rubric-based assessments and human preferences, alongside distinct differences in design practices and quality--efficiency trade-offs. FigmaEval provides a framework for systematically assessing professional UI design capabilities and understanding the strengths and limitations of current AI agents.
  </details>

- **[VC-OPD: Learning to See What Matters via Visual Cue On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32292)**  `arXiv:2611.32292`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qinxuan Wang, Zheyu Zhang, Yijing Yang, Han Shu, Chuang Wang, Peipei Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual On-Policy Distillation (OPD) commonly uses cropped or zoomed-in image regions as teacher-side privileged information to improve fine-grained perception in small Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). However, local pixel-level cues are insufficient for complex visual reasoning that requires global structure, spatial relationships, and high-level semantics. We propose Visual Cue On-Policy Self-Distillation (VC-OPD), which extends teacher-side privileged information beyond local image regions to question-conditioned visual cues. Advanced MLLMs generate cues capturing key visual details, structural relationships, and semantic evidence needed to answer each question. A multi-stage quality filtering process selects reliable cues, which are provided only to the teacher during training. This enables informative token-level supervision along student-generated trajectories, helping the student learn to identify and reason over critical evidence directly from the original image. No additional information is required at inference time. Since advanced MLLMs are used only for visual cue generation, VC-OPD is compatible with both open-weight and proprietary black-box models. Experiments on STEM and general visual reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements across model scales over reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation baselines. Notably, a 4B model trained with VC-OPD outperforms many baselines with 8B or more parameters, demonstrating effective capability transfer through question-conditioned visual cues.
  </details>

- **[Refining and Aligning Clinical Reasoning with Health Guidelines via Formal Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32247)**  `arXiv:2611.32247`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyang Fan, Yufan Cai, Zhe Hou, Jin Song Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical reasoning from incomplete or conflicting patient evidence must account for low-probability but high-risk concerns while remaining grounded in health guidelines. We introduce VerClin, an explainable framework that extracts guideline conditions, exclusions, recommendations, and priorities into executable knowledge and represents patient conversations as scoped cases. A deterministic four-valued reasoner produces traceable guideline matches while preserving uncertainty and enforcing exclusions. Multiple specialty agents then screen matched guidelines for case applicability, and a consultation model synthesizes accepted guidance through specialty recommendations and a sentence-preserving formal-constraint channel. We present accepted guidance in ascending order of the sum of its priority and trigger-strength ranks, which can surface high-risk guidance with weaker triggers. VerClin outperforms direct generation and Top-5 RAG on HealthBench Hard and HealthBench Professional. On the LLMEval-Med subset, it outperforms direct generation and ties Top-5 RAG. Component analyses further examine semantic closure, example-guided refinement, specialty screening, and constraint-conditioned delivery.
  </details>

- **[CoverMem: Coverage-Constrained Visual Memory for Budgeted Embodied Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32119)**  `arXiv:2611.32119`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruijie Ma, Haoyue Liu, Mingda Zhang, Dongxu Yue, Xiaoying Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Episodic-memory embodied question answering enables agents to answer questions based on previously recorded visual observations of the surrounding scenes. However, growing observation histories, varying observation quality across viewpoints, and limited computational resources make it challenging to construct and maintain compact visual memories while retrieving sufficient evidence for question answering. To address these challenges, we introduce CoverMem, which incorporates three key components: (1) a coverage-constrained memory construction method that selects a compact set of informative full frames satisfying object-quality and representative-scene coverage requirements; (2) a coverage-preserving incremental maintenance strategy that locally reselects only the affected frames to satisfy updated coverage requirements as observations arrive; and (3) a budgeted complementary frame selection method that balances question relevance, additional support for question requirements, and redundancy with selected frames within a fixed budget. Experimental results on three benchmarks covering diverse environments and question types demonstrate that CoverMem improves question-answering performance under limited visual budgets.
  </details>

- **[EffectWAM: Learning Action-Grounded Effects for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32053)**  `arXiv:2611.32053`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuan Zhang, Hao Cui, Jingfei Ni, Meiyi Wang, Xin Nie, Chi Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models connect visual prediction with executable robot control. We present EffectWAM, which develops this connection through three complementary components: Effect, camera positional encoding (CaPE), and Action Grounding. Effect represents visual change as the difference between independently encoded future observations and a shared current-observation anchor. CaPE conditions endpoint pooling on relative camera pose when reliable extrinsics are available, while sharing the same learned parameters with pose-free pooling for uncalibrated trajectories. Action Grounding trains the visual representation to retain measured changes in end-effector pose and gripper state. An exponential moving average of the observation encoder supplies Effect targets. A shared generation expert learns to jointly predict Effect and an executable action chunk through flow matching, and reuses the sampled pair to generate future video on demand. This allows the controller to execute actions without waiting for video synthesis. We train on more than 20,000 hours of heterogeneous embodied data, excluding unused chain-of-thought data, and accommodate dataset-specific joint, end-effector, and hand-pose representations. Our evaluation uses the Small (8B) configuration on all five benchmarks and additionally evaluates the Large (26B) configuration on RoboDojo. Experiments examine closed-loop manipulation across complementary benchmarks, and comparisons across multiple baseline policies show consistent improvements from Action Grounding.
  </details>

- **[Coding4Games: Can Modern Coding Agents Play Modern Video Games?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31974)**  `arXiv:2611.31974`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shifang Zhao, Haoyu Wang, Yuyang Yin, Dong Chen, Xingyilang Yin, Yunkang Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Games, especially modern AAA games, challenge language models to perceive complex scenes, control actions, and make decisions over long horizons. Existing game benchmarks primarily test language models as computer-use agents that query a language model throughout play, incurring repeated inference cost and, in real-time games, acting while the environment continues to evolve. We ask whether coding agents can learn from game interaction to develop standalone game-playing programs that run without language model inference. We thus introduce Coding4Games, a benchmark for evaluating whether coding agents can develop standalone programs that play modern games through visual observations and standard game inputs. Coding4Games has three defining features: 1) Diverse Modern Games, covering 15 games with diverse visual, strategic, and control demands; 2) Development-Test Separation, in which a coding agent uses a sandbox and the standard player interface to iteratively develop a program, after which the selected program is frozen and independently tested without language-model inference; and 3) Outcome and Process Evaluation, which measures not only game performance and reliability but also how agents use feedback, revise programs, and resolve problems during development. Across 15 games, the best program per game outscores the strongest GUI baseline on 13 and ties it on one. The human player outscores all agents on 10 games. We additionally examine the effects of development budgets. Coding4Games evaluates whether coding agents can turn their understanding of complex interactive environments into programs that work reliably on their own.
  </details>

- **[DexOPD: Dual-Extrapolation On-Policy Distillation for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31969)**  `arXiv:2611.31969`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunchao Ke, Yanzhe Hu, Yi Yang, Jiachun Jin, Zhihong Liu, Zhijie Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based diffusion models have become a mainstream paradigm for text-to-image generation, where task-specific post-training produces specialist models with complementary strengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (OPD) offers an efficient way to consolidate these specialists into a single unified student. Recent methods strengthen supervision through teacher extrapolation, constructing stronger proxy teachers that allow distilled students to surpass the original specialists. However, their extrapolation directions remain defined solely by frozen teacher and reference models, leaving the student's own training progress unexploited. To address this limitation, we introduce DexOPD, a dual-extrapolation on-policy distillation method for diffusion models. DexOPD retains extrapolated teacher supervision while leveraging a lagging historical student as the reference for student self-extrapolation. Sampling across multiple self-extrapolation gains from shared initial noise broadens exploration along the student's evolving trajectory, and task rewards convert pairwise differences in candidate quality into a normalized auxiliary regression direction. Empirically, we evaluate DexOPD on GenEval, OCR, and DrawBench. The resulting unified SD3.5-Medium student achieves a GenEval reward of 0.9736, an OCR score of 0.9515, and a DrawBench Aesthetic score of 6.4875, surpassing the corresponding specialist teachers as well as all evaluated OPD and multi-task reinforcement learning baselines across these three benchmarks while preserving standard single-model inference.
  </details>

- **[RepoEviAgent: Scalable Synthesis of Evidence-Grounded Repository-Level Question Answering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31873)**  `arXiv:2611.31873`  `cs.AI`  
  _Aoqi Hu, Yueao Xing_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world software engineering (SWE) tasks require agents to understand repository structures, cross-module dependencies, and runtime behaviors, posing significant challenges for constructing high-quality training and evaluation data. To address this, we propose RepoEviAgent, a scalable SWE data construction framework that integrates quality-aware data curation and role-specialized multi-agent task synthesis. Starting from 8,477 open-source repositories and approximately 2.48 million pull request records, we employ semantic alignment, complexity constraints, and multidimensional quality assessment to identify valuable SWE data. We further develop a sandbox-based multi-agent framework that integrates automated environment construction, execution validation, repository exploration, and evidence-grounded Q&A synthesis. Based on this framework, we construct RepoEviQA, a benchmark for Repository-Level Evidence-Grounded Question Answering, comprising 334 tasks across five programming languages and diverse SWE categories, with rigorous rubric-based evaluation. We evaluate five frontier coding models on RepoEviQA, revealing substantial performance differences across task categories and programming languages.
  </details>

- **[Modeling Event-Conditioned Emotional Expression Response with LLM Agents in a Synthetic Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31851)**  `arXiv:2611.31851`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rebecca Marigliano, Peter Carragher, Kathleen M. Carley_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emotions are central drivers of human behavior, yet most behavioral models fail to capture their role in shaping perception, decision-making, and collective action. Application areas such as emergency response and public health are especially constrained by the lack of emotion-aware modeling. While large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into agent-based models (ABMs), little work has examined how to endow LLM agents with emotional reasoning or validated their ability to reproduce empirically observed emotional patterns. This study addresses this gap by introducing a hierarchical prompting framework that conditions LLM agents with demographic, psychological, and contextual cues to generate event-conditioned emotional responses to real-world events. We validate the resulting event-conditioned emotional responses against large-scale social media reaction data derived from the Social Science One Facebook URL Shares Dataset, complemented by demographic and personality benchmarks. Our simulation results reproduce both polarized and consensus-driven emotional patterns with significant fidelity, supporting the application of LLM agents for emotion-aware modeling in behavioral simulations. We also find, however, that systematic gaps in emotional expression of LLM agents and human populations exist, such as the underexpression of anger relative to human baselines. As such, these findings highlight the need for continued calibration if LLM agents are to serve as proxies for human populations in behavioral modeling. By quantifying their divergence from human populations in terms of emotional expression, we hope that our methods can aid in this effort.
  </details>

- **[AGL-SKILL: Coding Harnesses as Agentic Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31794)**  `arXiv:2611.31794`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ryan Tsui, Yuge Zhang, Yuqing Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier models are increasingly used through harnesses as agents, with growing autonomy to do useful work. Agent performance can often be enhanced by the use of skills: reusable workflows, code, and prompts that provide task-related information or guide the behaviour of the model. Skills, being textual in nature, present themselves as another editable surface to be optimized; current methods have improved on handcrafting and one-shot generation by treating skill development as an iterative process, using score-driven search to tweak and improve performance. In this paper, we demonstrate the efficacy of the agents themselves as optimizers: across five benchmarks, a coding agent without guidance substantially improves every target and outperforms published skill-optimization methods. More broadly, this shows the capability of agents to do such work and to refine their own behaviour to provide task-specific context, as long as they are given a metric to measure against.
  </details>

- **[PRETZEL: A Benchmark for Cyclic Peptide Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31708)**  `arXiv:2611.31708`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yongchan Hong, Zihang Fang, Mukta Ubale, Yan Liu, Xingyao Chen, Vsevolod Katritch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Macrocyclic peptides are a growing therapeutic modality, and co-folding models are increasingly used to predict their structures. Yet published evaluations rely on small, loosely curated target sets that differ from paper to paper, so results are not comparable, and most report pose RMSD alone, without checking whether the predicted molecule is chemically correct. We introduce PRETZEL (Prediction of Ring-Enclosed Topology: Zero-shot Evaluation of cycLic peptides), a benchmark of 285 curated cyclic peptide-protein complexes spanning three cyclization chemistries, all post-dating the training cutoff of every model evaluated. PRETZEL has three components: PRETZEL-SET, an evaluation set annotated by cyclization chemistry and chemical novelty; PRETZEL-TWIST, a specification layer that compiles a single canonical macrocycle description into each engine's native input format and verifies the returned structure; and PRETZEL-SALT, an evaluation suite pairing standard pose metrics with validity checks for bond distances, bond angles, dihedrals, and ring closure. Using these, we evaluate AlphaFold3, Boltz-2, Protenix-v2, and HighFold3. Protenix-v2 achieves the best median RMSD at 9.05 A, with 19.3% of predictions under 2 A and 37.5% under 5 A. Accuracy varies by cyclization chemistry: models handle head-to-tail macrocycles relatively well but degrade on stapled targets. Chemical validity is weaker still: at most 8.1% of predictions have a fully valid set of inter-residue bonds, and no engine closes every macrocycle in more than 80.7% of its predictions, so pose accuracy alone is an insufficient criterion. These failures share a cause: co-folding models place macrocycles without representing them, treating cyclization and non-canonical chemistry as annotations layered onto a residue sequence rather than as first-class structure. PRETZEL is released with all inputs, predictions, and evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[Look Before You Commit: Visual-Evidence-Guided Decoding for Multimodal Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31677)**  `arXiv:2611.31677`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tongxiao Hu, Yikai Zhao, Qiyan Zhao, Xiaosong Yuan, Xuhang Chen, Pengzhou Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion multimodal large language models rely on confidence to decide when to commit candidate tokens during generation. In multimodal tasks, however, confidence cannot distinguish whether a candidate originates from image evidence or the language prior. We perform paired forward ablation and find that nearly nine out of ten high-confidence commits are insensitive to the image, while degenerate and hallucination samples exhibit significant increases in the entropy of deep-layer attention organization. Based on this diagnosis, we propose CD-APC, a training-free decoding method. CD-APC performs evidence-adjusted scoring within a base-supported pool and uses dual gating based on model acceptability and contrastive concentration to decide whether to commit, together with an inverse-window mechanism that adapts to inter-step difficulty. Across two backbones and five benchmarks, CD-APC consistently improves performance, indicating that stable visual evidence improves model outputs.
  </details>

- **[BRAG: Bayesian Retrieval Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31656)**  `arXiv:2611.31656`  `cs.AI`  
  _Javier Movellan, Manjot Bilkhu, Young-mok Jung, Sirajul Salekin, Henry Tran, Zhao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) ranks memory chunks by semantic similarity to the query. Many methods improve on this by learning from feedback on what was retrieved, but each is a separate heuristic, with no account of when it is the right choice. We introduce BRAG, a generative model of retrieval with use. Each chunk generates the queries it answers from a mixture of von Mises-Fisher components anchored at its original embedding, and each chunk has a latent utility that drifts over time. The model gives the optimal inference, a closed-form filter that stores a few keys and two scalars per chunk and updates them online from the labels of a curator, and it frames retrieval as a control problem. It also shows when existing methods are optimal. Query averaging, Rocchio-style feedback and frequency reranking are special cases with fixed parameters, NUDGE is the case of one key per chunk with no dynamics, and LADER the limit of one key per past query. On two public long-term memory benchmarks, BRAG matches these methods on queries asked before, as expected, because when nothing changes over time the assumptions behind these special cases hold. On harder problems, where a new chunk makes an old one obsolete, BRAG outscores them. They all stay at plain-search level (Recall@5 0.67-0.71), while BRAG retrieves the new chunk 0.80-0.86 of the time. We also ran BRAG on three of NUDGE's seven text benchmarks, and it matches or beats NUDGE on all three. On nine months of a personal agent's memory (20,640 chunks), BRAG raises Recall@5 on held-out queries from 0.75 to 0.98 and Recall@1 from 0.44 to 0.97, with little change on chunks never queried.
  </details>

- **[Wavefront: Low-Rank Drafting with Parallel Causal Refinement for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31638)**  `arXiv:2611.31638`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huy Huu Nguyen, Xin Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by letting a cheap drafter propose tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Parallel block drafters such as DFlash and P-EAGLE propose a whole block in one forward pass, but they lose the dependence between tokens in the block; DSpark restores part of it with a lightweight correction head that runs sequentially. Measuring DSpark, we find that this head overrides the base prediction on most slots, so the expensive base readout through the target's full lm_head mostly provides a seed. We therefore propose Wavefront, which follows a simple principle: shrink the base and strengthen the correction head. Wavefront replaces the full lm_head with a learned low-rank base, conditions the correction head on the whole drafted prefix within the same block, and resolves it with a few parallel Jacobi passes. A fixed pass budget makes the draft step free of data-dependent control flow, so we capture it as a single CUDA graph in vLLM with fused kernels. On Qwen3-8B, Wavefront improves throughput over xPress, DSpark and DFlash by 2.6%, 10.7% and 23.6% on average across math, code, chat and agentic benchmarks, and by 9.6%, 14.3% and 28.9% on SPEED-Bench long-context prompts. On Qwen3.8-27B it leads DFlash2, DSpark and xPress by 7.6%, 10.6% and 5.0%, and by 7.4%, 5.8% and 6.0% on the same long-context prompts. The gains persist as serving load grows to concurrency 32.
  </details>

- **[Self-Improving Long-Horizon Reasoning by Learning Summaries from Local Continuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31621)**  `arXiv:2611.31621`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoqun Cao, Tengyang Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning models degrade as their context grows, so longer reasoning traces do not by themselves give longer effective horizons. One way to extend the horizon is to compress the reasoning history into a compact state from time to time and continue from the state. Existing methods train the state with the final-answer reward or distill it from a stronger model. Neither signal is tailored to measure the contribution of the state alone. We observe that a reasoning model that degrades over long trajectories still behaves reliably in short contexts. We aim to preserve this short-context behavior when compressing the reasoning history. Motivated by this, we train a separate summarizer with Delta-KL, a reward computed from the frozen reasoning model's token-level predictions on sampled continuations, and run inference by truncating the trace, summarizing it, and continuing from the summary (TruSuCo). Training uses a few thousand questions with 4k-token reasoning prefixes and does not need answers or external teachers. Under a fixed 32k-token budget, the trained summarizer improves on vanilla reasoning and on an untrained summarizer in all nine settings across mathematical benchmarks and Reasoning Gym puzzles, by up to 7.7 points and 12 points, respectively. The improvement over vanilla reasoning grows with the budget. At 128k tokens, our method outperforms it by 10 points on AIME 2024--2026. Analyses show that truncation prevents the collapse of vanilla reasoning and that the trained summaries retain more of the reasoning history.
  </details>

- **[SpatialCraft: Evolving Procedural Intelligence from Experience and Skills for Spatial Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31475)**  `arXiv:2611.31475`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiwei Liu, Yulong Li, Xuhui Li, Zhixiang Lu, Haolin Yang, Jianxu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning agents increasingly rely on external tools, such as 3D reconstruction, segmentation, and geometric computation, to answer questions that a multimodal large language model (MLLM) cannot resolve from pixels alone. Tool access, however, shifts the bottleneck to orchestration: the agent must decide which tools to call, in what order, and how to interpret their outputs, yet a stateless agent repeats the same mistakes across tasks. We ask whether a frozen tool-using agent can improve its orchestration from its own interaction history, without parameter updates. We present SpatialCraft, a self-evolving framework that maintains two forms of external knowledge: Experiences, condition-action tactics distilled by contrasting rollouts of the same task, and Skills, multi-step procedures with explicit activation and termination conditions. Three designs couple the two streams. Experiences can be anchored to a Skill, appearing only while it is active rather than once per task. A Contrastive Utility Score estimates each Experience's effect by randomly withholding it across rollouts of the same task, separating its contribution from task difficulty, and Experiences with consistently positive utility are consolidated into the Skills they support. Across five spatial benchmarks and four backbones, SpatialCraft achieves the best accuracy in all 20 settings, showing that a frozen agent can learn to orchestrate spatial tools from its own experience.
  </details>

- **[PEAR: Adaptive Advantage Reshaping for Perception and Exploration in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32216)**  `arXiv:2610.32216`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junxin Wang, Yingfeng Luo, Xiangchen Song, Weiqiao Shan, Runsong Zhao, Xinyu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multimodal reasoning requires a model to reliably utilize visual information while sufficiently exploring diverse reasoning paths. To achieve this goal, prior work combines visual dependency and token entropy to reshape sequence-level advantages, but scaling the contribution of token entropy by visual dependency can leave high-entropy tokens with low visual dependency underweighted, despite their important role in reasoning exploration. To address this limitation, we propose PEAR, an adaptive advantage reshaping method for improving perception and exploration in multimodal reasoning. PEAR retains visual dependency as the base signal and adds an entropy-based exploration gain only at high-entropy positions, with lower visual dependency leading to a larger additional gain. The fused signal is then mapped to positive token-level weights that reshape the sequence-level advantage. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B/7B show that PEAR achieves the highest average performance among evaluated baselines across multimodal reasoning benchmarks, alongside leading results on textual reasoning and general multimodal benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Static Leaderboards: Capability-Focused Evaluation with Structural Diversity for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26251)**  `arXiv:2610.26251`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongyin Zhang, Mingyi Xiang, Rongzhao Chen, Yankai Wang, Jialin Qiao, Xiangdong Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Static public benchmarks for time series foundation models (TSFMs) typically have fixed and narrow test distributions. Their repeated reuse encourages benchmark-specific tuning, making reported performance reflect adaptation to the fixed test set rather than genuine forecasting generalization. In addition, a single aggregate score cannot fully characterize model capabilities across distinct temporal structures and input relationships. We propose CaFE (Capability-Focused Evaluation), a refreshable evaluation extension grounded in original benchmark data that enriches static benchmarks with structural diversity and enriches single-score black-box evaluation with multi-dimensional, capability-oriented assessment. CaFE first profiles the original benchmark and defines eight Structural Features (SFs) that characterize its temporal structure. For each SF, it resamples the original data according to its structural support, removes edge-of-distribution samples, and augments the retained samples by varying the SF's salience, producing samples with different structural profiles. After filtering out out-of-distribution samples while preserving diversity, CaFE yields a refreshed evaluation set. TSFMs are then evaluated on this set using both conventional forecast-error metrics and our proposed intervention-response metrics, giving each model a per-feature capability profile alongside endpoint accuracy. Experiments on GIFT-Eval and FEV-Mini20 show that CaFE yields stable evaluation results and reveals per-feature strengths and weaknesses that a single aggregate score hides. Moreover, on genuine data filtered by structural condition, models selected by CaFE outperform those chosen by the aggregate leaderboard. CaFE therefore makes model selection conditional on the structural response of interest.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing and Improving Probabilistic Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38005)**  `arXiv:2609.38005`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huaman Sun, Dingcheng Wang, Jason Hartline, Jessica Hullman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly proposed as decision assistants who must reason probabilistically from available evidence under explicit decision costs. We propose a decision-theoretic framework that decomposes LLMs' decision loss into two components: forming accurate beliefs from provided evidence and translating those beliefs into actions that optimize a provided utility function. Using a synthetic benchmark with known ground truth, we apply the decomposition to characterize probabilistic reasoning in frontier and open-sourced models. We further evaluate whether RL interventions targeting beliefs, decisions, or both improve these components across three domains, whether improvements transfer across components and elicitation formats, and whether decision performance can improve without improvement in belief formation. We find that targeting one component of probabilistic reasoning improves the target without necessarily transferring to others, and that jointly targeting belief formation and decision-making improves both but hinges on matched formats between training and evaluation.
  </details>

- **[A Table Is Worth 64 Tokens: Pixel-level Compression for Multi-Table Document Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26949)**  `arXiv:2608.26949`  `cs.AI`  
  _Iñigo Alonso, Mirella Lapata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answering questions over real-world documents requires processing long inputs that interleave text with tables. Optical context compression, which represents context as images, promises to reduce token cost, but its effect on table understanding remains unclear. We study pixel-level table compression for question answering over documents with multiple tables, evaluating seven VLMs across two benchmarks and five visual-token budgets. At moderate budgets, table images match html in accuracy with fewer tokens, but aggressive downscaling makes models reason longer without recovering accuracy, eroding the savings. Yet even at 64 visual tokens per table, tables preserve enough signal to identify whether they are relevant to a question. We exploit this asymmetry with a training-free, two-stage method: the model first identifies the tables needed to answer a question from a pixel-compressed context, and then reasons over those tables at native resolution. On long financial documents, it uses 41% fewer tokens than single-step QA over native-resolution tables while gaining 5 accuracy points, and 21% fewer than the most efficient compressed configuration at no accuracy cost. It also still saves tokens when applied to retrieved context.
  </details>

- **[DiSCO: Defending text-to-image generation through distribution-guided contrastive prompt optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17067)**  `arXiv:2608.17067`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tong Zhang, Motasem Alfarra, Carlos Hinojosa, Christos Louizos, Bernard Ghanem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As text-to-image generative models advance, they raise critical safety concerns, particularly the generation of Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content such as violence and nudity, further exacerbated by red-teaming adversarial attacks. Existing defenses predominantly operate under white-box assumptions, relying on text encoder optimization, weight editing, or inference-time intervention, and fundamentally cannot scale to proprietary models. Black-box alternatives based on LLM prompt rewriting offer broader applicability, yet fail in a critical regime we identify as the \textit{benign adversarial} problem: prompts that are linguistically safe but still trigger harmful generation due to the model's learned data distribution. We propose DiSCO, a zero-shot, strictly black-box defense that operates entirely at the prompt level as a plug-and-play module, requiring no model retraining, fine-tuning, or access to model internals. DiSCO performs distribution-guided suffix expansion via beam search, optimized through contrastive scoring over safe and unsafe image pools generated by the target model itself, with iterative adaptive feedback until safe content is produced. We demonstrate that DiSCO consistently enhances the safety of both undefended and defended models on the I2P benchmark under multiple red-teaming attacks, achieving 37.7% and 25.13% ASR reduction, respectively, while maintaining semantic fidelity and improving image coherence. As a black-box, architecture-agnostic module, DiSCO can be readily applied to any text-to-image system without necessitating any changes to the model itself.
  </details>

- **[FlowR2A: Learning Reward-to-Action Distribution for Multimodal Driving Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24231)**  `arXiv:2606.24231`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xirui Li, Zhe Liu, Xiaoqing Ye, Wenhua Han, Yifeng Pan, Junyu Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal driving planning faces a long-standing tension between two paradigms: scoring-based methods benefit from dense reward supervision but are confined to a fixed action vocabulary, while anchor-based methods generate proposals dynamically yet suffer from sparse supervision constrained to a single ground-truth trajectory. In this work, we propose FlowR2A, which resolves this tension by reframing simulation-based rewards from discriminative targets into generative conditions. By learning the reward-conditioned action distribution from dense trajectory-reward pairs with a flow-matching decoder, FlowR2A unifies the dense supervision of scoring-based methods with the proposal generation of anchor-based methods in a single generative model, forcing the model to internalize the correlation between an action and its outcomes in safety, progress, comfort, and rule compliance. To balance hard safety constraints against soft progress objectives, we introduce fine-grained per-timestep reward conditioning and reward noise augmentation. The generative formulation naturally supports controllable test-time sampling via reward guidance and anchored sampling, producing high-quality proposals. FlowR2A achieves state-of-the-art results on the NAVSIM v1 and v2 benchmarks, with multimodal proposals of substantially higher quality than prior methods.
  </details>

- **[A Matter of TASTE: Improving Coverage and Difficulty of Agent Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28556)**  `arXiv:2605.28556`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tomer Keren, Nitay Calderon, Asaf Yehudai, Yotam Perlitz, Michal Shmueli-Scheuer, Roi Reichart_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As agent capabilities advance, existing benchmarks, such as $\tau^2$-Bench, are becoming increasingly saturated. Yet constructing new benchmark tasks remains complex, costly, and labor-intensive. Moreover, the standard approach, in which scenarios are first written in natural language and then mapped to tool sequences, captures only a narrow subset of the tool-use patterns agents exercise. In this paper, we address these problems by reversing the task construction process. We propose TASTE: Task Synthesis from Tool Sequence Evolution, an automatic method that generates challenging tasks with broader tool-use coverage. TASTE utilizes an Adaptive Contrastive $n$-gram model trained on LLM-judged validity signals. This enables sampling valid tool sequences that cover a vast range of tool combinations. TASTE then selects representative sequences from the pool via clustering, instantiates them into complete benchmark tasks, and refines them through iterative difficulty evolution. Using TASTE, we construct $\tau^c$-Bench, a challenging extension of the three domains of $\tau^2$-Bench. We evaluate $11$ agent/user LLM pairs and find that models nearly saturating $\tau^2$-Bench suffer severe performance drops on our tasks (e.g., Gemini-3-Flash falls from $0.82\!-\!0.94$ to $0.28\!-\!0.61$). Beyond increasing difficulty, our generated tasks more than double the number of unique tool combinations agents must execute. Our results suggest high scores on existing benchmarks often reflect saturation rather than robust task-solving ability. By automating the generation of difficult, high-coverage benchmarks, TASTE enables continuous, scalable evaluation of future agents.
  </details>

- **[M2A: Synergizing Mathematical and Agentic Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09879)**  `arXiv:2605.09879`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junjian Wang, Xin Zhou, Qiran Xu, Kun Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While reasoning has become a central capability of large language models (LLMs), the reasoning patterns required for different scenarios are often misaligned. Mathematical reasoning typically relies on intrinsic logic to solve closed-world problems in a single response, whereas agentic reasoning requires not only internal reasoning but also multi-turn interaction with external environments, interleaving thought and action. This misalignment prevents mathematical and agentic reasoning from effectively benefiting from each other, often yielding unstable reasoning behavior and only limited performance gains under multi-task learning. In this paper, we propose M2A, a novel paradigm that synergizes mathematical and agentic reasoning via model merging. To avoid overfitting to superficial reasoning patterns under joint training, M2A operates directly in parameter space: it identifies the feature subspace critical for agent behavior, and merges the mathematical reasoning task vector only along its null space, thereby injecting reasoning capability along directions that do not perturb agent behavior. Unlike SFT or RL, M2A requires no additional gradient-update and exposes the merging coefficient as a simple knob for controlling reasoning length. Experiments in a challenging real-world coding agent setting show that our method effectively extends agentic reasoning depth and delivers substantial performance improvements. Applied to a fine-tuned Qwen3-8B, M2A improves its SWE-Bench Verified resolved rate from 44.0% to 51.2% without retraining the model. Further experiments demonstrate that M2A generalizes across model families, agent scaffolds, and task domains.
  </details>

- **[STAIRS-Former: Spatio-Temporal Attention with Interleaved Recursive Structure Transformer for Offline Multi-task Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11691)**  `arXiv:2603.11691`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiwon Jeon, Myungsik Cho, Youngchul Sung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) with multi-task datasets is challenging due to varying numbers of agents across tasks and the need to generalize to unseen scenarios. Prior works employ transformers with observation tokenization and hierarchical skill learning to address these issues. However, they underutilize the transformer attention mechanism for inter-agent coordination and rely on a single history token, which limits their ability to capture long-horizon temporal dependencies in partially observable MARL settings. In this paper, we propose STAIRS-Former, a transformer architecture augmented with spatial and temporal hierarchies that enables effective attention over critical tokens while capturing long interaction histories. We further introduce token dropout to enhance robustness and generalization across varying agent populations. Extensive experiments on diverse multi-agent benchmarks, including SMAC, SMAC-v2, MPE, and MaMuJoCo, with multi-task datasets demonstrate that STAIRS-Former consistently outperforms prior methods and achieves new state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[MADRA: Multi-Agent Debate for Risk-Aware Embodied Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21460)**  `arXiv:2511.21460`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junjian Wang, Lidan Zhao, Xi Sheryl Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit impressive reasoning capabilities but often suffer from "Embodied Semantic Hallucinations" - generating plans that are semantically fluent but physically unsafe due to a lack of grounded common sense. Existing safety alignment methods, such as RLHF or naive safety prompting, typically fall into a "Safety-Utility Trade-off," resulting in severe over-rejection of benign household instructions. To address this, we propose MADRA (Multi-Agent Debate for Risk Awareness), a training-free cognitive architecture that mimics System-2 deliberation. MADRA introduces a meta-cognitive Critical Agent that evaluates peer debates using a structured argumentation framework derived from the Toulmin Model, effectively mitigating the "herd mentality" in multi-agent systems. We also introduce SafeAware-VH, a benchmark featuring adversarial safe instructions designed to probe agents' sensitivity to physical risks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MADRA breaks the Pareto frontier, achieving over 90% rejection of unsafe tasks while maintaining high utility, significantly outperforming standard Chain-of-Thought and single-agent reflection baselines.
  </details>

- **[Fortune: Formula-Driven Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning over Complex Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23667)**  `arXiv:2505.23667`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lang Cao, Jingxian Xu, Hanbing Liu, Jinyu Wang, Mengyu Zhou, Haoyu Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tables are a fundamental medium for organizing and analyzing data, making table reasoning an essential capability for intelligent systems. Although large language models (LLMs) exhibit strong general reasoning abilities, they still struggle with accurate numerical reasoning over tabular data, particularly in complex table settings that require more than simple relational lookup. Spreadsheet formulas provide a powerful and expressive interface for executable symbolic operations, enabling sophisticated reasoning patterns that remain largely underexplored by existing LLMs. In this paper, we introduce Formula Tuning (Fortune), a formula-driven reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains LLMs to generate executable spreadsheet formulas for question answering over tabular data, using execution success and answer correctness as reward signals. Building upon Fortune, we train Formula-R1 and evaluate its performance across seven table reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating competitive performance, particularly on tasks involving complex tables and multi-step numerical computation. Furthermore, Formula-R1-Plus, which integrates textual and formula-based reasoning, achieves further performance improvements. Beyond these empirical results, we provide insights into the roles of reinforcement learning and executable formula representations in table reasoning, highlighting the broader potential of symbolic RL to enhance the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.
  </details>
